7. **Measure & debrief.** Attack yourself: tampered, expired, missing, and a look at the stored hash — four for four refused/opaque. _Ask:_ which of your five decisions would an attacker be happiest you'd botched? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** show the four refusals; decode your own JWT in front of yourself and confirm nothing secret is in it.

- **Interview (90s):** "I put a token auth flow in front of my incidents API. The decision I'd defend is short access tokens plus rotating refresh tokens — here's the theft blast-radius argument, and here's the statelessness I traded away to be able to revoke."
- **Design review:** a peer says "just use `localStorage`, it's simpler." Walk them through the exact XSS scenario, then say honestly when their choice is fine.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I finally built token auth from scratch instead of reaching for a library — the eye-opener was realizing a JWT isn't secret, it's just tamper-proof."
- **Reach-back (contract thread):** M9-style thinking — you'll attack your own code deliberately elsewhere. Which of your five decisions here is the one an attacker is happiest to find you got wrong?

---

# PART II — Change Over Time

_Three ways the browser keeps a UI in sync with data that won't hold still: reactive streams as the abstraction (M5), cached state under pressure (M6), and state pushed from a server in real time (M7). This is the time thread at browser altitude._

---

## M5 — Two Typeaheads · async/await vs RxJS · (M)

> **Story.** As a front-end engineer, I want the same typeahead built twice — async/await vs RxJS — against a slow, jittery endpoint I control, so that I meet the stale-response race and can argue, from experience, when Observables earn their weight. **MVP.** Two implementations, identical contract: debounce, loading/empty/error states, and provably no stale result. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - Fast typing fires far fewer requests than keystrokes.
> - A staged race (earlier request returns later) never shows the earlier result.
> - Both versions present identical loading/empty/error behavior.

**Anchors:** _Waiting, All the Way Up_ (the event-loop and Observable floors — this is the module that book was written to enable); the function-first React/TypeScript guide.

### Ground Floor — How JavaScript waits

*Read this before the Briefing. It assumes you can write React and TypeScript, but not that you can explain what actually happens between a keystroke and a rendered result. You'll build a naive search box over a throwaway list first, against a slow server you control. This tells you what each piece is for and what to look up, never how to write it. Debouncing, the race fix, and RxJS operators beyond the basics are left for the module.*

#### GF.1 — The picture first

```
keystroke ─▶ input event ─▶ React state update ─▶ effect runs ─▶ fetch("/search?q=re")
keystroke ─▶ input event ─▶ React state update ─▶ effect runs ─▶ fetch("/search?q=rea")
                                                                      │       │
                                     network: each one takes its own time
                                                                      ▼       ▼
                                               responses land in WHATEVER order they finish
                                                      ─▶ setResults(...) ─▶ render
```

Everything in this module happens because the arrows on the right don't come back in the order the arrows on the left went out.

#### GF.2 — The event loop, from zero

**Look up:** *Waiting, All the Way Up*, the event-loop chapters. Then fill gaps with the following:

- The call stack, the task queue, and the microtask queue
- Why JavaScript is single-threaded and still doesn't freeze while a network request is pending
- Where `fetch` actually does its waiting (not on your thread)
- Promises: pending, fulfilled, rejected; `.then` chains; what `async`/`await` turns into

**Question to settle:** you call `fetch` twice in a row. Which finishes first? What decides it?

#### GF.3 — A slow server you control

You can't study ordering against a server that always answers instantly.

**Build:** a tiny search endpoint over a hard-coded list of words (fruit, cities, anything) that waits a **random** delay before answering. Make the delay range wide enough that answers often come back out of order.

**Look up:** a minimal HTTP server in Node (or reuse FastAPI from M2); `setTimeout` as an artificial delay; query string parsing; and either the Vite proxy from M1 or CORS headers, so your React app can reach it.

#### GF.4 — The naive search box

**Build:** an input, a results list, and a fetch that fires on every change. Use the simplest thing that works.

**Look up:**

- Controlled inputs in React
- `useEffect` dependency arrays: when the effect re-runs, and when its cleanup function runs
- The Network tab in DevTools: filtering to fetch requests, reading each one's timing, and throttling the connection

#### GF.5 — Run it and observe

Type a word quickly. Then watch.

**Notice and leave alone:**

1. **Count requests vs keystrokes** in the Network tab. Don't fix it. That's M5's debounce decision.
2. **Find a moment where the results on screen don't match the input.** It'll happen with the random delay; if it doesn't, widen the range. Write down exactly which request's answer you're looking at. That's the trap. Don't fix it here.
3. **There's no loading, empty, or error state yet,** or there's a crude one. Leave it; both builds need identical ones.

#### GF.6 — Cancellation, as a concept

**Look up:** `AbortController` and `AbortSignal`; what happens to a fetch when it's aborted; what an aborted fetch's promise does (and what error it rejects with); and whether the *server* knows a request was aborted.

Don't wire it into your search box yet. Just abort one fetch by hand in a scratch file and see what happens.

#### GF.7 — Observables, from zero

**Look up, in this order:**

- What an Observable is, vs a Promise: one value vs many over time; lazy vs eager
- `subscribe`, the observer object (`next`, `error`, `complete`), and unsubscribing
- `fromEvent` for turning DOM events into a stream
- `pipe`, and the two simplest operators, `map` and `filter`
- How to subscribe inside a React component and clean up on unmount

**Build:** in a scratch component, turn the input's events into a stream, `map` them to the input's text, and log every value. That's all. The time and cancellation operators are M5's real material.

**Question to settle:** if you forget to unsubscribe when the component unmounts, what keeps running?

#### GF.8 — Hand-off: what's yours in M5

| Ground Floor left this… | M5 asks you to… |
|---|---|
| A request per keystroke | Choose debounce vs throttle, then build it by hand in version (a) |
| Out-of-order answers overwriting newer ones | Make a stale result provably impossible, in both versions |
| `AbortController` tried in isolation | Decide between cancelling stale requests and ignoring their answers |
| A stream that only logs | Build the full reactive pipeline, and know the `switchMap` family |
| No retry | Decide on retry and backoff |
| Crude or missing states | Identical loading, empty, and error states in both builds |

Then read the Briefing below.

#### GF.9 — Check yourself before moving on

- Explain, using the event loop, why two fetches can come back in either order.
- When does a React effect's cleanup function run?
- What does aborting a fetch do on the client, and what does it *not* undo on the server?
- Promise vs Observable: name two differences you could demonstrate in code.
- Describe one stale result you saw: which input was on screen, and which request's answer was showing?

### Briefing

"Reactive programming" sounds like a paradigm you need a philosophy degree for. It isn't. It's one idea: instead of _asking_ for a value when you want it (pull), you _declare_ what should happen whenever a new value arrives (push), and something else runs your declaration each time. A stream of keystrokes, a stream of mouse moves, a stream of server responses — RxJS models all of them as an **Observable**, a thing you can filter, delay, and cancel with the same vocabulary. The way to learn whether it earns its complexity is to build the same feature twice — once with plain async/await, once reactive — and feel the difference in your hands. The center of gravity is **cancellation and time**: what a program does when answers arrive out of order.

### The trap

The **stale-response race**, and it is the single most instructive bug in async UI. You type "rea," a request goes out; you type "react," a second goes out; the _first_ request happens to be slower and returns _last_, overwriting your "react" results with "rea" results. The UI now shows answers to a question the user already changed. Every naive typeahead has this bug. Fixing it is the whole point, and the two implementations fix it in tellingly different ways.

### The feature

> The same typeahead search, built twice against one deliberately slow, jittery endpoint you control: **(a)** `fetch` + `AbortController` + async/await, with a debounce you write by hand. **(b)** RxJS: a keystroke stream through `debounceTime` → `distinctUntilChanged` → `switchMap` into the request. Identical user-facing contract: a loading state, an empty state, an error state, and — provably — never a stale result on screen.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- Typing quickly fires far fewer requests than keystrokes (debounce works).
- A staged race — where an earlier request is artificially slower than a later one — never shows the earlier request's results.
- Both versions present identical loading/empty/error behavior to the user.

### Decision map

1. **Debounce vs throttle.** Options: debounce (wait for a pause) · throttle (at most one per interval). _Axis:_ fewest requests vs steady responsiveness. _Forcing:_ For a search box, do you want to fire _during_ fast typing or only after the user pauses? Which one would you pick for a scroll handler instead, and why is that different?
2. **How you defeat the race.** Options: _cancel_ the stale request (abort) · _ignore_ the stale response when it lands. _Axis:_ saving server work vs client simplicity. _Forcing:_ If you only ignore stale responses, did the server still do the work? If you abort, what has to be true about your fetch layer?
3. **The switchMap family (the classic interview question).** Options: `switchMap` · `mergeMap` · `concatMap` · `exhaustMap`. _Axis:_ what to do when a new input arrives mid-flight — cancel the old, run both, queue, or ignore the new. _Forcing:_ For a typeahead you want `switchMap`'s "cancel the old" — but can you say which of the other three you'd want for a "submit button that must not double-fire," and which for "upload these files in order"?
4. **Retry and backoff.** Options: no retry · retry with increasing delay. _Axis:_ resilience vs hammering a struggling server. _Forcing:_ If the endpoint fails once, should you retry immediately? What does exponential backoff protect?
5. **The verdict you must argue.** _When do Observables earn their weight over promises?_ This is not a decision I answer for you — it's the one you write a paragraph on after building both, and defend in the design-review room.

### Research targets

- _Push vs pull — what makes something "reactive"?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain, tab closed, the difference between calling a function for a value and declaring a reaction to a stream. _Seed:_ Waiting, All the Way Up — the Observables section.
- _`debounceTime`, `distinctUntilChanged`, `switchMap` — what each does to a stream._ — **25 min.** Done when you can narrate what happens to the keystroke stream at each stage. _Seed:_ rxmarbles.com — watch the marble diagrams move.
- _`AbortController` — how a fetch is actually cancelled._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say what abort does to the in-flight request and how you wire it to a new keystroke.
- _switchMap vs mergeMap vs concatMap vs exhaustMap._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can match each to a real scenario without looking. _Seed:_ one good marble-diagram comparison.

### Out of scope (fence)

Full RxJS operator catalogue, Subjects and multicasting, schedulers, building your own Observable from scratch (fascinating — park it), state-management libraries. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Open the Network tab; type a word fast. **The number:** keystrokes vs requests actually sent (debounce working?), and cancelled-request count (race defense working?). Then stage the 3-seconds-then-1-second response race and confirm, on screen, that the stale result never wins.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>My hand-rolled debounce fires anyway / fires twice</summary>

**Orient:** debounce is a timer that gets _reset_ on every new event; the bug is almost always in the reset, not the timer.

**Approach:** each keystroke must cancel the previously scheduled call before scheduling a new one. If you're storing the timer handle somewhere that resets between keystrokes (a fresh closure each render, say), you've lost the handle you needed to cancel. Think about _where_ the pending-timer reference has to live to survive across keystrokes.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ in your own words, what sequence of events puts wrong results on screen in the stale-response race? **Check:** you can draw the two-request timeline on paper.
2. **Research.** The four targets; the switchMap-family question gets the deep box and rxmarbles gets real time. _Objective:_ each operator matched to a scenario, no tab.
3. **Commit (DSA).** Debounce vs throttle, cancel vs ignore, and the operator choice. **Check:** written, with rejected alternatives.
4. **Build — the rig.** The slow, jittery endpoint you control. **Check:** you can dial latency per request — you'll need it to stage the race.
5. **Build — version (a).** fetch + AbortController + hand-rolled debounce. **Check:** keystrokes ≫ requests in the Network tab; staged race never shows stale results.
6. **Build — version (b).** The Rx pipeline: `debounceTime` → `distinctUntilChanged` → `switchMap`. **Check:** same two proofs, same user-facing states as (a).
7. **Measure & debrief.** Count keystrokes vs requests vs cancellations; run the 3s-then-1s race in both. _Ask:_ so when do Observables earn their weight? Write the paragraph — it's the module's real deliverable. Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** show request-vs-keystroke counts; run the staged race in both versions and show neither shows stale data.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a typeahead twice to compare async/await with RxJS. The core problem both solve is the stale-response race — here's the scenario — and `switchMap`'s cancel-the-previous behavior expresses it in one operator, which is the argument for Rx here."
- **Design review:** a peer says "RxJS is overkill, just debounce a fetch." Given your two working versions, when are they _right_? Name the complexity threshold where Observables start paying for themselves.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I finally get RxJS — I built the same search box with and without it, and the stale-request race is the thing that makes the reactive version worth the learning curve."
- **Reach-back (time thread):** the WebSocket chat in M7 is another stream. Which of these operators would help you handle a flood of incoming messages?

## M6 — The Cache You Can Explain · LRU in React · (M) · _hosts the rep circuit_

> **Story.** As an engineer drilling for interviews while building real tools, I want a genuinely O(1) LRU cache wired into my React app, so that I can whiteboard the two-structures-as-one trick and prove the constant-time claim with a benchmark — and open the rep circuit that runs under the rest of the campaign. **MVP.** Typed `get`/`put`, correct eviction, a flatness benchmark, and a React fetch hook that serves hits without the network. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - `get` returns and bumps recency; `put` beyond capacity evicts exactly the least-recently-used entry.
> - The benchmark stays flat from 10³ to 10⁶ entries.
> - A cache hit in the React app skips the network (visible in DevTools).

**Anchors:** `leetcode_thinking_toolbox.md` (the primary text — this module is where its pattern vocabulary goes live); `DATA_STRUCTURES___ALGORITHMS.md`.

### Ground Floor — Nodes, pointers, and how to time code

*Read this before the Briefing. It assumes you know TypeScript and basic Big-O vocabulary, but not that you've built a linked structure by hand or benchmarked anything. You'll build the separate pieces first: a linked list on its own, a timing harness, and an uncached fetch hook. This tells you what each is for and what to look up, never how to write it. Combining a map and a list into an O(1) LRU is the whole module, so none of that is here.*

#### GF.1 — The picture first

```
What you'll have after the Ground Floor (separate pieces, not yet connected):

  Map<key, value>          a linked list of nodes          a timer harness
  ┌─────┬───────┐          [A] ─▶ [B] ─▶ [C] ─▶ null       N = 10³ … 10⁶
  │ key │ value │                                          ops/sec per N
  └─────┴───────┘

  useThing(id) ─── fetch ───▶ server        (every call goes to the network)
```

M6 is about wiring the first two into one structure and putting it between the hook and the network.

#### GF.2 — Big-O, honestly

**Look up:** `leetcode_thinking_toolbox.md` and the DSA book in this project, specifically the sections on complexity and on hash tables. Then confirm the following:

- Average vs worst case, and what "amortized" means
- Why hash lookup is O(1) on average, and what makes it degrade
- Why finding an item in an array or list *by value* is O(n), however you write the loop
- How `Map` differs from a plain object in JavaScript (key types, iteration order, size)

**Question to settle:** an operation does one hash lookup and then one loop over every entry. What's its complexity?

#### GF.3 — TypeScript classes and generics

**Look up:** classes in TypeScript (fields, constructors, `private`); generic type parameters on a class; constraining a generic with `extends`; and how to write a type for "a node that holds a value of type T and points to another node, or nothing."

#### GF.4 — A linked list on its own

**Build:** a singly linked list with no connection to any cache. It should let you add to the front, add to the end, remove from the front, find a node by value, and print itself in order. Draw each operation on paper before you code it.

**Look up:** singly vs doubly linked lists; head and tail pointers; and the edge cases that break naive code (empty list, one element, removing the tail).

**Question to settle:** for each operation you built, what's its complexity? Which one is O(n), and why can't a singly linked list fix that?

Don't build the doubly linked list the LRU needs. That decision, and its design, belong to the module.

#### GF.5 — A benchmark harness

**Responsibility:** answer "does this operation stay flat as the structure grows?" with numbers instead of a feeling.

**Look up:**

- `performance.now()` vs `Date.now()`, and why resolution matters
- Warm-up runs and why the first measurements in a JIT-compiled runtime lie
- Running many operations and measuring the total, rather than timing one
- Running the benchmark in Node instead of the browser, and why that's steadier

**Build:** a harness that fills a structure to size N, times a large batch of one operation, and prints time per operation for N from a thousand to a million. Test it on two operations you already know the answer for: `Map.get` (should stay flat) and your linked list's find-by-value (shouldn't).

#### GF.6 — The uncached fetch hook

**Build:** a React hook that fetches one record by id, from whichever throwaway API you built earlier. A component that lets you switch between a few ids.

**Look up:** custom hooks; `useRef` vs `useState` (what survives a re-render, what causes one); module-level variables in an ES module and how many copies exist; and React Context.

#### GF.7 — Run it and observe

**Notice and leave alone:**

1. **Your benchmark's two lines.** One should be flat, one should climb. If they don't, fix the harness before trusting it with your LRU.
2. **Network tab while you switch back and forth between two ids.** Every switch is a request. That's what M6's cache removes.
3. **The three places you just read about holding state** (a module variable, a ref, context). Where a cache instance should live is M6's fifth decision; don't pick yet.

#### GF.8 — Hand-off: what's yours in M6

| Ground Floor left this… | M6 asks you to… |
|---|---|
| A map and a list, unconnected | Combine them into one structure with O(1) `get` and `put` |
| A singly linked list | Decide whether to build the doubly linked list or lean on `Map` order, and defend it |
| A benchmark tested on known cases | Prove your LRU stays flat from 10³ to 10⁶ |
| A hook that always hits the network | Serve repeat lookups from the cache, visibly |
| Three possible homes for state | Choose where the cache instance lives, and how it's invalidated |
| No eviction | Design the eviction API |

Then read the Briefing below. From here on, start each build block with two timed rep-circuit problems, as the Briefing explains.

#### GF.9 — Check yourself before moving on

- Draw removing the tail of a singly linked list. Why is it O(n)?
- What does "O(1) amortized" mean, and which built-in structure you used depends on it?
- Why is the first round of a JavaScript benchmark untrustworthy?
- What did your benchmark show for `Map.get` and for list find, at 10³ and 10⁶?
- `useRef` vs a module-level variable: which is shared across components, and which dies on unmount?

### Briefing

A cache trades memory for time: keep recent answers around so you don't recompute or re-fetch them. But memory is finite, so a cache needs an _eviction policy_ — a rule for what to throw out when it's full. **LRU** (Least Recently Used) throws out whatever hasn't been touched in the longest time, on the bet that recently-used things will be used again. Building one that is genuinely O(1) — constant time for both read and write, no matter how full — is a rite-of-passage data-structures problem _and_ a real tool you'll drop into the React app to cache API responses. The center of gravity is **the two data structures working as one**: a hash map for instant lookup, a linked list for instant reordering.

This module also **hosts the rep circuit**: from here on, start build blocks with two timed problems (20–25 min) drawn from the toolbox's pattern vocabulary — two-pointer, sliding window, hashing, BFS/DFS, and the rest — spaced across the whole campaign, never crammed. The circuit is not extra; it's how recognition becomes reflex.

### The trap

The **O(1) claim that is secretly O(n)**. It's easy to build an LRU that _works_ and quietly does a linear scan to find the least-recently-used item, or to shuffle an array on every access. It passes every correctness test and is slow exactly when it matters. And in React specifically: JavaScript's `Map` preserves insertion order, which _tempts_ you to lean on that ordering and skip building the linked list — which teaches you nothing about why the data structure is shaped the way it is.

### The feature

> A generic, typed LRU cache with `get(key)` and `put(key, value)`, both O(1), evicting the least-recently-used entry when capacity is exceeded — plus a small benchmark proving the constant-time claim empirically. Then wire it into the React app as the cache behind a data-fetching hook (e.g. caching the incident lookups from M1).

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- `get` on a present key returns the value _and_ marks it most-recently-used.
- `put` beyond capacity evicts exactly the least-recently-used entry.
- The benchmark shows operation time staying flat as the cache grows from a thousand to a million entries.
- The React hook serves cache hits without a network request (visible in the Network tab).

### Decision map

1. **Exploit `Map` insertion order vs build an explicit doubly-linked list.** Options: lean on `Map` ordering (delete-and-reinsert to bump recency) · hand-build a DLL + hash map. _Axis:_ fewer lines vs understanding what's underneath. _Forcing:_ Which one could you _explain on a whiteboard_ in an interview? Which one hides the mechanism the interviewer is actually asking about? (For learning, the harder path is the point — but name the trade honestly.)
2. **Eviction API design.** Options: silent eviction · an eviction callback/event. _Axis:_ simplicity vs letting the caller react (e.g. to clean up). _Forcing:_ Does anything need to _happen_ when an entry is evicted, or is dropping it enough for your React use?
3. **Key equality and generics.** Options: string keys only · generic keys with a constraint. _Axis:_ simplicity vs reuse. _Forcing:_ If keys can be objects, what does "same key" mean, and does that reach back to the hashability problem from the hash-map thread?
4. **Invalidation in the React layer.** Options: never invalidate (pure LRU) · time-to-live on entries. _Axis:_ max hit rate vs never serving stale data. _Forcing:_ For cached incident data, is a five-minute-old answer acceptable? Who decides — the cache or the caller?
5. **Where the cache instance lives in React.** Options: module-level singleton · `useRef` per component · context. _Axis:_ sharing across components vs isolation and testability. _Forcing:_ Should two components fetching the same incident share a cache hit? What happens to a `useRef` cache on unmount?

### Research targets

- _Why does LRU need both a hash map and a linked list?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can say what each structure gives you and why neither alone is O(1) for both operations. _Seed:_ your toolbox doc's data-structure section.
- _What is a doubly-linked list and why doubly?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain why you need the _previous_ pointer to remove a node in O(1).
- _How do you empirically test an O(1) claim?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can describe a benchmark that would _expose_ a hidden O(n).
- _Rep-circuit orientation (ongoing):_ skim the toolbox's pattern list and its recognition signals. Done when you can name the 8–10 patterns and one trigger phrase for each. _Seed:_ leetcode_thinking_toolbox.md.

### Out of scope (fence)

Other eviction policies (LFU, ARC, clock — note them, park them), thread-safety/concurrency, cache-stampede handling, a full React-Query-style fetching library. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Benchmark `get`/`put` at 10³ vs 10⁶ entries. **The number:** does per-operation time stay flat (O(1)) or climb (you've got a hidden linear scan)? Plus the rep-circuit scoreboard each session: solved cold / needed a hint / needed to look it up.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>I can do O(1) lookup or O(1) reordering, not both at once</summary>

**Orient:** you already met this shape — a hash map gives instant _find_, a linked list gives instant _splice_; the trick is making them point at the same nodes.

**Approach:** the hash map maps key → the _linked-list node_ holding that entry (not the value directly). Lookup finds the node instantly; because you hold the node, you can unlink and move it to the front instantly (that's why doubly-linked — you need its neighbors). Eviction removes the tail node, and — the step people forget — also deletes that key from the hash map.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ why is a cache that scans for its victim still "correct" — and when would you ever notice it isn't O(1)? **Check:** you can describe the test that would expose it.
2. **Research.** The three targets plus the rep-circuit orientation. _Objective:_ you can say why neither a map nor a list alone gives O(1) for both operations.
3. **Commit (DSA).** The `Map`-ordering shortcut vs the explicit doubly-linked list — decide like it's an interview. **Check:** five decisions written.
4. **Build — the structure.** `get`/`put` with eviction. **Check:** a scripted sequence proves recency bumping and exact LRU eviction.
5. **Walk into the trap.** Benchmark 10³ vs 10⁶ entries. _Ask:_ flat or climbing — and if it climbs, which line of yours is the hidden scan? **Check:** flat curve in hand.
6. **Build — the wiring.** The React fetch hook backed by your cache. **Check:** second fetch of the same incident makes no network request.
7. **Measure & debrief.** Keep the flatness chart; start the rep-circuit scoreboard (cold / hinted / looked-up). _Ask:_ could you whiteboard the node-pointing-map diagram right now? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** show the flat benchmark curve; show a cache hit skipping the network in the React app.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built an O(1) LRU with a hash map over a doubly-linked list. The subtle part is that the map points at list _nodes_, which is what makes eviction and recency-bump both constant time — and I benchmarked it to prove it stays flat at a million entries."
- **Design review:** a peer used the `Map`-insertion-order shortcut. Is it wrong? Discuss when the shortcut is fine and when you'd insist on the explicit structure.
- **Meetup (30s):** "Classic LRU cache, but I actually benchmarked mine to catch the sneaky O(n) version — then dropped it into a React fetch hook."
- **Reach-back (hash thread):** your cache's hash map is doing exactly what a database index does. Why does Postgres reach for a _btree_ index instead of a hash one for range queries? (You'll answer this properly around M2/M18.)

## M7 — The Room That Talks · WebSocket chat · (M)

> **Story.** As a full-stack engineer, I want a minimal WebSocket chat with presence and history, so that I own the concrete bidirectional stream under every "live" feature and the reconnect/broadcast decisions that come with it. **MVP.** Multiple clients, near-instant fan-out, presence, and history for late joiners; two browser windows are the demo rig. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - A message from one client appears near-instantly in the others.
> - Presence reflects connects and disconnects.
> - A new joiner sees what was said before them.

**Anchors:** _Waiting, All the Way Up_ (the event-loop and async floors); the networking primer.

### Ground Floor — A connection that stays open

*Read this before the Briefing. It assumes you've built HTTP endpoints but not a persistent connection. You'll build an echo server and then a dumb broadcast first, using two browser windows. This tells you what each piece is for and what to look up, never how to write it. Presence, history, delivery guarantees, and reconnection are the module's work.*

#### GF.1 — The picture first

```
HTTP (M1–M4):     client ──request──▶ server ──response──▶ client      (line goes quiet)

WebSocket:        client ──HTTP GET + "Upgrade: websocket"──▶ server
                  ◀────────── 101 Switching Protocols ──────────
                  ════════ one TCP connection, held open ════════
                  client ◀──── message ────▶ server   (either side, any time)
                                 ...
                  either side closes, or the network drops (sometimes silently)
```

The server keeps a **set of open connections** in memory. Everything in M7 is about what you store alongside that set, and what happens when one disappears.

#### GF.2 — The protocol, from zero

**Look up:**

- Why plain HTTP can't let the server speak first, and the older workarounds (polling, long-polling, Server-Sent Events)
- The WebSocket handshake: the upgrade request, status 101, and what happens to the HTTP connection afterward
- Frames, text vs binary messages, and close codes
- `ws://` vs `wss://`, and why production always uses the second

**Do:** open DevTools on any site that uses WebSockets, find the connection in the Network tab, and read its message frames live.

#### GF.3 — An echo server

**Build:** a Node server, using the `ws` package, that accepts connections and sends every message straight back to whoever sent it. Log each connection, message, and close on the server.

**Look up:** the `ws` server's connection, message, and close events; the per-connection socket object; and how to run it alongside (or inside) an ordinary HTTP server.

#### GF.4 — The browser side

**Build:** a plain page, or a React component, that opens a connection, shows every message received, and has an input to send one.

**Look up:**

- The browser `WebSocket` API: its `open`, `message`, `close`, and `error` events, `send`, and `readyState`
- Why messages are strings or binary, so you'll `JSON.stringify` and `JSON.parse` your own envelope
- In React: opening a connection in an effect, closing it in the cleanup, and what Strict Mode's double-mounting does to your connection count in development

#### GF.5 — A message envelope

**Responsibility:** one agreed shape for everything that travels over the socket, so either side can tell kinds of message apart.

**Do:** write the envelope down as a contract before coding: a `type` field and a payload. This is allowed; it's a spec, like M1's contract.

**Question to settle:** once you have more than one kind of message, how does the receiver know which code should handle it?

#### GF.6 — Dumb broadcast

**Build:** change the echo so every message is sent to **every** connected client, not just the sender. Open two windows and talk between them.

**Look up:** keeping a collection of connected sockets on the server; iterating over it to send; and checking a socket is still open before sending to it.

#### GF.7 — Run it and observe

**Notice and leave alone:**

1. **Close one window.** Watch the server log. How long until the server knows, and does it always know? Try killing the browser process or turning off wifi instead of closing the tab. That gap is where M7's ghosts come from.
2. **Restart the server.** What do both windows show? What happened to everything that was said? Who's "online" now?
3. **Open a third window after some messages have been sent.** What does it see?

Don't fix any of these. They're presence, persistence, and reconnection: the module's decisions.

**Look up**, to understand what you saw: TCP keepalive vs WebSocket ping/pong, and why a dead connection can look alive for a while.

#### GF.8 — Hand-off: what's yours in M7

| Ground Floor left this… | M7 asks you to… |
|---|---|
| Raw `ws` and the browser API, because they're simplest | Decide raw vs a library, and what the library would hand you |
| Nothing kept except the socket set | Split ephemeral state from persistent state, and put each where it belongs |
| Late joiners see nothing | Give them history |
| Closed windows linger, or don't, unpredictably | Presence with no ghosts |
| Messages sent once, hope for the best | Choose a delivery guarantee |
| A dropped connection just stays dropped | Handle reconnection and catching up |
| Everyone gets everything | Decide broadcast scope |

Then read the Briefing below.

#### GF.9 — Check yourself before moving on

- Walk through the handshake: what the client sends, what the server answers, what the connection becomes.
- Why can't plain HTTP do what you just built? What did people do before WebSockets?
- Why does your server need a collection of sockets, and what's in it right after a server restart?
- How did the server learn a window closed, and in which case didn't it?
- Why `JSON.stringify` an envelope instead of sending bare text?

### Briefing

Ordinary HTTP is a series of one-shot exchanges: the client asks, the server answers, the line goes quiet. That's fine for fetching a page, useless for a chat, where the _server_ needs to speak first ("someone else just messaged you") without being asked. A **WebSocket** is a single connection, held open, that either side can send down at any time — a real two-way pipe instead of a call-and-response. Building a chat is the canonical way to learn it, and it teaches a distinction that reaches far past chat: **ephemeral state vs persistent state**. The center of gravity is **the persistent connection and what travels over it**.

### The trap

**Putting everything in the persistent store, including things that shouldn't persist** — and its cousin, forgetting connections die. "Who is currently online" and "who is typing" are _ephemeral_: true only while a connection lives, meaningless after it drops. Message history is _persistent_: it must survive disconnects and restarts. Conflate them and you get ghosts — users shown online forever because nobody cleaned up when their socket closed — and a presence list that grows without bound.

### The feature

> A minimal real-time chat: multiple clients connect over a WebSocket, messages sent by one appear near-instantly for all, a presence indicator shows who's currently connected, and message history persists so a new joiner sees what was said before them. Two browser windows are your demo rig.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- A message typed in window A appears in window B without a refresh.
- Closing window A removes that user from window B's presence list (no ghost).
- A window joining late sees prior message history; presence shows only the currently-connected.

### Decision map

1. **Raw WebSocket vs a library (Socket.IO et al.).** Options: the browser's `WebSocket` API directly · a library with reconnection/fallbacks. _Axis:_ learning the primitive vs production niceties for free. _Forcing:_ What does the library do for you that you'd otherwise hand-write — and is learning that hand-work the goal this month?
2. **Ephemeral vs persistent state — the split.** Options: keep presence in server memory, messages in a database · everything in one store. _Axis:_ correctness vs uniformity. _Forcing:_ When the server restarts, what _should_ vanish and what _must_ remain? Where does each kind of state therefore have to live?
3. **Message delivery guarantees.** Options: fire-and-forget · acknowledged delivery. _Axis:_ simplicity vs reliability. _Forcing:_ If a message is sent while a client is momentarily disconnected, is losing it acceptable for a chat? What would change your answer for, say, a trading app?
4. **Handling reconnection.** Options: ignore drops · detect and reconnect, re-sync missed messages. _Axis:_ simplicity vs real-network survival. _Forcing:_ Phones lose connection constantly — what does your UI show during a drop, and how does it catch up after?
5. **Broadcast scope.** Options: broadcast to everyone · rooms/channels. _Axis:_ trivial vs scalable structure. _Forcing:_ Does every message go to every connected client? At what point does that become a problem?

### Research targets

- _How does a WebSocket differ from HTTP, and how does the connection start?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain the upgrade handshake in one breath. _Seed:_ MDN's WebSocket page.
- _Ephemeral vs persistent state — where each lives._ — **25 min.** Done when you can classify presence, typing indicators, and history correctly and say why.
- _What happens on the server when a client's socket closes?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can describe the close event and what cleanup it must trigger to avoid ghosts.
- _Reconnection and missed-message re-sync — the pattern._ — **25 min.** Done when you can sketch how a reconnecting client asks "what did I miss?"

### Out of scope (fence)

Horizontal scaling across multiple server instances (the pub/sub-backplane problem — note it, park it), end-to-end encryption, typing indicators as a full feature, read receipts, WebRTC peer-to-peer. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Two windows. Send a message: **the number** is the visible latency to appearance (should feel instant). Then close one window and confirm presence updates in the other within a beat — no ghost. Optionally inject artificial latency and watch behavior.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>Ghost users — closing a tab doesn't remove someone from the online list</summary>

**Orient:** presence is only true while a connection is alive, so the list has to be maintained by connection _lifecycle events_, not by messages.

**Approach:** the server holds a live set of connected users. Two events mutate it: a connection opening (add), and a connection _closing_ (remove) — and it's the close handler people forget to wire. On any change, broadcast the new presence set. If tabs still ghost, your close event isn't firing or isn't finding the right user to remove.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ what does HTTP's request/response shape make awkward that a socket makes natural — and what new failure modes do you inherit in exchange? **Check:** two of each, aloud.
2. **Research.** The module's targets, timeboxed. _Objective:_ you can narrate a WebSocket handshake and a broadcast without notes.
3. **Commit (DSA).** Message shape, history storage, reconnect policy. **Check:** written with rejected alternatives.
4. **Build — the room.** Server plus two browser windows exchanging messages. **Check:** a message in one window appears near-instantly in the other.
5. **Build — presence.** Joins and leaves visibly tracked. **Check:** closing a window updates the other's presence list.
6. **Build — memory.** History persisted; late joiners catch up. **Check:** a third window opened late sees the conversation.
7. **Measure & debrief.** Kill the server mid-chat and watch both clients; reconnect. _Ask:_ what did your reconnect policy silently promise about missed messages? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** the two-window exchange; the clean presence update on disconnect; a late joiner seeing history.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a WebSocket chat, and the design decision I care about is the ephemeral/persistent split — presence lives in server memory and dies with the connection, history lives in the database and survives restarts. Conflating them is how you get ghost users."
- **Design review:** a peer put presence in the database "to be safe." Walk through why that's the wrong store, and when persistence of presence-like data _would_ be justified.
- **Meetup (30s):** "Real-time chat over WebSockets — the fun bug was ghost users lingering online forever, which turned out to be a lesson about what state deserves to persist."
- **Reach-back (contract thread):** this is your `incidents` domain going live — imagine pushing incident updates over this socket instead of chat messages. What in your M1 contract would need to change to become a _stream_ instead of a _fetch_?

---

# PART III — Data: Analysis & Migration

_Data at rest and data in motion between shapes. Analyze it in two languages (M8), do a research-grade analytics slice aimed at the kind of work the Penn role wants (M9), and move it from one schema to another without losing a row (M10). This is the back half of the data-lifecycle thread._

---

## M8 — Two Languages, One Question · data analysis in Python + R · (S)

> **Story.** As an analyst who wants range, I want one real question answered twice — pandas and tidyverse — to the same number, so that I can move between the two data dialects and trust a notebook only when it reproduces. **MVP.** Two notebooks, one dataset, one headline figure, each with a grouped aggregation and a chart. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - Both notebooks, restarted and run top-to-bottom, produce the same headline figure.
> - Each includes a grouped aggregation and a chart that supports a claim in the prose.
> - A reader who knows neither dialect can follow the story from the markdown alone.

**Anchors:** the Python and data-science books in this project (the pandas/SQL material); `Databases_and_DBMS_info.md`.

### Ground Floor — Notebooks and two data dialects

*Read this before the Briefing. It assumes you can write Python but haven't done much pandas, and have never touched R. You'll get both running and answer a toy question in each first. This tells you what each piece is for and what to look up, never how to write it. The real dataset, the real question, and reproducibility discipline are the module's.*

#### GF.1 — The picture first

```
notebook (.ipynb / .qmd)
  ├─ markdown cell     prose: the question, the claim
  ├─ code cell  ──▶ KERNEL (a live Python or R process holding every variable you've made)
  │                    ◀── output: a table, a number, a chart
  └─ ... cells can run in ANY order; the kernel remembers ALL of it
```

The kernel's memory is the whole story of this module's trap. Know it's there from the start.

#### GF.2 — Jupyter, from zero

**Look up:** installing JupyterLab in a virtual environment; what a kernel is; the execution counter beside each cell; and the "Restart kernel and run all" command.

**Do:** make a notebook, define a variable in cell 2, use it in cell 3, delete cell 2, and run cell 3 again. Explain what you see.

#### GF.3 — pandas, the first hour

**Look up, in this order:**

- What a DataFrame and a Series are
- Reading a CSV; `head`, `info`, `describe`, `dtypes`
- Selecting columns, filtering rows with a boolean mask, sorting
- Adding a derived column
- Split-apply-combine as an *idea*, then what `groupby` does in pandas
- One chart from a DataFrame (pandas' built-in plotting or matplotlib)

**Do:** on any small public CSV, answer one toy question with one grouped aggregation and one chart.

#### GF.4 — R and the tidyverse, the first hour

**Do:** install R, then an environment to run it in (RStudio, or an R kernel in Jupyter, or Quarto). Install the tidyverse.

**Look up, in this order:**

- R basics: vectors, `<-`, data frames vs tibbles, how packages load
- `readr` for reading the CSV
- The `dplyr` verbs: `filter`, `select`, `mutate`, `arrange`, `group_by`, `summarise`
- The pipe (`|>` or `%>%`) and why the tidyverse is built around it
- One `ggplot2` chart: data, aesthetics mapping, a geom

**Do:** the *same* toy question on the *same* CSV, in R. Check you get the same number.

#### GF.5 — Missing values, as a concept

**Look up:** how pandas represents missing data (`NaN`, `None`, `pd.NA`) and how R does (`NA`); and what a mean or sum does by default in each when it meets one.

**Do:** blank out a few values in your toy CSV and rerun both. Did either number change? Did either warn you?

Don't decide how to handle missing data yet. That's M8's third decision.

#### GF.6 — Run it and observe

**Notice and leave alone:**

1. **Restart and run all** on both toy notebooks. Did both still work? If either broke, find out why. That's M8's trap in miniature.
2. **Put your pandas group-by and your dplyr version side by side.** Which reads more like the sentence you'd say out loud? Hold that thought for the first decision.
3. **Your file was tiny.** Ask yourself what happens to "load everything into a DataFrame" at a few million rows.

#### GF.7 — Hand-off: what's yours in M8

| Ground Floor left this… | M8 asks you to… |
|---|---|
| A toy CSV and a toy question | One real question, on a real dataset that isn't toy-tiny |
| Both dialects tried | Decide which idiom expresses your transform better, and say why |
| Two separate notebooks by default | Decide whether to keep them apart or bridge them |
| Missing values noticed | Choose how to handle them, knowing how each language represents them |
| All work done in memory | Decide whether heavy aggregation should happen in SQL first |
| One restart-and-run-all test | Make both notebooks reproduce, every time |

Then read the Briefing below.

#### GF.8 — Check yourself before moving on

- What is a kernel, and what does it remember that the notebook file doesn't show?
- Describe split-apply-combine, then name the pandas method and the dplyr verbs that do it.
- What does a mean do with a missing value by default in pandas, and in R?
- What does `ggplot2` mean by an *aesthetic*?
- Did your two toy notebooks agree? If not, why not?

### Briefing

A data-analysis notebook is a document that interleaves prose, code, and its output — you think in it, and the reader watches you think. Two languages own this space for different reasons: **Python** (with pandas) is the general-purpose workhorse that also does everything else; **R** (with the tidyverse) was built by statisticians for exactly this and often expresses a transformation more directly. Doing the _same_ analysis in both, in one project, teaches the shape of data work independent of either dialect — and being able to read both is a real edge, because data teams are mixed. The center of gravity is **the split-apply-combine pattern**: group the data, do something per group, stitch the results back — the move under almost every real analysis.

### The trap

The **notebook that doesn't reproduce**. Cells can be run out of order, and a notebook carries hidden state from every cell you ran and then deleted or edited. So a notebook can show a beautiful result that _cannot be regenerated_ from a fresh top-to-bottom run — the analysis equivalent of a lie. Restart-and-run-all is the only honest test, and most notebooks fail it the first time.

### The feature

> One real question answered over one real dataset (public or synthetic, but not toy-tiny), carried out **twice** — once in a Python/pandas notebook, once in R/tidyverse — reaching the same numeric answer. The analysis must include at least one group-by aggregation and one visualization, and the notebook must pass restart-and-run-all cleanly.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- Both notebooks, restarted and run top-to-bottom, produce the same headline figure.
- Each includes a grouped aggregation and at least one chart that actually supports a claim in the prose.
- A reader who knows neither dialect can follow the _story_ from the markdown alone.

### Decision map

1. **pandas idiom vs tidyverse idiom for the same transform.** Options: method-chaining in pandas · pipe-and-verb in tidyverse. _Axis:_ which expresses _this_ transformation more legibly. _Forcing:_ Take your group-by — which language says it in fewer moving parts, and can you articulate _why_ (the tidyverse was built for this shape)?
2. **How the two languages meet.** Options: run them as fully separate notebooks · bridge (reticulate/rpy2) and call one from the other. _Axis:_ clean separation vs a single integrated document. _Forcing:_ Is the goal to _compare_ the dialects (keep them apart) or to _use each for its strength_ in one flow (bridge them)?
3. **Handling missing data.** Options: drop rows · impute · carry NULLs through. _Axis:_ simplicity vs not silently distorting the result. _Forcing:_ How does each language _represent_ missing (NaN vs NA), and does your aggregation silently skip or silently corrupt when it hits one?
4. **Where the transformation happens.** Options: pull raw data, transform in the notebook · push the heavy aggregation into SQL first. _Axis:_ notebook flexibility vs not loading a million rows into memory. _Forcing:_ This reaches back to M2 — at what data size does "load it all and group in pandas" stop being viable?
5. **Reproducibility discipline.** Options: trust cell order · enforce restart-and-run-all + pinned data. _Axis:_ speed of exploration vs a result you can stand behind. _Forcing:_ If a hiring manager ran your notebook fresh, would it produce your number?

### Research targets

- _What is split-apply-combine?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can describe the pattern and point to it in both `groupby` and `group_by`. _Seed:_ the data-science book's grouping chapter.
- _How does missing data differ between pandas (NaN) and R (NA)?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can say what each does inside a `mean()`.
- _Why do notebooks break reproducibility, and what's the fix?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain hidden state and name restart-and-run-all as the test.
- _(Optional) reticulate or rpy2 — how the bridge works._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say how a data frame crosses the language boundary.

### Out of scope (fence)

Statistical modeling and machine learning, interactive dashboards (that's more M12/M14 territory), big-data tooling (Spark, Dask — note, park), notebook-to-production pipelines. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Restart-and-run-all on both notebooks. **The number:** do both produce the identical headline figure from a cold start? (A mismatch means hidden state somewhere — find it.)

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ how can a notebook show a result that cannot be regenerated — where does the hidden state live? **Check:** you can name two ways a notebook lies.
2. **Research.** The targets, timeboxed. _Objective:_ split-apply-combine explained once, in neither dialect's syntax.
3. **Commit (DSA).** Dataset, the one question, and where each transform happens. **Check:** the question written as a sentence with a measurable answer.
4. **Build — Python.** Load, group, aggregate, chart, prose. **Check:** Restart & Run All passes; the figure supports the claim.
5. **Build — R.** The same question in tidyverse idiom — not a transliteration. **Check:** Restart & Run All passes.
6. **Walk into the trap.** Compare the two headline figures. _Ask:_ if they differ, is it missing-data handling (NaN vs NA), grouping, or something you assumed? **Check:** they match, and you know why they didn't at first.
7. **Measure & debrief.** The number is the match itself. _Ask:_ which dialect fought you, where, and what does each optimize for? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** cold-run both notebooks; show the matching number and the supporting chart.

- **Interview (90s):** "I ran the same analysis in pandas and tidyverse to stay fluent in both — the interesting part was seeing where R's verb pipeline reads more clearly than method chaining, and enforcing restart-and-run-all so the result is actually reproducible."
- **Design review:** a peer's notebook has a great result but won't cold-run. Explain the hidden-state problem without condescension and the one habit that fixes it.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been doing the same analysis in Python and R side by side — good way to feel what each language is actually _for_."
- **Reach-back (data-lifecycle thread):** if the dataset were a million rows, which step would you push back into SQL (per M2), and why?

---

## M9 — The Reproducible Result · research-data analytics (Penn track) · (M)

> **Story.** As a research-track analyst (the Penn lane), I want a pipeline from raw data to a defensible figure that anyone can regenerate with one command, so that my results carry provenance, not vibes. **MVP.** Ingest, documented cleaning, one grouped comparison, a chart, and a one-command reproduction path. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - A colleague on a clean machine goes from raw data to the final figure with a single documented command.
> - Every transformation and dropped record is documented with a why.
> - The headline claim survives a sanity check you designed to break it.

**Anchors:** the Python/data-science books; the honest-verb discipline (this module touches a domain you don't claim expertise in — the discipline matters here more than anywhere).

### Ground Floor — From notebook to pipeline

*Read this before the Briefing. It assumes M8's Ground Floor, so pandas and a notebook are familiar, but not that you've turned analysis into something that runs with one command. You'll restructure a toy analysis into a pipeline first. This tells you what each piece is for and what to look up, never how to write it. Environment pinning, provenance, domain quirks, and sanity checks are the module's.*

#### GF.1 — The picture first

```
data/raw/            ← never edited, ever
   │
   ▼  step 1: ingest     (read, check row counts, write)
data/interim/
   │
   ▼  step 2: clean      (every change logged with a reason)
data/processed/
   │
   ▼  step 3: analyze    (the grouped comparison)
results/
   │
   ▼  step 4: plot
figures/final.png

one command at the top runs all four, in order
```

A notebook is a place to think. A pipeline is a place to *rerun*. M9 needs both, and this Ground Floor builds the second.

#### GF.2 — A script is not a notebook

**Look up:**

- Running a Python file from the command line; `if __name__ == "__main__":`
- Command-line arguments with `argparse`
- Paths with `pathlib`, and why paths relative to the current directory break when someone runs your script from elsewhere
- Python's `logging` module vs `print`

**Do:** take the toy analysis from M8's Ground Floor and move it out of the notebook into a script that reads an input path and writes an output file.

#### GF.3 — Raw stays raw

**Look up:** the convention of an immutable `raw/` folder and derived folders (the "cookiecutter data science" layout is a common reference); and why you write intermediate files instead of passing everything through memory.

**Build:** split your script into steps, each reading the previous step's output file and writing its own.

**Question to settle:** if someone hands you a corrected raw file next month, what's the least work it takes to regenerate the final figure?

#### GF.4 — One command

**Look up:** a `Makefile` as a pipeline runner (targets, dependencies, why it only reruns what changed), or a single orchestrating script; and what `make -n` shows you.

**Build:** one command that runs every step from raw to figure.

#### GF.5 — Counting what you touch

**Look up:** logging row counts before and after each step; `value_counts` for checking categories; and assertions in a data script (`assert`, and what happens when one fails mid-pipeline).

**Build:** make every step log how many rows came in and how many went out.

#### GF.6 — A figure as a file

**Look up:** saving a matplotlib figure to a file; setting its size and resolution; axis labels and units; and why a figure needs a caption saying what data and which step produced it.

#### GF.7 — Run it and observe

Delete everything except `raw/` and run your one command.

**Notice and leave alone:**

1. **Did it regenerate the same figure?** It should. If it didn't, find out why.
2. **Look at your row-count logs.** Somewhere rows probably disappeared. Can you say *why* for every one? M9 requires that you can.
3. **Your environment.** It works on your machine, today, with whatever library versions you have. What happens next year? That's M9's second decision.
4. **Anything in your toy data you didn't understand,** like an odd category or an ambiguous column. You probably just guessed. M9 asks what you should do instead.

#### GF.8 — Hand-off: what's yours in M9

| Ground Floor left this… | M9 asks you to… |
|---|---|
| A toy analysis | A representative biomedical-style dataset and a defensible result |
| Notebook moved wholesale into a script | Decide notebook vs pipeline, or how to have both |
| Whatever library versions you had | Make the environment reproducible next year |
| Row counts logged | Document every dropped or changed record with a why |
| Guesses about odd data | Handle domain quirks honestly, surfaced for an expert |
| A figure that regenerates | A sanity check designed to break your headline claim |

Then read the Briefing below.

#### GF.9 — Check yourself before moving on

- Why must `raw/` never be edited, even to fix an obvious typo?
- What does Make do when you rerun after changing only the plotting step?
- How does a script find its input files regardless of where it's run from?
- Name every step between raw data and your figure, and what each writes.
- For one row your pipeline dropped, can you say why?

### Briefing

Research software engineering is a distinct craft: you support _scientists_ by turning their data and questions into reliable, reproducible analysis and tooling. The Penn role you applied to lives in computational pathology — a platform for annotating cells and tissue in medical images, where the tabular _outputs_ of imaging (measurements, annotations, labels) become datasets that must be analyzed carefully and reproducibly. You don't need to be a pathologist to do this well; you need to handle a domain scientist's data faithfully, make the analysis reproducible, and communicate it clearly. This module builds a representative slice of that work on a public or synthetic biomedical-style dataset. The center of gravity is **reproducible, honest analysis of data you didn't generate and don't fully own**.

An honesty note, because it's the whole point of the honest-verb discipline: this module makes you _fluent in the shape of the work_, not an expert in the domain. In conversation you say "I built a reproducible analysis pipeline over a representative dataset" — never "I have computational pathology experience." The distinction is exactly what keeps you credible in the interview.

### The trap

**The result that can't be traced back to the data.** In research, a number is worthless if you can't say precisely which inputs and which steps produced it — and worse, subtle data issues (a mislabeled category, a unit mismatch, a filtered-out subgroup) can produce a _plausible_ wrong answer that survives right up until someone tries to reproduce or build on it. The trap is confident output over untrustworthy lineage.

### The feature

> A reproducible analysis pipeline over a representative dataset (e.g. a public dataset of image-derived measurements, cell counts, or clinical-style tabular records): ingest the raw data, document and handle its quirks, compute a defensible summary result with at least one grouped comparison, visualize it, and package it so a colleague can regenerate the exact result from the raw input with one command.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- A colleague (or you, on a clean machine) can go from raw data to your final figure with a single documented command.
- Every transformation and every dropped/filtered record is documented with a _why_.
- The headline claim is supported by a chart and survives a sanity check you designed to try to break it.

### Decision map

1. **Notebook vs script pipeline.** Options: a narrative notebook · a parameterized script/pipeline. _Axis:_ exploratory storytelling vs one-command reproducibility. _Forcing:_ Who's the audience — a reader following your reasoning, or a colleague who needs to _rerun_ it on new data? Can you get both?
2. **Environment reproducibility.** Options: "works on my machine" · pinned dependencies (lockfile/container). _Axis:_ speed now vs regenerable next year. _Forcing:_ If someone runs this in six months with newer library versions, does your number survive? What's the cheapest way to guarantee it?
3. **Data provenance.** Options: transform in place · keep raw immutable, derive everything. _Axis:_ convenience vs traceability. _Forcing:_ Can you point at any figure and name every step from raw input to it? What did you _remove_ from the data, and would a reviewer agree with each removal?
4. **How you handle domain quirks you don't fully understand.** Options: guess and proceed · document assumptions explicitly and flag them. _Axis:_ speed vs honesty. _Forcing:_ When a column's meaning is ambiguous, do you silently assume, or surface the assumption for a domain expert to correct? Which behavior gets you hired onto a research team?
5. **Sanity-check design.** Options: trust the pipeline · build a check that would catch a plausible error. _Axis:_ faster vs trustworthy. _Forcing:_ What's a wrong-but-plausible result this analysis could produce, and what check would catch it?

### Research targets

- _What does reproducible research actually require?_ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can name the layers (data, environment, code, one-command run) and why each matters. _Seed:_ search "reproducible research checklist" — read one authoritative source.
- _What is data provenance and why does research live or die on it?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain tracing a result to its inputs.
- _Representative domain framing:_ skim what computational-pathology annotation data looks like as tabular output. — **25 min.** Done when you can describe, honestly and roughly, the _kind_ of data the Penn platform produces — without overclaiming expertise. _(Parking-lot everything deeper; this is orientation, not a rabbit hole.)_
- _(Optional) one-command reproducibility tooling_ (Make, a simple pipeline runner). — **25 min.** Done when you can wire raw→result behind a single target.

### Out of scope (fence)

Actual medical/clinical interpretation, statistical significance testing beyond the basics, image processing itself (you're working with derived tables, not pixels — note, park), ML modeling. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

On a clean checkout, run your one command. **The number:** does it regenerate the exact final figure from raw data, unattended? Reproducibility is binary here — it does or it doesn't.

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ what makes a number defensible in research — what question must you be able to answer about every figure? **Check:** "which inputs, which steps" recited with a straight face.
2. **Research.** The targets; provenance gets your attention. _Objective:_ you can define reproducibility as a property of the pipeline, not the person.
3. **Commit (DSA).** Notebook vs script pipeline, environment pinning, quirk policy. **Check:** written DSA, plus the sanity check you'll later use to attack your own claim.
4. **Build — ingest and clean.** Raw data untouched in `data/raw/`; every drop and transform logged with a why. **Check:** the quirk log is honest and non-empty.
5. **Build — the analysis.** The grouped comparison and its chart. **Check:** the figure regenerates from raw with one command.
6. **Walk into the trap.** Run your pre-designed sanity check against your own result. _Ask:_ what would this look like if I were wrong? **Check:** the claim survives, or the pipeline is fixed and rerun.
7. **Measure & debrief.** Clean-machine (or clean-clone) reproduction, one command, same figure. _Ask:_ which step would a skeptical reviewer poke first? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** clean-machine one-command regeneration; walk the provenance from raw file to final chart.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a reproducible analysis pipeline over a representative research dataset — one command takes raw data to the final figure, every filtering step is documented, and I built a sanity check to catch a plausible wrong answer. I'd be careful to say this is the _shape_ of research-data work, not domain expertise I'm claiming."
- **Design review:** a peer's analysis isn't reproducible and drops rows without explanation. Raise it the way you'd want it raised on a research team — collaboratively, provenance-first.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been practicing research-software-engineering habits — the whole game is that someone else can regenerate your exact result from the raw data."
- **Reach-back (honest-verb thread):** write the one sentence you'd say about this project to a hiring manager. Does it claim exactly what you did and nothing more?

---

## M10 — The Migration That Fights Back · a data migration with real snags · (M)

> **Story.** As the engineer who wants to be trusted with the data, I want to run a migration that real-world mess actively resists — and be able to roll it back — so that I've earned the war story every senior data person has. **MVP.** A backfilled new required column plus a constraint the messy data violates, handled; forward idempotent, rollback working. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - The migration succeeds against the messy dataset; every offending row is fixed, quarantined, or rejected with a logged reason — never silently dropped.
> - Running the forward migration twice does not corrupt or double-apply.
> - The rollback returns a working prior state; the new required column is correctly backfilled.

**Anchors:** _Where the Data Lives_ Part 8 (migrations — the book flags this as the skill interviews probe hardest); your M2 Postgres table as the source.

### Ground Floor — Changing a table that already has data

*Read this before the Briefing. It assumes M2's Ground Floor, so you can create tables and query them, but not that you've changed the shape of a table already full of rows. You'll practice on a clean throwaway table first. This tells you what each piece is for and what to look up, never how to write it. Messy data, backfills, idempotency, and rollback design are the module's.*

#### GF.1 — The picture first

```
schema v1  ──▶  migration 001 (forward)  ──▶  schema v2
           ◀──  migration 001 (rollback) ◀──

A migration = a script that changes SCHEMA (columns, constraints)
              and sometimes DATA (filling a new column)
              — applied to a database that may be in use while it runs
```

The rows are already there. Every statement you run has to make sense for every one of them.

#### GF.2 — Schema changes, from zero

**Look up:**

- `ALTER TABLE`: adding, renaming, and dropping columns; changing a column's type
- Constraints: `NOT NULL`, `UNIQUE`, `CHECK`, foreign keys, and adding them to an existing table
- Default values, and what adding a column *with* a default does to existing rows
- *Where the Data Lives* Part 8, on migrations

**Do:** on a clean throwaway table with a few tidy rows, add a column, rename one, add a `CHECK` constraint, and drop a column. After each, look at the table again.

#### GF.3 — Transactions

**Look up:**

- `BEGIN`, `COMMIT`, `ROLLBACK`, and what "atomic" means
- That Postgres can run most schema changes *inside* a transaction, and why that's unusual among databases
- Locks: what an `ALTER TABLE` locks, and what happens to other sessions trying to read or write meanwhile

**Do:** open two `psql` sessions. In one, `BEGIN` and alter the table without committing. In the other, try to read and write the table. Then `ROLLBACK` the first and check the table is untouched.

#### GF.4 — Migration files and tools

**Look up:**

- Numbered migration files as plain SQL, and why order matters
- What migration tools add (Alembic for Python, Flyway for Java): a table that records which migrations have run, and an up/down pair per migration
- Where that bookkeeping table lives, and what happens if someone edits an already-applied migration

**Build:** two migration files for your throwaway table, applied with whichever tool you pick or by hand, plus their reversals.

#### GF.5 — Updating existing rows

**Look up:** `UPDATE ... SET ... WHERE`; `UPDATE` using values from another column or another table; and `RETURNING` for seeing what changed.

**Do:** add an empty column to your clean table and fill it from existing columns.

#### GF.6 — Run it and observe

Now, on your clean throwaway table only, deliberately insert one row with a null where you're about to require a value, and one duplicate where you're about to require uniqueness.

**Notice and leave alone:**

1. **Add `NOT NULL` and `UNIQUE`.** Read the errors exactly. What did Postgres refuse, and did any part of your migration get applied? This is M10's trap at toy scale.
2. **Run a migration twice.** What happens the second time? M10 requires it to be safe.
3. **Drop a column, then try to reverse it.** What did your "down" migration actually give back? That gap is M10's fifth decision.

Don't clean up the bad rows. Deciding what to do with them is the module.

#### GF.7 — Hand-off: what's yours in M10

| Ground Floor left this… | M10 asks you to… |
|---|---|
| A clean table with two planted bad rows | A realistically messy dataset, every bad row handled |
| Direct `ALTER`s | Decide in-place vs expand-contract |
| Constraint errors read, not resolved | Fix, quarantine, or reject offending rows, with logged reasons |
| One `UPDATE` over a few rows | Choose a backfill strategy for a large, live table |
| Migrations that may fail on a second run | Make the forward migration idempotent |
| A "down" migration that didn't bring data back | Design a rollback that returns a working state |

Then read the Briefing below.

#### GF.8 — Check yourself before moving on

- What does adding `NOT NULL` to a column with one null in it do, and does the table change at all?
- Why does running a schema change inside a transaction matter if it fails halfway?
- What does a migration tool's bookkeeping table record, and why should you never edit an applied migration?
- What did your second session experience while the first held an uncommitted `ALTER`?
- After dropping a column, what can a rollback restore, and what can't it?

### Briefing

A data migration changes the shape of data that already exists — and often, data that is _live_, being read and written while you change it. This is categorically harder than designing a schema fresh, because you inherit every mess the old schema allowed: nulls where you now need values, duplicates where you now need uniqueness, three spellings of the same category, encodings from a decade ago. Experienced engineers respect migrations the way sailors respect weather. Doing one with deliberate snags — the kind that only bite on real data — is how you earn that respect before a production incident teaches it to you. The center of gravity is **changing data safely while it's in use, and being able to undo it**.

### The trap

**The migration that passes on a clean dev copy and detonates on production data.** Your three test rows are tidy; the ten-year-old table has a row with a null in the column you're about to make `NOT NULL`, a duplicate in the column you're about to make unique, and a value that violates the foreign key you're adding. The migration ran green in staging and takes down production at 2am. Its evil twin: **the irreversible migration** — you dropped the old column, the new logic has a bug, and there is no way back.

### The feature

> Take your M2 `incidents` table (or a copy), seed it with _deliberately messy_ real-world-style data, then perform a non-trivial migration end to end: a schema change that requires backfilling a new required column from existing data, plus a change that the messy data _violates_ — and handle it. Produce both a forward migration and a working rollback, and make the forward migration _idempotent_ (safe to run twice).

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- The migration succeeds against the _messy_ dataset, not just a clean one — every offending row is handled (fixed, quarantined, or explicitly rejected with a logged reason), not silently dropped.
- Running the forward migration a second time does not corrupt or double-apply.
- The rollback returns the database to a working prior state.
- A new required column is correctly backfilled from existing data.

### Decision map

1. **In-place vs expand-contract (parallel-change) migration.** Options: alter the column directly · add new alongside old, backfill, switch reads, then drop old. _Axis:_ simplicity vs zero-downtime safety and reversibility. _Forcing:_ If the app is _running_ during the migration, which approach lets old and new code coexist? Which one can you undo after you've dropped the old column? (You can't — that's the point of expand-contract.)
2. **What to do with rows that violate the new constraint.** Options: fail the whole migration · fix them programmatically · quarantine them for review · reject with logged reasons. _Axis:_ strictness vs progress vs data loss. _Forcing:_ Is silently dropping a bad row ever acceptable? Who needs to _know_ a row was bad?
3. **Backfill strategy for a large table.** Options: one big `UPDATE` · batched updates. _Axis:_ simplicity vs locking the table and blocking the live app. _Forcing:_ What does a single `UPDATE` over ten million rows do to everyone else trying to use the table meanwhile?
4. **Idempotency.** Options: assume it runs once · make it safe to re-run. _Axis:_ less code vs surviving a mid-migration crash. _Forcing:_ If the migration dies halfway and you rerun it, does it resume cleanly or double-apply? Which failure would you rather debug at 2am?
5. **Reversibility.** Options: forward-only · paired rollback. _Axis:_ speed vs a safety net. _Forcing:_ When the new code has a bug in production, what's your move if there's no rollback? What data would a rollback _lose_ if writes happened in between?

### Research targets

- _What is the expand-contract (parallel-change) migration pattern?_ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can narrate the five phases and say which ones can coexist with running code. _Seed:_ Where the Data Lives, Part 8.
- _Why can a single big `UPDATE` take down a live app?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain locking/blocking and why batching helps.
- _What makes a migration idempotent?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can describe a check that makes re-running safe.
- _Backfilling a `NOT NULL` column on an existing table — the safe sequence._ — **25 min.** Done when you can order the steps so the constraint is never violated mid-flight.

### Out of scope (fence)

Cross-database migrations (Postgres→something else), migration frameworks' internals, online schema-change tools (gh-ost, pt-osc — note, park), data warehousing ETL at scale, blue-green deployment mechanics. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Run the forward migration against the messy dataset; then run it _again_. **The number:** did it handle every offending row (count them — fixed/quarantined/rejected, zero silently lost) and survive the second run unharmed? Then run the rollback and confirm a working prior state.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>I can't make the column NOT NULL — existing rows have nulls</summary>

**Orient:** you can't add the constraint until the data satisfies it, so the constraint is the _last_ step, not the first.

**Approach:** the safe order is add-the-column-nullable → backfill the nulls with a real value (computed from other columns, or a documented default) → _verify_ no nulls remain → _then_ add the `NOT NULL` constraint. If you can't compute a sensible value for some rows, that's a decision (default? quarantine? reject?), not an error to paper over.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ why does a migration that passed on dev detonate on production data — what specifically differs? **Check:** three concrete examples of "messy" named before you seed them.
