## M16 — Text Without the DOM Tax · a pretext rendering playground · (M)

> **Story.** As a design-engineer chasing the floor under text, I want a pretext-powered rendering playground that does one thing the DOM can't do gracefully, so that I understand what measurement costs and why reflow is a tax I can refuse. **MVP.** One hero effect (flow-around-a-shape or shrink-wrapped headlines), measured with pretext, rendered to canvas/SVG, verified against the browser's own line breaks. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - Layout is computed without ever calling `offsetHeight`/`getBoundingClientRect` to measure.
> - On resize, only the cheap layout step reruns — the expensive prepare step does not.
> - pretext's line breaks match the browser's for the same text and font; emoji and non-Latin input don't corrupt layout.

**Anchors:** _Pixels With Intent_ (its rendering-pipeline and reflow material is the exact backdrop for this module; its Part 7 paper-build of a layered card stack is the spirit of "derive the layout yourself"). Library: **pretext** — `github.com/chenglou/pretext`.

### Ground Floor — What the browser does to draw text

*Read this before the Briefing. It assumes you can write JavaScript but haven't studied the rendering pipeline or drawn text yourself. You'll break a paragraph into lines by hand on a canvas first, so pretext has something to replace. This tells you what each piece is for and what to look up, never how to write it. The hero effect, the font sync, and prepare vs layout are the module's.*

#### GF.1 — The picture first

```
The browser, every time the page changes:
  DOM + CSS ─▶ STYLE ─▶ LAYOUT (reflow) ─▶ PAINT ─▶ COMPOSITE ─▶ pixels
                          ▲
           reading offsetHeight here can force this whole step to run NOW

Doing it yourself on a canvas:
  text ─▶ split into pieces ─▶ MEASURE each piece's width ─▶ decide line breaks ─▶ draw each line
                                       ▲
                          the canvas can measure without a reflow
```

pretext does the second row for you, carefully. This Ground Floor has you do a rough version first.

#### GF.2 — The rendering pipeline

**Look up:** *Pixels With Intent*, its rendering-pipeline and reflow material. Then make sure you can explain:

- Style, layout, paint, and composite, and what triggers each
- Forced synchronous layout (layout thrashing): reading a geometry property right after changing the DOM
- The DevTools Performance panel, and how to spot a layout event in a recording

**Do:** in a scratch page, change an element's text and read its `offsetHeight` in a loop. Record it in the Performance panel and find the layout work.

#### GF.3 — Canvas 2D, the first hour

**Look up:**

- Getting a 2D context from a `<canvas>`
- `fillText` and the `font` property, and how the font string's syntax mirrors CSS shorthand
- `measureText`, and what's in the object it returns
- Device pixel ratio, and why canvas text looks blurry on a retina screen until you scale it

**Do:** draw one line of text on a canvas, sharp on your screen.

#### GF.4 — Line-breaking by hand

**Build:** a function that takes a paragraph and a maximum width, splits it into words, measures each with `measureText`, and draws as many words per line as fit. It only has to be rough.

**Look up:** greedy line breaking, and that better algorithms exist (Knuth–Plass, used by TeX). Park the latter.

**Question to settle:** how many times did you call `measureText` for one paragraph? What would that be on every resize?

#### GF.5 — Text isn't characters

**Look up:**

- Code points, UTF-16 code units, and graphemes, and why `"👍🏽".length` isn't 1
- `Intl.Segmenter` for splitting by grapheme and by word
- Why some scripts don't put spaces between words, and what that does to "split on spaces"
- Right-to-left text, as a concept only

**Do:** print the `.length` and the grapheme count for a few strings: an emoji with a skin tone, a family emoji, an accented letter, an Arabic word.

#### GF.6 — pretext, by its own README only

**Do:** install pretext and read its README's API section. Don't read anyone's demo source. That's the module's rule too.

**Look up:** what `prepare` takes and returns, what `layout` takes and returns, and which of the two the README says is expensive.

#### GF.7 — Run it and observe

**Notice and leave alone:**

1. **Put the same paragraph in a normal DOM element** next to your canvas version, at the same width. Do the line breaks match?
2. **Change the CSS font on the DOM paragraph without changing the canvas font string,** or the other way round. What happens to the match? That's M16's trap.
3. **Feed your hand-built breaker an emoji-heavy sentence and an Arabic one.** What broke?
4. **Resize repeatedly.** What does your hand-built version redo every time? pretext's split between `prepare` and `layout` is about exactly that.

#### GF.8 — Hand-off: what's yours in M16

| Ground Floor left this… | M16 asks you to… |
|---|---|
| A rough greedy breaker | Use pretext, picking the API tier your effect needs |
| A plain rectangle of text | Build one hero effect the DOM can't do gracefully |
| Two font strings that can drift apart | Keep measure and render on one source of truth |
| Everything recomputed on resize | Prepare once; rerun only the cheap layout |
| Emoji and Arabic breaking things | Handle graphemes correctly |
| Canvas, by default | Choose a render target, and handle accessibility |

Then read the Briefing below.

#### GF.9 — Check yourself before moving on

- Name the pipeline stages, and which one reading `offsetHeight` can force.
- What does `measureText` return, and why doesn't calling it trigger a reflow?
- Why is `"👍🏽".length` not 1, and what does `Intl.Segmenter` give you instead?
- Did your canvas breaks match the DOM's? Where didn't they, and why?
- According to the README, which pretext step is expensive?

### Briefing

Here's a thing most web developers never question: when you ask the browser how tall a paragraph is (`offsetHeight`, `getBoundingClientRect`), it may have to **reflow** — recompute layout for a chunk of the page — which is one of the most expensive things the browser does. **pretext** is a pure-JavaScript library that sidesteps this entirely: it measures and lays out multiline text _without touching the DOM_, using the browser's own canvas text-measurement as ground truth, then does line-breaking as pure arithmetic over cached widths. That unlocks things the DOM makes painful — text that flows around a shape, headlines that shrink-wrap to their tightest width, masonry sized without guessing, rendering to canvas/SVG/WebGL. The center of gravity is a mind-bender worth internalizing: **text is not characters — it's graphemes, measured segments, and widths — and layout is arithmetic you can do yourself.**

This module is about _using_ the library to make something expressive. Read pretext's README API to know the tools; but resist reading other people's demo source until you've drafted your _own_ layout approach — the point is to build the instinct, not to transcribe a solution.

### The trap

**Measurement drift and the hot-path mistake.** pretext is only accurate if the font, line-height, and letter-spacing you _measure_ with exactly match what you _render_ with — the moment your canvas font string diverges from your actual CSS, every line break is subtly wrong and you'll chase a ghost. And the performance trap the library exists to avoid: re-running the expensive `prepare()` step on every resize instead of only the cheap `layout()` — which throws away the entire point. Underneath both: treating text as a string of characters, so an emoji or an Arabic run (which are multiple code units but one _grapheme_) breaks your measurement.

### The feature

> A text-rendering playground that does at least one thing the DOM can't do gracefully, built on pretext. Pick a hero effect:
> 
> - **Flow text around a floated shape** — lines beside the shape are narrower, lines below it are full width (pretext's variable-width per-line layout), rendered to canvas.
> - **Shrink-wrap / balanced headlines** — find the tightest width that still fits the text on the intended number of lines (pretext's line-walking to search widths).
> 
> Whichever you pick: measure with pretext (no `offsetHeight`), render the result yourself (canvas or SVG), and verify your line breaks match what the browser would actually do.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- Text lays out correctly _without_ your code ever calling `offsetHeight`/`getBoundingClientRect` for measurement.
- On resize, only the cheap layout step reruns — the expensive prepare step does not.
- The line breaks pretext computes match the browser's own rendering of the same text at the same font (accuracy check).
- Non-Latin text or emoji in the input doesn't corrupt the layout (graphemes handled).

### Decision map

Write your DSA before building.

1. **Which pretext use case.** Options: measure-height-only (`prepare`/`layout`) · full manual per-line layout (`prepareWithSegments` + line-walking). _Axis:_ simplicity vs the power to place each line yourself. _Forcing:_ Does your hero effect just need a _height_ (masonry, overflow-check), or does it need to place _each line at a different width_ (flow-around, canvas render)? That answer picks your API tier.
2. **Render target.** Options: canvas · SVG · positioned DOM. _Axis:_ pixel control and WebGL-readiness vs accessibility and selectability. _Forcing:_ Canvas text isn't selectable or screen-reader-visible — is that acceptable for your effect, and if not, what's your fallback for real users?
3. **Keeping measurement and rendering in sync.** Options: hardcode the font in two places and hope · derive one font descriptor used for _both_ measure and render. _Axis:_ convenience vs correctness. _Forcing:_ Where could your measured font and rendered font diverge, and what's the one source of truth that prevents it? (This _is_ the trap — design against it.)
4. **prepare vs layout on the hot path.** Options: re-prepare on every change · prepare once, layout on resize. _Axis:_ less thinking vs the entire performance premise. _Forcing:_ What work does `prepare()` do that you must _not_ repeat when only the width changed? What's cheap enough to run every frame?
5. **Shrink-wrap search (if you pick that effect).** Options: linear scan of widths · binary search on width using line-count. _Axis:_ simple vs efficient. _Forcing:_ You want the narrowest width that keeps the text at N lines — how do you search that space, and why is line-count a monotonic signal you can binary-search on? (Reaches back to the rep circuit.)

### Research targets

- _What is reflow, and why is `offsetHeight` sometimes expensive?_ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain layout thrashing and why measuring text without the DOM is worth a library. _Seed:_ Pixels With Intent (rendering pipeline); one "layout thrashing" article.
- _pretext's two use cases — the API shape._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say what `prepare`/`layout` give you vs `prepareWithSegments` + the line-walkers, tab closed. _Seed:_ the pretext README (read _this_, not demos).
- _Graphemes vs code points vs code units — why text isn't characters._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain why an emoji is one grapheme but several code units, and why measurement must respect that. _Seed:_ search "JavaScript Intl.Segmenter grapheme."
- _Canvas `measureText` as the measurement ground truth._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say why measuring on a canvas doesn't trigger layout the way the DOM does.

### Out of scope (fence)

Building your own text-measurement engine (that's pretext's job — note the fascination, park it), full internationalization/bidi rendering correctness, font loading and FOUT/FOIT, rich-text editing, WebGL text rendering (note, park). → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Render the same string at the same font two ways — via pretext, and via a real DOM element — and compare where the lines break. **The number:** do pretext's line breaks and computed height match the browser's actual rendering? Then open the Performance panel during a resize: **how many forced reflows does your code trigger?** (The answer you want is zero — that's the whole point.)

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>My pretext line breaks don't match the browser's</summary>

**Orient:** pretext is accurate _by construction_ if it measures with the same font as you render — a mismatch means your measure-font and render-font differ somewhere, even subtly.

**Approach:** check that the exact font string you pass to `prepare()` (family, size, weight, style) is identical to the CSS actually painting the text, that your `lineHeight` argument matches your CSS `line-height`, and that `letterSpacing` matches. The README calls out specific gotchas — `system-ui` is unsafe for accuracy on some platforms; use a named font. Also confirm you're not comparing against a DOM element with different padding or box-sizing affecting the available width.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ what exactly does the browser do when you read `offsetHeight`, and why is that sometimes a tax? **Check:** reflow explained in three sentences.
2. **Research.** The four targets. _Objective:_ pretext's prepare-vs-layout split explained — what's expensive once vs cheap per-frame.
3. **Commit (DSA).** Which hero effect, render target, and the sync strategy between measured and rendered font. **Check:** written; the font-string discipline is explicit.
4. **Build — measurement floor.** Text measured and drawn at one width, no DOM reads. **Check:** your rendered line breaks match the browser's for the same text and font, side by side.
5. **Build — the hero.** Flow-around-shape or shrink-wrap search. **Check:** the effect works on real prose, not just one test string.
6. **Walk into the trap.** Resize continuously. _Ask:_ what reruns per frame — and is prepare in the hot path? **Check:** only layout reruns on resize; throw emoji and non-Latin text at it without corruption.
7. **Measure & debrief.** Time prepare vs layout; show the ratio. _Ask:_ where else does "measure once, lay out many" apply (M17's loop, M28's scroll)? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** the hero effect running; the line-break match against the DOM; the Performance panel showing zero forced reflows on resize.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a text-flow effect with pretext, which measures multiline text without touching the DOM — so no reflow. The insight I'd share is that `offsetHeight` can force expensive layout, and pretext does the measurement on canvas as pure arithmetic instead. The trap is keeping the measure-font and render-font perfectly in sync, or the line breaks drift."
- **Design review:** a peer measures text with `getBoundingClientRect` in a resize handler. Explain layout thrashing and why moving measurement off the DOM is the fix.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been playing with pretext — it lays out text without the DOM, so you can flow text around shapes and shrink-wrap headlines, things CSS makes weirdly hard. The mind-bender is that 'text' is really graphemes and measured widths."
- **Reach-back (floor thread):** M15's layout was _expensive_ precisely because of what pretext avoids. And M18's C memory is the deepest version of "every convenience is a receipt." State the floor-thread thesis in one sentence using all three.

---

## M17 — Sixty Frames, Held · an award-tier 3D interaction · (L)

> **Story.** As a design-engineer, I want one award-tier 3D interaction that holds sixty frames and cleans up after itself, so that craft means measured performance and a graceful floor, not a machine-melting demo. **MVP.** One polished interactive 3D element: input-responsive, smooth, steady frame rate, disposed resources, reduced-motion/no-GPU fallback. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - Steady ~60fps during use, measured, with nothing heavy allocated inside the render loop.
> - Repeated mount/unmount does not grow memory (resources disposed).
> - With reduced motion or no WebGL, the page still works — a sensible fallback, not a void.

**Anchors:** _Waiting, All the Way Up_ (the `requestAnimationFrame` heartbeat is the same event-loop idea); _Pixels With Intent_ (the GPU/compositing material). Library: Three.js (with React Three Fiber if you choose — you have prior reps there).

### Ground Floor — A scene, a camera, a loop

*Read this before the Briefing. It assumes you know JavaScript and maybe a little React Three Fiber, but not that you can explain the render loop or measure a frame. You'll build a throwaway spinning shape you can push with the pointer first. This tells you what each piece is for and what to look up, never how to write it. The per-frame discipline, the animation driver, budgets, fallbacks, and disposal are the module's.*

#### GF.1 — The picture first

```
Set up once:   renderer (owns the <canvas> and the WebGL context)
               scene  ──contains──▶ mesh = geometry (shape) + material (surface)
                                    light(s)
               camera (where you look from)

Every frame (requestAnimationFrame, ~60×/s → 16.7 ms budget):
   read input ─▶ update positions/rotations ─▶ renderer.render(scene, camera)
                                                       │
                                     CPU sends draw calls ─▶ GPU draws pixels
```

#### GF.2 — The GPU and WebGL, as concepts

**Look up:**

- What a GPU is good at, and why the CPU has to send it work in batches
- What WebGL is: the browser's API to the GPU; vertices and fragments, as ideas only
- What a draw call is, and why counting them matters
- What Three.js adds on top of raw WebGL

#### GF.3 — The scene graph

**Look up:** Three.js's own "Creating a scene" guide; then `Scene`, `PerspectiveCamera`, `WebGLRenderer`, `Mesh`, a basic geometry, two kinds of material (one that needs lights and one that doesn't), and one or two light types.

**Build:** a single shape, lit, on screen, filling the window.

#### GF.4 — The loop

**Look up:**

- `requestAnimationFrame`: when the browser calls it, and what it does when the tab is hidden
- *Waiting, All the Way Up*, on how this fits the event loop
- Three.js's clock or timer utilities, and what "delta time" means

**Build:** make the shape rotate continuously.

#### GF.5 — Input

**Look up:** pointer events (`pointermove`, `pointerdown`); converting pointer coordinates to the -1 to 1 range Three.js uses; scroll position as an input; and handling a window resize (camera aspect and renderer size).

**Build:** make the shape tilt toward the pointer.

#### GF.6 — Measuring a frame

**Look up:**

- The DevTools Performance panel and its frames track
- The FPS meter or rendering stats overlay in DevTools
- `stats.js`, the small FPS panel Three.js examples use
- `renderer.info`, and what it reports about draw calls, geometries, and textures

**Do:** show live FPS and draw calls while your shape moves.

#### GF.7 — Run it and observe

**Notice and leave alone:**

1. **Add fifty copies of your shape.** Watch FPS and the draw-call count. That's M17's budget decision.
2. **Throttle your CPU** in the Performance panel (4× or 6× slowdown). Does your rotation slow down, or stay the same speed and get choppier? That's the animation-driver decision.
3. **If you're using React, mount and unmount the scene twenty times.** Watch `renderer.info` and the memory panel. Does anything climb? That's the resource-lifecycle decision.
4. **Turn on reduced motion in your OS settings,** or disable WebGL in your browser. What does your page show?
5. **Look at what your loop creates each frame.** Is anything constructed with `new` inside it? Don't change it; just know.

#### GF.8 — Hand-off: what's yours in M17

| Ground Floor left this… | M17 asks you to… |
|---|---|
| A tilting shape | One award-tier interaction, referenced in Figma first |
| Raw Three.js or R3F, whichever you grabbed | Choose, with the render loop in mind |
| A loop that may allocate | Decide what runs per frame and what runs once |
| Whatever drove your rotation | Choose an animation driver that survives frame drops |
| FPS and draw calls watched | Set and hold a performance budget |
| Whatever reduced-motion showed | Degrade gracefully |
| Memory you watched, maybe climbing | Dispose resources on teardown |

Then read the Briefing below.

#### GF.9 — Check yourself before moving on

- Name every object in your scene and what each is responsible for.
- What is a draw call, and how many did your fifty shapes cost?
- When does `requestAnimationFrame` call your function, and what happens when the tab is hidden?
- What did CPU throttling do to your rotation, and what does that tell you about how you drove it?
- What did your page show with reduced motion on?

### Briefing

The interactive 3D pieces that win awards look like magic and are, underneath, a tight loop and a strict performance budget. Three.js is the library that wraps WebGL — the browser's door to the GPU — into a workable **scene graph**: a `scene` holds `mesh`es (shapes) with `material`s (surfaces) lit by `light`s, viewed through a `camera`, drawn by a `renderer` — sixty times a second. Building one award-tier _interaction_ (not a whole site — a hero element, a scroll-driven effect, a responsive 3D object) teaches the two things that separate a portfolio piece from a laptop-melting embarrassment. The center of gravity is **the render loop and the performance floor**: what runs every frame, and what the GPU will and won't do in 16.7 milliseconds.

### The trap

**Jank, and the machine-melting portfolio piece.** Do the wrong work inside the per-frame loop — allocate objects, recompute geometry, touch the DOM — and the frame rate collapses into stutter. Forget to _dispose_ geometries and materials when you're done and memory climbs until the tab dies. And the cruel one for a _portfolio_: it runs at 60fps on your machine and turns a reviewer's older laptop into a space heater, because you never set a budget or a fallback. The tell: it's beautiful and your fan is loud.

### The feature

> One polished, interactive 3D element at genuine craft level: it responds to input (pointer, scroll, or both), animates smoothly, holds a steady frame rate, cleans up after itself, and degrades gracefully where 3D isn't available or isn't wanted (reduced-motion, no-GPU). Study the _effect_ you admire from award sites — but draft your own approach before reading anyone's source.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- The interaction holds a steady ~60fps on your hardware during use (measurable, not asserted).
- Mounting and unmounting the scene repeatedly does _not_ grow memory without bound (resources are disposed).
- With `prefers-reduced-motion` set, or on a context without WebGL, the page still works — a sensible static fallback, not a broken void.
- Nothing heavy is allocated inside the render loop.

### Decision map

Write your DSA before building. (Design the intended effect in Figma / as a reference board first.)

1. **Raw Three.js vs React Three Fiber.** Options: imperative Three.js · declarative R3F. _Axis:_ seeing the machinery vs React-native composition and lifecycle. _Forcing:_ You have R3F reps — is the goal this month to go _deeper_ into the underlying Three.js you'd otherwise abstract away? Which choice teaches you more about the render loop itself?
2. **What runs per-frame vs once.** Options: rebuild/allocate in the loop · create once, mutate cheaply per frame. _Axis:_ naive vs the entire performance premise. _Forcing:_ Which operations (geometry creation, material setup, object allocation) must happen _outside_ the loop? What's the only kind of work cheap enough to do every frame? (This _is_ the trap.)
3. **Animation driver.** Options: time-based (delta since last frame) · frame-count-based · scroll-position-based. _Axis:_ frame-rate independence vs simplicity vs scroll-coupling. _Forcing:_ If the frame rate dips, does your animation _slow down_ (frame-based) or stay correct (time-based)? Why does multiplying by delta-time matter?
4. **Performance budget.** Options: no budget · explicit limits on polygon count, draw calls, texture size. _Axis:_ freedom vs running on a normal laptop. _Forcing:_ What's your draw-call count, and what happens to it as you add objects? What texture resolution is enough vs vanity? (Reaches back to M18 — the GPU, like memory, is finite.)
5. **Graceful degradation.** Options: assume WebGL and motion-tolerance · detect and fall back. _Axis:_ simplicity vs real users. _Forcing:_ What does a reviewer on a low-end device or with reduced-motion enabled see? Is "nothing" or "jank" an acceptable answer for a _portfolio_?
6. **Resource lifecycle.** Options: create and forget · dispose geometries/materials/textures on teardown. _Axis:_ less code vs no memory leak. _Forcing:_ What holds GPU memory after your component unmounts, and what has to explicitly release it?

### Research targets

- _The Three.js mental model: scene, camera, renderer, mesh, material, light._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can name each part's job and how they combine into one rendered frame. _Seed:_ the Three.js "Creating a scene" doc; three.js-journey (skim the free intro).
- _The render loop and `requestAnimationFrame` — and delta-time._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain frame-rate-independent animation. _Seed:_ Waiting, All the Way Up (the rAF/event-loop section).
- _Draw calls and the GPU performance budget._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say what a draw call is and why fewer is faster.
- _Disposing Three.js resources — the leak._ — **25 min.** Done when you can name what must be `.dispose()`d and when.

### Out of scope (fence)

Writing custom shaders/GLSL (genuinely deep — note, park it hard), physics engines, loading complex external 3D models, post-processing pipelines, a full multi-section site. Keep it to _one_ interaction. → parking lot. **And: reading award-site source is a post-commit reward** — draft and build your own approach first, then diff yours against theirs to learn.

### Measure ritual

Turn on a frame-rate meter (browser FPS counter or Three.js stats) and interact hard. **The number:** does it hold ~60fps (≤16.7ms/frame), and what's your draw-call count? Then mount/unmount the scene many times watching the memory graph — **does memory return to baseline, or climb?** (Climbing = a disposal leak.)

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>My animation stutters / frame rate tanks under interaction</summary>

**Orient:** something expensive is running inside the per-frame loop that shouldn't be — the loop should _mutate_ cheap values, never _build_ things.

**Approach:** audit what executes every frame. Creating geometries or materials, allocating vectors/objects, or reading layout from the DOM inside the loop are the usual culprits — move all of them to run _once_ at setup, and per-frame only update positions/rotations/uniforms on already-created objects. If it's still janky, check your draw-call and polygon counts against a budget; the GPU may simply be asked for too much per frame.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ what work is forbidden inside a per-frame loop, and what happens to a scene you unmount but never dispose? **Check:** both answered with mechanisms, not vibes.
2. **Research.** The four targets. _Objective:_ the scene/camera/renderer/mesh model drawn from memory; delta-time explained.
3. **Commit (DSA).** All six decisions, including the performance budget as a number and the degradation plan. **Check:** written before any npm install of a 3D library.
4. **Build — the stage.** Scene, lighting, the element, static. **Check:** renders at your budget's draw-call count.
5. **Build — the life.** Input-driven animation on delta time. **Check:** the FPS meter holds ~60 during interaction; the frame callback allocates nothing.
6. **Build — the exits.** Reduced-motion fallback, no-WebGL fallback, disposal on unmount. **Check:** both fallbacks render something sensible.
7. **Walk into the trap, then debrief.** Mount/unmount in a loop while watching memory. **Check:** flat, not stairs. _Ask:_ which resource would have leaked, and who owns its lifetime? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** the interaction at a held frame rate (show the meter); the memory graph returning to baseline across mount/unmount; the reduced-motion fallback.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built an award-tier 3D interaction in Three.js and held 60fps by keeping the per-frame loop to cheap mutations — no allocation, no geometry rebuilds. Two things I'm proud of: time-based animation so it stays correct if frames drop, and disposing GPU resources on teardown so it doesn't leak. And it degrades for reduced-motion and no-WebGL, because a portfolio piece can't melt a reviewer's laptop."
- **Design review:** a peer's scene is gorgeous but janky and leaks. Diagnose per-frame allocation and missing disposal as a colleague, not a critic.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been building an award-site-style 3D interaction — the real craft turned out to be the performance budget, keeping it at 60fps without cooking the GPU."
- **Reach-back (floor thread):** the render loop is the same `requestAnimationFrame` heartbeat behind smooth UI everywhere, and the GPU is as finite as M18's memory. What does "the machine is finite" force on you here that it also forced in C?

---

# PART VI — The Machine

_Two modules on the floor everything else stands on. A memory allocator in C, where you personally own every byte (M18), and a small game that makes you live in that world while also running a loop that never stops (M19). This is the deepest point of the floor thread — "every convenience is a receipt," and here you pay in full, on purpose. You already have a dynamic-array lesson compiling from your C curriculum; this builds on that footing._

---

## M18 — Every Byte Is Yours · a memory allocator in C · (M)

> **Story.** As a builder who wants to own the floor, I want an arena allocator in C that survives a memory checker on every path, so that stack-vs-heap, ownership, and the famous four bugs are things I've lived, not read. **MVP.** An arena allocator plus a driver that exercises it, checker-clean including failure paths. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - Correct across several input paths and sizes; allocation failure handled, not ignored.
> - The checker reports zero leaks and zero errors on every path.
> - No path reads uninitialized memory or writes out of bounds.

**Anchors:** _The Machine Under Everything_ (this is the module that book exists to make legible); your `CURRICULUM_C_RUST.md` and the dynamic-array lesson you already have compiling (`dyn_array`).

### Ground Floor — Pointers, the heap, and a checker

*Read this before the Briefing. It assumes you've written some C (the `dyn_array` lesson counts) but not that you can predict where a value lives or prove a program clean. You'll write small throwaway programs that make each region of memory, and each of the four famous bugs, visible. This tells you what each piece is for and what to look up, never how to write it. The arena, its ownership model, growth, failure policy, and alignment are the module's.*

#### GF.1 — The picture first

```
A running C program's memory (simplified, addresses grow ↑):

  ┌──────────────┐  high
  │    STACK     │  local variables; one frame per function call;
  │      ↓       │  gone when the function returns
  │              │
  │      ↑       │
  │    HEAP      │  malloc'd blocks; live until YOU free them
  ├──────────────┤
  │ globals/data │
  │    code      │  low
  └──────────────┘

A pointer is just a number: the address of something in one of these regions.
```

#### GF.2 — Compiling so the compiler helps

**Look up:** `gcc` or `clang` flags `-Wall -Wextra -g`; what each does; and `-fsanitize=address` (AddressSanitizer). Make warnings-as-errors your default for this module.

**Do:** compile a scratch file that uses an uninitialized variable, once without the flags and once with them. Read the difference.

#### GF.3 — Pointers, from zero

**Look up:** *The Machine Under Everything*, its pointer and memory chapters. Then confirm:

- `&` (address-of) and `*` (dereference), and pointer types
- Pointer arithmetic: why `p + 1` moves by `sizeof(*p)` bytes, not one
- `sizeof` on a type, on an array, and on a pointer, and why the last two differ
- Arrays decaying to pointers when passed to a function
- `NULL`, and what dereferencing it does

**Do:** print the addresses of a local variable, a global, and a `malloc`'d block. Which region is each in?

#### GF.4 — The heap by hand

**Look up:** `malloc`, `calloc`, `realloc`, and `free`: what each takes, what each returns, and what each guarantees about the contents of the memory.

**Do:** revisit your `dyn_array`. For every allocation in it, write a comment naming what frees it and when. Notice whether any answer was hard to give.

#### GF.5 — The checker

**Look up:** Valgrind's memcheck: running a program under it, reading "definitely lost" vs "still reachable," and reading an invalid-read report back to a line of source. Then AddressSanitizer as the faster alternative.

**Do:** run your `dyn_array` test under both. Get it clean before moving on.

#### GF.6 — Meet the four bugs on purpose

**Build:** four tiny throwaway programs, one per bug: a leak, a use-after-free, a double free, and a write one byte past a block's end. Each should look like it works when you run it normally.

**Then:** run each under Valgrind and under AddressSanitizer. For each, write one line: what the checker said, and on which line it pointed.

**Notice and leave alone:**

1. **Which bugs "worked" without the checker?** That's M18's trap: passing a casual test run.
2. **Call `realloc` on a block while another pointer points into it.** Print both addresses before and after. Did the block move? That's M18's third decision.
3. **Ask `malloc` for something absurd,** like most of your address space. What came back? That's the failure-policy decision.
4. **Allocate a `char`, then a `double`, then a `char`** and print their addresses. Notice any gaps? That's alignment, the fifth decision.

#### GF.7 — Hand-off: what's yours in M18

| Ground Floor left this… | M18 asks you to… |
|---|---|
| Individual `malloc`/`free` calls | Decide whether an arena or a pool fits, and build one |
| Ownership comments on `dyn_array` | Design an ownership model with exactly one owner per allocation |
| A `realloc` that may have moved | Decide fixed capacity vs growth, and handle moved memory |
| A `NULL` you saw and didn't handle | Choose an allocation-failure policy |
| Gaps between addresses noticed | Respect alignment when carving memory |
| Four toy bugs, each caught | Zero leaks and zero errors on every path, including failure paths |

Then read the Briefing below.

#### GF.8 — Check yourself before moving on

- Where does a local variable live, where does a `malloc`'d block live, and when does each die?
- Why does `p + 1` on an `int *` move by more than one byte?
- What's the difference between Valgrind's "definitely lost" and "still reachable"?
- Name the four bugs. For each, did it crash on a normal run?
- What did `realloc` do to the address in your experiment?

### Briefing

In JavaScript and Python, memory is invisible: you make objects, they vanish when unneeded, and a garbage collector you never see does the bookkeeping. C removes the collector and hands _you_ the ledger. Memory comes in two regions: the **stack** (automatic, scoped, freed when a function returns — fast and safe but rigid) and the **heap** (manual, via `malloc`/`free` — flexible but entirely your responsibility). Building a small **allocator** — a program that manages heap memory itself — is the most direct way to _see_ the model that everything higher up is hiding. The center of gravity is exactly this: **the stack/heap distinction, pointers, lifetime, and ownership** — who is responsible for freeing what, and when.

### The trap

The famous four, and this feature can hit all of them: the **memory leak** (you `malloc` and never `free` — invisible until it exhausts memory under load), **use-after-free** (you `free` then read the freed pointer — sometimes works, sometimes corrupts, always a bug), **double-free** (freeing the same block twice — undefined behavior), and the **buffer overflow** (writing past a block's end — the classic security hole). Every one of them can _pass a casual test run_ and detonate later. The tell: "it works," but you haven't run a memory checker.

### The feature

> A program that manages heap memory manually and survives scrutiny. The recommended target is a small **arena (pool) allocator**: request one big block from the system up front, then hand out and track smaller chunks from it, with a way to reset or release the whole arena at once — used by a tiny driver program that exercises it. (If you'd rather, a generic dynamic structure with explicit ownership works too — but the goal is the same: non-trivial manual memory management you can _prove_ correct.)

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- The program runs correctly across several input paths and sizes.
- A memory checker reports **zero leaks and zero errors** on every path — including the failure paths.
- Allocation failure (the system says no) is handled, not ignored.
- No path reads uninitialized memory or writes out of bounds.

### Decision map

Write your DSA before building.

1. **Arena/pool vs individual `malloc`/`free`.** Options: many small `malloc`/`free` calls · one big arena carved up yourself. _Axis:_ simplicity and fine-grained freeing vs speed and bulk cleanup. _Forcing:_ If you allocate ten thousand small things with the same lifetime, is freeing them one-by-one the right model, or freeing the whole arena at once? What does the arena give up (individual frees) to gain speed?
2. **Ownership model.** Options: caller owns and frees · the structure owns and frees · reference counting. _Axis:_ clarity vs flexibility vs complexity. _Forcing:_ For every allocation, can you name _exactly one_ owner responsible for freeing it? Where ownership is ambiguous, that's where leaks and double-frees breed — can you make it unambiguous?
3. **Handling `realloc` and moved memory.** Options: fixed capacity · grow with `realloc`. _Axis:_ simplicity vs dynamic sizing. _Forcing:_ When `realloc` moves a block to a new address, what happens to every pointer that was pointing into the old location? (This is a dangling-pointer factory — how do you avoid it?)
4. **Allocation-failure policy.** Options: assume success · check every allocation and handle NULL. _Axis:_ less code vs not crashing on out-of-memory. _Forcing:_ `malloc` can return NULL — what does your program do then, and what happens if you don't check and dereference it?
5. **Alignment and sizing (arena path).** Options: hand out raw byte offsets · respect alignment requirements. _Axis:_ naive vs correct on real hardware. _Forcing:_ Why might returning an unaligned pointer for certain types be a problem, and how does that shape how you carve the arena?

### Research targets

- _Stack vs heap — what actually differs._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain, tab closed, why stack allocation is automatic and fast and heap allocation is manual, and when you're forced onto the heap. _Seed:_ The Machine Under Everything (the memory sections).
- _`malloc`/`free`/`realloc` — the contract with the system._ — **25 min.** Done when you can state what each returns, what ownership it transfers, and what `realloc` may do to your pointer.
- _The four memory bugs — how each happens and how a checker finds it._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can describe leak, use-after-free, double-free, and overflow, and what valgrind/ASan reports for each. _Seed:_ the valgrind quick-start.
- _Pointer basics: dereferencing, dangling, and NULL._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain what a dangling pointer is and how `free` creates one.

### Out of scope (fence)

Writing a full general-purpose allocator to compete with the system one (fascinating — park it), custom garbage collectors, thread-safe allocation, memory-mapped files, the deep internals of how the OS gives you pages. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Run under **valgrind** (or AddressSanitizer) on every input path, including the ones designed to fail. **The number:** leaks and errors — and the target here genuinely _is_ zero, "All heap blocks were freed — no leaks are possible." Then deliberately introduce one bug (skip a `free`, or read after free) and confirm the checker _catches_ it — so you trust the tool.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>realloc broke my program — pointers into the block stopped working</summary>

**Orient:** `realloc` is allowed to _move_ your data to a new address and free the old one; any pointer you were holding into the old block is now dangling.

**Approach:** after a `realloc`, the _only_ valid pointer to the block is the one `realloc` returned — reassign it, and treat every previously-stored pointer into that block as invalid. The design fix is to not hold long-lived raw pointers _into_ a growable block; hold an index or an offset, and compute the pointer from the current base when you need it. (This is exactly why your dynamic array stores a base pointer and length rather than scattered interior pointers.)

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ for each of the famous four bugs, what does the failure look like from the outside? **Check:** four bugs, four observable symptoms, no notes.
2. **Research.** The four targets. _Objective:_ the malloc/free contract recited, including what the system never checks for you.
3. **Commit (DSA).** Arena semantics, ownership model, failure policy, alignment. **Check:** written; ownership stated as "who frees what, when."
4. **Build — the arena.** Init, alloc, reset, destroy. **Check:** the driver exercises normal paths clean under the checker.
5. **Build — the edges.** Exhaustion, zero-size, alignment-hostile sizes. **Check:** allocation failure is handled, not ignored; still checker-clean.
6. **Walk into the trap.** Deliberately write one of the four bugs in a scratch branch of the driver. _Ask:_ what exactly does the checker report, and would you have caught it without it? **Check:** you can read the tool's accusation fluently. Revert.
7. **Measure & debrief.** Zero leaks, zero errors, every path — the number is 0/0. _Ask:_ what does the arena make impossible that per-object `free` invites? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** the valgrind-clean run across all paths; then the introduced-bug run where the checker catches it.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built an arena allocator in C to actually see the memory model the higher-level languages hide. The core discipline is single-owner-per-allocation, which is what prevents leaks and double-frees, and the subtle bug was `realloc` moving a block and dangling every interior pointer. I proved the whole thing valgrind-clean, including failure paths."
- **Design review:** a peer uses scattered `malloc`/`free` where an arena fits. Discuss when bulk lifetime management beats individual frees — and when it doesn't.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been writing a memory allocator in C — the humbling part is that 'it works' means nothing until valgrind agrees; four whole classes of bug hide behind a passing test run."
- **Reach-back (floor thread):** every GC'd `new` in JavaScript, every Python object, is doing _this_ underneath. Having built it by hand, what convenience are those languages selling you, and what's the receipt (name it for M16 and M17 too)?
