## M24 — Data That Can Hurt · a health-tech feature done responsibly · (M)

> **Story.** As an engineer who may touch health tech, I want a small vital-signs feature done the responsible way — FHIR-compatible shape, validated units, controlled access, clean logs — so that I know where ordinary CRUD instincts become dangerous and where my build's boundary sits. **MVP.** A personal-metric tracker modeling Patient + Observation, validating plausibility on the way in. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - Records conform to a FHIR-compatible structure (or you can show the mapping).
> - Implausible or wrong-unit values are caught, not stored; sensitive fields never appear in logs.
> - Access is controlled, and you can name what would still be missing for production.

**Anchors:** the security/privacy mindset from M4; the honest-data discipline from M9 (you're not claiming clinical expertise); _Where the Data Lives_ on data modeling.

### Ground Floor — What makes health data different

*Read this before the Briefing. It assumes you can build a validated API from earlier Ground Floors, but not that you've read a health-data standard or thought about a log file as a liability. You'll read FHIR and hand-write a few resources first. This tells you what each piece is for and what to look up, never how to write it. Schema choice, validation, privacy, auditing, and the honest boundary are the module's.*

#### GF.1 — The picture first

```
An ordinary app:     { "bp": 120 }                              ← what? whose? when? in what?

A FHIR Observation:  resourceType: "Observation"
                     status:  final
                     code:    which measurement, from a shared code system (LOINC)
                     subject: which Patient (a reference, not a name)
                     effectiveDateTime: when it was measured
                     value:   number + UNIT, from a shared unit system (UCUM)

Everywhere the data goes:  API ─▶ database ─▶ LOGS ─▶ error messages ─▶ backups
                           every one is a place it can leak
```

#### GF.2 — FHIR, from zero

**Look up:**

- What HL7 FHIR is, and the interoperability problem it solves
- What a FHIR *resource* is, then read two in the official spec: `Patient` and `Observation`
- Codes: LOINC for what was measured, and why a free-text label isn't enough
- UCUM for units, and the difference between `mg` and `g` in a field that only holds a number
- How one resource references another

**Do:** find the official FHIR example for a blood-pressure Observation and read every field. Then hand-write a weight Observation for an invented patient.

#### GF.3 — The legal and ethical frame, as vocabulary

**Look up:** what HIPAA covers in the US and what it doesn't (personal apps often fall outside it); what PHI and PII mean; and data minimization as a principle.

You're not becoming a compliance expert. You're learning the words so you can say what you *didn't* build. That's M24's fifth decision.

#### GF.4 — Plausibility, as a concept

**Look up:** typical and physiologically possible ranges for one vital sign, from a reputable medical source; and the difference between "unusual" and "impossible."

**Question to settle:** a weight of 70 arrives. Is it plausible? What would you need to know first?

#### GF.5 — Where data leaks

**Look up:** what your framework logs by default (request bodies? query strings? exception messages?); log levels; and structured logging with redaction.

**Do:** in an API you built earlier, send a request with a fake "sensitive" field and then read every log line the server wrote. Was the field anywhere in them?

#### GF.6 — Run it and observe

**Notice and leave alone:**

1. **Your hand-written Observation.** Could someone else's system read it without asking you what anything means? Compare it with the `{ "bp": 120 }` version. That's M24's first decision.
2. **Send a weight of 7000** to an earlier API that stores numbers. Did anything stop it? That's the validation decision.
3. **Your log check.** Wherever your fake sensitive field showed up, it would show up for real data too. That's the privacy decision.
4. **Ask who viewed a record yesterday.** Your earlier APIs can't say.

#### GF.7 — Hand-off: what's yours in M24

| Ground Floor left this… | M24 asks you to… |
|---|---|
| FHIR resources read and hand-written | Decide FHIR-compatible vs your own schema |
| Ranges looked up | Validate units and plausibility on the way in |
| A sensitive field found in logs | Keep sensitive fields out of logs, and control access |
| No record of access | Decide on auditability |
| Compliance vocabulary | Document honestly what's missing for production |

Then read the Briefing below.

#### GF.8 — Check yourself before moving on

- What's a FHIR resource? Name the fields an Observation needs to mean something.
- Why does an Observation carry a LOINC code instead of just a label?
- What's UCUM, and what error does it prevent?
- Where did your fake sensitive field end up in your logs?
- Name one thing HIPAA doesn't cover that people often assume it does.

### Briefing

Health tech is a domain where getting the engineering _right_ is a safety issue, not just a quality one — and it has its own vocabulary of constraints: sensitive personal data with legal protections, interoperability standards so systems can safely exchange records, and a correctness bar where a unit error or a mislabeled field can harm a person. **FHIR** is the modern standard for representing and exchanging health data — a shared shape so a record from one system means the same thing in another. Building a small feature that handles health data _responsibly_ — standards-aware, privacy-aware, correctness-first — teaches what makes this domain different. The center of gravity is **treating health data as sensitive and standardized, not as ordinary rows.**

An honesty note (the M9 discipline again): this makes you fluent in the _engineering posture_ health tech demands. You'd say "I built a FHIR-shaped health-data feature with privacy and validation built in" — not "I have clinical or regulatory expertise."

### The trap

**Treating health data like any other data.** The ordinary-CRUD instinct — store it plainly, log freely, validate loosely, invent your own schema — is exactly wrong here. Sensitive fields logged in plaintext, no access control, no audit trail, home-grown formats no other system can read, and unchecked units (was that dose in mg or g?) are the failure modes, and in this domain they range from a compliance violation to a genuine harm. The tell: you handled a blood-pressure reading the same way you'd handle a to-do item.

### The feature

> A small feature that ingests, stores, and displays health data the responsible way: model the records in a **FHIR-compatible shape** (e.g. an Observation like a vital sign, or a simple Patient + Observation pair), validate units and plausible ranges on the way in, control access to the data, and keep sensitive fields out of plaintext logs. A personal-metric tracker (weight, blood pressure, glucose) is a fine, concrete vehicle.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- Records conform to a FHIR-compatible structure (validates against the standard, or you can show the mapping).
- Units are explicit and validated; an implausible or wrong-unit value is caught, not stored blindly.
- Access to the data is controlled (ties to M4); sensitive fields do not appear in logs.
- You can explain what would need to change to be actually production/regulation-ready (showing you know the boundary of what you built).

### Decision map

Write your DSA before building.

1. **FHIR-compatible vs roll-your-own schema.** Options: model on FHIR resources · invent your own shape. _Axis:_ interoperability and rigor vs freedom. _Forcing:_ If another system had to read your data tomorrow, which choice makes that possible? What does a standard buy you beyond "looks professional"?
2. **Units and validation.** Options: store a bare number · store value + unit with range/plausibility checks. _Axis:_ simplicity vs safety. _Forcing:_ Is "120" a blood pressure, a weight, or a glucose reading — and in what unit? What catches a value that's physically impossible or off by a factor of ten?
3. **Privacy posture.** Options: store and log freely · minimize, access-control, keep sensitive data out of logs. _Axis:_ convenience vs not leaking health data. _Forcing:_ Which fields are sensitive, where do they currently appear (database, logs, error messages), and what's the minimum exposure you can operate with? (The M4 mindset, raised to a legal bar.)
4. **Auditability.** Options: none · record who accessed/changed what. _Axis:_ simplicity vs the accountability health data demands. _Forcing:_ If someone asked "who viewed this record and when," could your system answer? (Same append-only instinct as M13's shop-floor history.)
5. **Knowing your boundary.** Options: claim it's production-ready · document what's _missing_ for real compliance. _Axis:_ overclaiming vs credibility. _Forcing:_ What did you deliberately _not_ build (real auth-grade security, encryption at rest, full regulatory compliance), and can you name it honestly?

### Research targets

- _What is FHIR and what problem does it solve?_ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain "a shared shape for health data so systems interoperate," and name a resource type like Observation. _Seed:_ the HL7 FHIR overview (read the intro, park the depth).
- _Why health data has special handling requirements._ — **25 min.** Done when you can name sensitivity, interoperability, and correctness as the three pillars, without needing to be a compliance lawyer.
- _Units and safe validation of clinical-style values._ — **25 min.** Done when you can describe validating value + unit + plausible range.
- _Privacy-by-design basics (minimization, access control, not logging sensitive data)._ — **25 min.** Done when you can list what _not_ to do with sensitive fields.

### Out of scope (fence)

Actual regulatory compliance (HIPAA/GDPR implementation), real encryption-at-rest engineering, clinical decision logic, EHR integration, medical accuracy claims. You're building the _posture_, and naming the boundary is part of the deliverable. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Validate a record against the FHIR shape and run your unit/range checks with good and bad values. **The number:** does a valid record conform and an invalid/implausible one get rejected — and can you grep your logs to confirm no sensitive field leaked? (Conforms, rejects, and clean logs is the bar.)

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ what makes health data different in kind, not just degree — and what does the ordinary-CRUD instinct get wrong first? **Check:** three concrete harms named (logged vitals, no access control, homemade schema drift).
2. **Research.** The four targets. _Objective:_ FHIR's problem-solved stated; an Observation's core fields sketched from the spec.
3. **Commit (DSA).** FHIR-compatible vs roll-your-own, unit strategy, privacy posture, audit plan. **Check:** written; "what I am not building" is a section, not a footnote.
4. **Build — the shape.** Patient + Observation modeled, stored. **Check:** a record validates against the standard, or your mapping table says exactly where it diverges.
5. **Build — the gate.** Plausibility and unit validation on the way in. **Check:** a 400-kg weight and a mmHg-vs-kPa confusion are both caught, not stored.
6. **Build — the walls.** Access control (M4's machinery) and clean logs. **Check:** an unauthorized request is refused; grep the logs — no vitals anywhere.
7. **Measure & debrief.** The audit trail answers who-saw-what. _Ask:_ name three things still missing before this could touch real patients — knowing the boundary is the deliverable. Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** a conforming record; a wrong-unit value getting caught; logs with no sensitive data; and your spoken list of what's _not_ production-ready.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a health-metric feature modeled on FHIR so it's interoperable, with unit-and-range validation because a wrong-unit value is a safety issue, access control, and no sensitive data in logs. I'd be clear this is the responsible _engineering posture_ — I'm not claiming clinical or regulatory expertise, and I can tell you exactly what a real deployment would still need."
- **Design review:** a peer stored vitals as bare numbers in a custom schema with verbose logging. Raise interoperability, unit safety, and privacy — as a colleague, correctness-first.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been learning what makes health-tech engineering different — modeling data in FHIR and treating it as genuinely sensitive, because in this domain a unit bug isn't a typo, it's a hazard."
- **Reach-back (honest-verb + security threads):** this leans on M4's access control and M9's honest-data posture. Write the one sentence you'd say to a health-tech hiring manager that claims exactly what you built and nothing more.

---

## M25 — Descend and Rearrange · a filesystem tool in Python · (S)

> **Story.** As an engineer who automates his own machine, I want a filesystem tool that descends, reports, and reorganizes by rule — dry-run by default — so that bulk operations against real folders are something I can trust myself with. **MVP.** Size reports, rule-based conditional moves/renames, dry-run default, explicit execute. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - Dry-run reports exactly the planned actions and changes nothing; execute does exactly what dry-run promised.
> - A symlink cycle does not cause an infinite loop.
> - Permission errors and collisions are handled with reasons — no crashes, no silent overwrites.

**Anchors:** the Python books; the tree thread (closing it with M11 and M20); the safety instincts from M10's migration.

### Ground Floor — Walking your own disk

*Read this before the Briefing. It assumes you can write Python and know M11's tree walk, but not that you've walked a real filesystem or moved files from code. You'll build a throwaway read-only tool first, on a sandbox folder you create. This tells you what each piece is for and what to look up, never how to write it. The walking API, dry-run design, symlinks, rules, and the collision policy are the module's.*

#### GF.1 — The picture first

```
sandbox/                       a directory is a node; its entries are children
├── photos/
│   ├── a.jpg     (2.1 MB)     a file is a leaf with metadata: size, mtime, type
│   └── trip/
│       └── b.jpg (4.8 MB)
├── notes.txt     (3 KB)
└── shortcut ──▶ ../         ← a SYMLINK: a pointer that can lead back UP the tree

READ (safe):    walk ─▶ stat each entry ─▶ report
WRITE (danger): walk ─▶ decide ─▶ rename / move    ← no undo
```

#### GF.2 — A sandbox first, always

**Do:** before writing any code, make a throwaway folder tree with a script: nested folders, files of different sizes and extensions, a couple of same-named files in different folders, and one symlink pointing back up to an ancestor. Never point this module's code at a real folder until the module says so.

**Look up:** creating symlinks (`ln -s` in your WSL shell); `ls -la`, and how it shows a link; and `du -sh`.

#### GF.3 — Paths and metadata

**Look up:**

- `pathlib.Path`: joining, `name`, `suffix`, `parent`, resolving to an absolute path
- `stat()`: size, modification time, and what `st_mode` tells you about file type
- `is_file`, `is_dir`, `is_symlink`, and which of them follow links
- `os.walk`, `os.scandir`, and `Path.rglob`, as three ways to walk. Choosing is M25's first decision.

#### GF.4 — A read-only reporter

**Build:** a command-line tool that walks a folder and prints each file's path and size, then the total size per top-level folder, like `du`. It only reads. Run it on the sandbox.

**Look up:** `argparse` for taking the folder as an argument; and human-readable sizes (KB, MB).

#### GF.5 — Moving files, as a concept

**Look up:** `shutil.move` vs `Path.rename`, and what happens to each when the destination already exists, on Linux and on Windows; moving across filesystems; and permission errors (`PermissionError`).

Don't move anything yet, not even in the sandbox.

#### GF.6 — Run it and observe

**Notice and leave alone:**

1. **Did your reporter visit the symlink?** Did it follow it? If it did, how far did it go before you stopped it? That's M25's trap.
2. **Compare your total with `du -sh`.** If they differ, find out why. Links and hidden files are the usual suspects.
3. **Make one file unreadable** (`chmod 000`) and rerun. What did your tool do?
4. **Imagine moving both same-named files into one folder.** What would `shutil.move` do to the second one? That's the collision decision.
5. **Imagine running a move rule with a typo in it on your real Downloads folder.** That's why the module insists on a dry run.

#### GF.7 — Hand-off: what's yours in M25

| Ground Floor left this… | M25 asks you to… |
|---|---|
| Whichever walk you used | Choose `os.walk`, `pathlib`, or `scandir`, and defend it |
| A read-only reporter | Add rule-based moves and renames, dry-run by default |
| A symlink that may have looped | Decide how to handle symlinks and detect cycles |
| No rules at all | Choose how rules are expressed |
| An unreadable file, and a name clash imagined | Handle errors and collisions with reasons, never silent overwrites |

Then read the Briefing below.

#### GF.8 — Check yourself before moving on

- What's a symlink, and which `pathlib` checks follow one?
- What does `stat()` give you, and which field is the size?
- What does `shutil.move` do when the destination name already exists?
- How did your tool behave on the unreadable file?
- Why build a sandbox before writing the walk?

### Briefing

Your disk is a tree: folders contain files and folders, nested arbitrarily deep. A tool that _walks_ that tree — measuring sizes like `du`, inspecting types and names — and then _acts_ on it (reorganize, move, rename by rule) is a genuinely useful thing to own and a clean capstone for the tree thread. It's also a controlled way to practice something dangerous: **bulk operations on real files**, where one bad rule scrambles a directory with no undo. The center of gravity is **recursive traversal plus _safe_ mutation** — the same descent as the BOM and the parser, now with the added weight that mistakes are destructive.

### The trap

**Destructive bulk operations with no dry-run and no undo** — and the symlink cycle. Rename or move hundreds of files by a rule with a bug, and you've made a mess you can't cleanly reverse; there is no `Ctrl-Z` for the filesystem. And the traversal itself has teeth: a symbolic link can point _back up_ the tree, so a naive recursive walk that follows links can loop **forever** — the exact cycle problem from the BOM (M11), wearing a different hat. The tell: you ran it for real on the first try, or your walk hung on a linked directory.

### The feature

> A command-line tool that descends a directory tree and can reorganize it by rules — safely. It reports sizes (à la `du`), inspects file types and names, and can conditionally move/rename/reorganize files based on rules you define (by extension, size, name pattern, date). Crucially: it **defaults to a dry-run** that shows exactly what _would_ happen without touching anything, executes only on explicit confirmation, logs what it did, and handles the dangerous edges (symlink cycles, permission errors, name collisions).

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- Dry-run mode reports the correct planned actions (sizes, matches, intended moves/renames) and changes _nothing_.
- Executing does _exactly_ what the dry-run promised — no surprises.
- A symlink that creates a cycle does not cause an infinite loop.
- Permission errors and name collisions are handled gracefully (skipped-with-reason or resolved), not crashes or silent overwrites.

### Decision map

Write your DSA before building.

1. **`os.walk` vs `pathlib` vs `os.scandir`.** Options: `os.walk` (classic recursive walk) · `pathlib` (object-oriented paths) · `scandir` (efficient, metadata-rich). _Axis:_ familiarity vs ergonomics vs performance. _Forcing:_ Do you need file _metadata_ (size, type) cheaply during the walk? Which avoids extra stat calls? Which reads most clearly?
2. **Dry-run vs execute (the safety core).** Options: execute directly · dry-run by default, execute on a flag. _Axis:_ speed vs not destroying data. _Forcing:_ This _is_ the trap — what's the cost of running a buggy rule for real on a thousand files, and what makes dry-run-first non-negotiable here?
3. **Symlink handling.** Options: follow links · don't follow · follow but detect cycles. _Axis:_ completeness vs not looping forever. _Forcing:_ A link pointing to an ancestor directory — what does your walk do? How do you detect you've been somewhere before? (This is M11's cycle detection, exactly.)
4. **Rule definition.** Options: hardcoded · CLI flags · a config file. _Axis:_ simplicity vs flexibility and reuse. _Forcing:_ How does a user express "move all `.jpg` over 5MB older than a year into `archive/`" — and how complex should the rule language get before it's a rabbit hole?
5. **Collision and error policy.** Options: overwrite/crash · skip-with-reason, resolve (rename), or prompt. _Axis:_ simple vs safe. _Forcing:_ Moving a file to a name that already exists — overwrite (data loss!), skip, or auto-rename? What happens on a permission-denied file mid-walk? (The M10 "handle the bad rows, don't silently drop" instinct.)

### Research targets

- _Recursive directory traversal in Python (`os.walk`/`scandir`/`pathlib`)._ — **25 min.** Done when you can describe walking a nested tree and getting each file's size and type.
- _Symlinks and why following them can loop._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain the cycle and how detecting visited paths (by real/canonical path) prevents it. (Cross-reference your M11 notes.)
- _Safe file operations in Python (`shutil`, atomicity, collisions)._ — **25 min.** Done when you can name the safe way to move/rename and what happens on a name clash.
- _Building a small CLI (argparse) with a dry-run flag._ — **25 min.** Done when you can wire a `--dry-run` default and an `--execute` opt-in.

### Out of scope (fence)

A full sync tool (rsync territory), deduplication by content hash (note — park; fun), a GUI, watching the filesystem for changes, cloud storage, undo/versioning (real, but scope it out — dry-run is your safety instead). → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Build a messy test tree (nested folders, mixed types, a symlink cycle, a name collision), run in dry-run. **The number:** does the dry-run report the correct sizes and planned actions and touch nothing — and does executing produce _exactly_ the dry-run's plan? Confirm the symlink cycle doesn't hang and collisions are handled, not silently overwritten.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>My walk hangs / revisits folders when there's a symlink</summary>

**Orient:** a symlink can point back to a directory you've already entered, so an unguarded recursive walk re-enters it endlessly — this is the BOM cycle from M11, on disk.

**Approach:** either don't follow symlinks during the walk (often the right default for a reorganizer), or, if you must follow them, track the _canonical real paths_ you've already visited and skip any you've seen before. Resolving each directory to its real path (following the link to where it actually points) and keeping that set is what breaks the cycle — exactly the "visited set" idea from your parser and BOM work.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ what has no Ctrl-Z, and what two properties make a bulk tool trustworthy anyway? **Check:** dry-run-by-default and exact-promise-keeping named as the safety core.
2. **Research.** The four targets. _Objective:_ the symlink cycle explained — how following links loops, and what breaks it.
3. **Commit (DSA).** Traversal API, rule format, collision policy, symlink stance. **Check:** written; "what execute may never do without dry-run showing it first" stated as an invariant.
4. **Build — the playground.** A disposable sandbox tree with nasty cases: deep nesting, collisions, a permission trap, a symlink cycle. **Check:** the sandbox contains every case your plan fears.
5. **Build — the survey.** Descend and report sizes/types à la `du`. **Check:** numbers match `du` within reason; the cycle doesn't hang it.
6. **Build — the rules.** Dry-run planning of moves/renames. **Check:** the plan is exactly right against the sandbox, and nothing changed.
7. **Walk into the trap, then debrief.** Execute. **Check:** reality matches the dry-run promise file-for-file; collisions and permission errors are handled with reasons. _Ask:_ what rule would you never let this tool run against your real home directory, and why? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** the dry-run plan on the messy tree; execution matching it exactly; the symlink cycle handled and a collision resolved.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a filesystem tool that walks nested directories and reorganizes them by rule — and the whole design centers on _safety_, because there's no undo for the filesystem. It's dry-run by default and executes only what the dry-run promised. The gotcha was symlink cycles causing infinite loops — the same cycle-detection I used in a recursive BOM engine, just on disk."
- **Design review:** a peer wrote a bulk-rename that executes immediately and overwrites on collision. Walk through the data-loss risks and the dry-run-first, collision-safe design.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I made a `du`-plus-reorganizer in Python — the interesting part wasn't the walking, it was making destructive bulk operations _safe_ with a dry-run, and not looping forever on a symlink."
- **Reach-back (tree thread — closing it for good):** the recursive BOM (M11), the search-query AST (M20), and this filesystem walk are the _same traversal_ three times, and two of them needed cycle detection. State, in one sentence, the single skeleton under all three — and you've earned the tree thread.

---

# Epilogue — When are you done?

Not when twenty-five folders exist. You're done with a module when you can stand in the **three rooms** and hold your own: give the ninety-second interview answer with a decision and a number, defend one choice against a named alternative like a peer, and drop the thirty-second meetup hook. You're done with the **Gauntlet** when you can do that across this whole surface — when a job description's buzzwords read as _things you've built and can talk about_ rather than things you'd need to look up.

Come back to the premise you started with. Readiness was never a feeling to wait for; it's the inventory you just built, and the fluency you built alongside it. You said you make your own luck by getting ready _now_ — so the work _was_ the signal, all along. The parking lot holds everything you deferred; the threads tie the twenty-five into one story you can tell; the rep circuit kept your pattern-recognition sharp underneath. Cherry-pick against the market, run the modules that match the postings in front of you, and let the inventory meet the opportunity.

Now go build the first one. Two-minute rubber-duck debrief when you finish it: _what did I decide, and why._

# The Gauntlet — Volume II

## Twenty-one more · modules M26–M46 · self-guided

---

## How to read this

This is a continuation of the main volume (M1–M25), not a restart. **Everything in Volume I's preamble still governs** — the no-implementation-code rule (contracts, schemas, type signatures, and enums are specs and are allowed; the build is always yours), the anti-rabbit-hole protocol (timeboxes, done-whens, the parking lot, the rabbit-hole tells, the fences), the _measure ritual_ (one tool, one number, framed as a question), and the _three rooms_ (interview, design review, meetup) that are the real deliverable.

Two things are different in this volume:

1. **Every module opens with a Scrum story** — a user story, an MVP, and acceptance criteria, stated before anything else, so you know the target and the finish line the moment you land on a module.
2. **The coach is built in.** Volume I leaned on an external `/challenge-phase-guide` runtime. This volume doesn't need it — the coaching is written into the document as a self-run loop with explicit gates (next section). You refer to a module, run yourself through it, and do your own spot-checks. No live conversation required; make your own way to completeness.

The numbers continue from Volume I, so **reach-back questions span both books** — a module here can shake hands with M5's RxJS or M18's C memory as easily as with its neighbor. Use it à la carte: when a job description names one of these, jump straight to it.

---

## The coach, built in — how to run a module solo

This replaces the live runtime. Run every module through this loop; the **gates** are the "don't advance until…" checks the coach used to enforce. You enforce them on yourself now.

1. **Read the story block first.** Know the MVP and the acceptance criteria before the briefing. **Gate 0:** can you restate the MVP in one sentence? If not, reread.
2. **Work the Ground Floor, then read the Briefing and The Trap.** Say the trap back in your own words. **Gate 1:** can you name the trap without looking?
3. **Work the Decision Map — commit before you compare.** For each decision, write your **DSA** (your choice + your reasons + one alternative you rejected and why) in a `decisions.md` _before_ you research or build. This is the load-bearing habit; the code just confirms it. **Gate 2:** is your DSA written down?
4. **Run the Research Targets under their timeboxes.** The clock governs the question, not the reading list — hunting your own sources is legitimate research inside the box. Exit each on its done-when (a capability you can _say_). Park anything off-fence in `parking-lot.md`, one line, and move on. **Gate 3:** can you answer each done-when out loud, tab closed?
5. **Build to the MVP — and no further.** The acceptance criteria are the ceiling for this pass; the fence holds everything else. Depth comes from deriving _within_ the fence, not wandering wider.
6. **Run the Measure Ritual.** One tool, one number. **Gate 4:** do you have the number, _and_ can you say what it means?
7. **Prove it — the three rooms.** Say all three out loud: the 90-second interview answer, the design-review exchange, the 30-second meetup hook. **Gate 5:** did you actually speak them, not just read them? (Saying it is the skill.)
8. **Rubber-duck debrief.** Two minutes, out loud, no notes: _what did I decide today, and why._ Then answer the module's reach-back question.

**On hints.** The hint ladders are sealed in `<details>` blocks, three tiers: orient → approach → near-spoiler. You are your own hint-gate: open tier 1 only when genuinely stuck, tier 3 only after two real attempts. Sealed answer keys (named per module) are **post-commit reading** — after your own attempt, never before.

**On your spot-checks.** When you come back to verify a completed module, the eight gates above are the checklist. If a gate wouldn't pass, that module isn't done — and which gate fails tells you exactly what's missing.

---

## The module shape

Same nine-part spine as Volume I, with the Scrum block promoted to the top:

- **Story / MVP / Acceptance criteria** (the block up top) → **Anchors** → **Ground Floor** (zero to a working throwaway of the plumbing, no code, ending in a hand-off table) → **Briefing** (zero-start, climbing the jargon ladder) → **The trap** (named, unresolved) → **The build** (scope and contract only — schemas and type signatures allowed, no code) → **Decision map** (4–6 named decisions, write your DSA before building) → **Research targets** (question · timebox · done-when · 0–2 optional seeds) → **Out-of-scope fence** → **Measure ritual** → **Hints ladder** (only where a problem is genuinely hard) → **Prove it — the three rooms** (ending in a reach-back).

---

## The threads

Volume I's five threads keep running, and two new ones join. Watch for the reach-back questions that test them.

- **The contract thread** (from M1) gains a new member: M38 serves your `incidents` contract in a _fourth_ ecosystem (C#/.NET), and M41 watches it from the ops floor. _A contract outlives any one implementation._
- **The time thread** (data over time) continues through scroll-as-input (M28), time-of-day ranking (M29), audio playback (M30), and reaches its deepest floor in M36 (the Observable rebuilt from raw C streams). Lazy iteration (M40) is the same instinct in Python.
- **The floor thread** ("every convenience is a receipt") continues through Electron's bundled runtime (M31), the ESP32's kilobytes (M32), the Observable-from-scratch (M36), and — newly — M43, which rebuilds reactivity in vanilla JS to show _what React was doing for you_.
- **The security thread** (from M4) extends: M39 hardens the app surface (injection, XSS, SSRF, secrets) and M34 watches the network for what's talking to your machine.
- **The instruments thread** (new): tools that make thought or computation _legible and manipulable_ — a place to capture notes (M26), grabbing selected text and the clipboard (M27), a design file as spec (M44), and interactive diagrams that turn an algorithm into an object you can inspect (M37). Your own portfolio thesis in miniature.
- **The sovereignty thread** (new): computing you can _see all the way down and needn't trust anyone else for_ — a resilient network needing no infrastructure (M33), knowing exactly what's talking to your machine (M34), firmware on hardware you fully own (M32), and local-first data you keep (M45). The permacomputing instinct.
- **The sync line** (new, resurrected from the v2 plan): a local-first spaced-repetition deck (M45) and a shared multiplayer room (M46) deliberately duplicate their sync plumbing — so you _see_ the shared kernel before extracting it. _Extract the kernel after the duplication is visible, never before_ — your own principle, made into an exercise.

**Two deliberate exceptions to your open-source, non-Microsoft grain:** M38 (C#/.NET) and M42 (Power BI) cut against it on purpose — they're employability checkboxes, named as such so the exception proves the rule. M42's output also stays a standalone credential artifact, kept separate from your engineering-portfolio narrative, per your own positioning. Keep or skip either on that basis.

# PART A — Capture & Text

_Two small modules that are secretly the seed of every tools-for-thought project you'll ever build: a place to put words that persists them, and the browser APIs that let you grab words the user has already produced. The instruments thread, from zero._

---

## M26 — The Simplest Honest Loop · a notetaking feature · (S)

> **Story.** As a builder of tools-for-thought, I want a text field whose submissions persist and display newest-first, so that I have the irreducible seed every notes and annotation tool is built from — and I meet stored XSS where it actually lives. **MVP.** A textarea → submit → store the note with a server timestamp → render all notes newest-first, escaped. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - A submitted note survives a full page reload (it's in a database, not memory).
> - Notes render newest-first, and the ordering comes from the _query_, not a client-side reverse (so it survives pagination).
> - A note whose text contains HTML or a `<script>` tag renders as _literal text_ — it does not execute or alter the page.
> - An empty state (no notes yet) is shown distinctly.

**Anchors:** M1/M2 (you built the _contract_ version of a persisted resource; this is its plainest, most human form); _Where the Data Lives_; M4's security mindset applied to displayed input.

### Ground Floor — A form, a POST, a row

*Read this before the Briefing. It assumes M1 and M2's Ground Floors, so you've served a list from a database, but not that you've sent data the *other* way, from a form into a row. You'll build a throwaway guestbook first: type a name, save it, see it in a list. This tells you what each piece is for and what to look up, never how to write it. Ordering, escaping, timestamps, and optimistic display are the module's.*

#### GF.1 — The picture first

```
<textarea> + button
     │  submit event
     ▼
fetch POST /api/entries   body: {"text": "..."}   Content-Type: application/json
     │
     ▼
server route ──▶ validate ──▶ INSERT INTO entries ... ──▶ 201 Created + the new row
     │
     ▼
client re-fetches (or appends) ──▶ renders the list ──▶ the TEXT A STRANGER WROTE is now on your page
```

M1–M2 only read. This is the first time data flows in.

#### GF.2 — Forms, from zero

**Look up:**

- HTML forms: `<form>`, `<textarea>`, `<button type="submit">`, and what the browser does on submit by default (a full page navigation)
- `event.preventDefault()`, and why you call it when submitting with `fetch` instead
- Controlled inputs in React, and clearing the field after a successful submit

#### GF.3 — Sending data

**Look up:** `fetch` with `method: "POST"`, a JSON body, and the `Content-Type` header; how your backend framework reads a JSON request body (a `@RequestBody` in Spring, a Pydantic model parameter in FastAPI); and status 201 Created.

#### GF.4 — Creating a row

**Look up:** `INSERT ... RETURNING` in Postgres; column defaults, including a default of the current time; and why `INSERT` uses parameters, never string formatting (M2's Ground Floor covered why).

**Build:** the throwaway guestbook end to end: a form that posts a name and message, a table that stores them, and a list that shows them.

#### GF.5 — How the browser turns a string into a page

**Look up:**

- `innerHTML` vs `textContent` on a DOM element, and what each does with a string containing `<b>`
- How React inserts text from a variable into JSX, and what `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` is
- What cross-site scripting (XSS) is, as a definition only. Where it lives in this feature is M26's trap.

#### GF.6 — Run it and observe

**Notice and leave alone:**

1. **Submit an entry containing `<b>hi</b>`.** Does it show as bold, or as the literal characters? Then try it in a vanilla-JS page that renders with `innerHTML`, if you're curious. Don't decide where to defend yet. That's M26's second decision.
2. **Which entry is at the top of your list,** and what decided that? Add a few, reload, and check again. That's the ordering decision.
3. **Where does each entry's time come from,** if it has one? That's the timestamp decision.
4. **Submit, then kill the server before it responds.** What does the user see? That's the optimistic-display decision.
5. **Load the page with an empty table.** What shows?

#### GF.7 — Hand-off: what's yours in M26

| Ground Floor left this… | M26 asks you to… |
|---|---|
| A guestbook | A notes feature, create and list only |
| Whatever order rows came back in | Newest-first from the query, surviving a `LIMIT` |
| A `<b>` tag test, unresolved | Render user text inert: decide escape on output vs sanitize on input |
| Time from wherever | Decide who sets `created_at` |
| Submit and hope | Decide optimistic vs confirmed display |
| An empty page that just looks empty | A distinct empty state |

Then read the Briefing below.

#### GF.8 — Check yourself before moving on

- What does a form do on submit by default, and why do you stop it?
- What header tells your server the body is JSON?
- What does `RETURNING` give you after an `INSERT`?
- `innerHTML` vs `textContent`: what does each do with `<b>hi</b>`?
- What did your empty guestbook show?

### Briefing

This is the smallest complete full-stack loop there is, and every app you admire is secretly made of it repeated: a place to type, a submission that gets _stored_ so it survives a refresh, and a display that reads it back in a sensible order. Building it deliberately makes the round trip visible — a textarea's text becomes an HTTP request becomes a database row becomes, on the next load, rendered output. The center of gravity is **the round trip and the ordering** — and, quietly, what happens when the thing you store is text a stranger wrote.

### The trap

Two, and they're why a "trivial" notes app earns a module. **Stored XSS:** you save whatever the user typed and render it straight back, so a note containing `<script>` (or an `onerror` image tag) _executes_ when displayed — for you and anyone else who loads it. And **ordering in the wrong place:** "newest first" feels like "reverse the array in JavaScript," which works with three notes and breaks the instant you paginate, because you're only reversing the _page_ you fetched.

### The build

A textarea, a submit, persistence with a timestamp, and a reverse-chronological display rendered _safely_. The note shape is a spec you may take as given: `{ id, body, created_at }`, list ordered by `created_at DESC`. Create-and-list only.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Where ordering happens.** Options: `ORDER BY created_at DESC` in the query · fetch-then-reverse in JS. _Axis:_ correctness at any scale vs momentary convenience. _Forcing:_ If you show ten per page, does reversing the fetched array give the newest ten, or the reverse of an arbitrary ten?
2. **Escape on output vs sanitize on input.** Options: store raw, escape when rendering · clean the text before storing. _Axis:_ preserving what the user wrote vs mangling it early. _Forcing:_ If you sanitize on input and later want the raw text elsewhere, is it gone? Why is escape-at-the-moment-of-output more robust? (This is the trap.)
3. **Timestamp source.** Options: server sets `created_at` · client sends its time. _Axis:_ trustworthy ordering vs client control. _Forcing:_ With wrong client clocks, whose time orders the list correctly? (Reaches back to M2's `timestamptz`.)
4. **Optimistic vs confirmed display.** Options: show the note immediately · wait for the server's success. _Axis:_ snappy feel vs never showing a note that failed to save. _Forcing:_ If the save fails after you've shown it, what does the user believe happened?

### Research targets

- _Stored XSS, and why "escape on output" is the fix._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain why rendering user text unescaped runs their code, and where escaping belongs. _Seed:_ the OWASP XSS prevention cheat sheet (output-encoding section).
- _`ORDER BY` and why not to sort in application memory._ — **25 min.** Done when you can state why ordering belongs in the query, especially with a limit.
- _The `created_at` timestamp pattern._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say who should set it and why.

### Out of scope (fence)

Editing/deleting, rich text or markdown (which _reintroduces_ XSS harder — note, park), search, tags, authentication. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Submit a note whose text is `<b>hi</b>` or an image tag with an `onerror`. **The number:** does it render as visible literal text (safe) or does the formatting/script take effect (vulnerable)? Then add a note, reload, and confirm the newest sits at the top — proving the order came from the query.

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ where does stored XSS actually live in this feature, and why does client-side reversing break under pagination? **Check:** both traps explained before any code.
2. **Research.** The three targets. _Objective:_ escape-on-output justified against sanitize-on-input in one paragraph.
3. **Commit (DSA).** The four decisions, written. **Check:** each has a rejected alternative and a reason.
4. **Build — the loop.** Textarea → store with timestamp → render the list. **Check:** a note survives a full reload.
5. **Build — the order.** Newest-first from the query itself. **Check:** the ordering survives a `LIMIT` — the newest ten, not the reverse of an arbitrary ten.
6. **Walk into the trap.** Submit `<b>hi</b>` and an `onerror` image tag. **Check:** both render as literal text, inert.
7. **Measure & debrief.** The empty state shows distinctly; reload keeps order. _Ask:_ what did M1's contract discipline add that this plain loop lacks — and what does this loop teach that M1 hid? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** submit a script-containing note and show it rendered inert; reload and show newest-first surviving.

- **Interview (90s):** "It's a notes feature, but the two non-trivial parts are the ones I'd flag: I escape on output so a note containing a script tag can't run — that's stored XSS, and it lives in exactly this feature — and I order newest-first in the query, not with a client-side reverse, because reversing only flips the page you fetched once you paginate."
- **Design review:** a peer sanitizes on input and reverses client-side. Walk through "the raw text is gone" and "pagination breaks the reverse," constructively.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I built the simplest full-stack loop — type, store, show newest-first — and used it to actually understand stored XSS, which hides in every notes app."
- **Reach-back (instruments + data-lifecycle threads):** this is the humblest ancestor of dev-suite and every tool-for-thought you want. What's the same round trip you'd reuse, and what did M1's _contract_ discipline add on top of this plain version?
