## M27 — Grab What They Highlighted · selected text & clipboard in JS · (S)

> **Story.** As a tools-for-thought builder, I want to capture the user's text selection and read/write the clipboard, so that I have the capture primitives under every highlighter and clip-to-notes feature — handled on the browser's security terms, not fighting them. **MVP.** Read the current selection (its text and position); copy text to and read text from the system clipboard via the async API, on a user gesture, handling a denied permission. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - Highlighting text — even across element boundaries — yields the exact selected text.
> - A copy action lands text on the _real_ system clipboard (verify by pasting into another app).
> - A paste action reads the clipboard back into the page, on a user gesture.
> - Clipboard access without a user gesture (or when denied) fails _safely_ with a handled state, not an uncaught crash.

**Anchors:** M16 (text as a first-class concern); the "protective vs extractive friction" framework (the clipboard's permission gate is _protective_ friction — it buys the user a security property); your tools-for-thought instincts.

### Ground Floor — The DOM as a tree of nodes

*Read this before the Briefing. It assumes you know JavaScript and the DOM at the level of `querySelector`, but not that you've worked with text nodes or browser permissions. You'll log selections and poke at the clipboard on a throwaway page first. This tells you what each piece is for and what to look up, never how to write it. Which APIs, gestures, rich content, and denial handling are the module's.*

#### GF.1 — The picture first

```
<p>Hello <b>bold</b> world</p>

becomes nodes:
   P (element)
   ├── "Hello "     (text node)
   ├── B (element)
   │    └── "bold"  (text node)
   └── " world"     (text node)

Select from "lo" to "wo":  a RANGE = (start node, start offset) → (end node, end offset)
                           crossing three text nodes and one element

The clipboard: shared with EVERY app on the machine, so the browser guards it
```

#### GF.2 — Nodes, not just elements

**Look up:** DOM node types (element, text, comment); `childNodes` vs `children`; `nodeType`; and walking a node's children recursively.

**Do:** in the console, take any paragraph containing a link or bold text and print every child node with its type.

#### GF.3 — Selection and Range, as concepts

**Look up:**

- `window.getSelection()`, the `Selection` object, and `toString()`
- `Range`: start and end containers and offsets
- The `selectionchange` and `mouseup` events, and which fires when
- A form field's `selectionStart` and `selectionEnd`, as the simpler cousin

**Build:** a throwaway page with a few paragraphs containing bold and links. Log the selection's text and its start and end containers whenever the selection changes.

#### GF.4 — Why the clipboard is guarded

**Look up:**

- Secure contexts: why some APIs only exist on `https://` or `localhost`
- User activation (a "user gesture"): what counts, and how long it lasts
- The Permissions API, and how browsers differ on clipboard permissions
- `navigator.clipboard` vs `document.execCommand('copy')`, as history only. Choosing is M27's first decision.

#### GF.5 — Run it and observe

**Notice and leave alone:**

1. **Select text that starts in plain text and ends inside the bold.** What are the start and end containers? Are they the same node?
2. **Select inside a `<textarea>`** on the same page. Does `getSelection()` behave the same way?
3. **Call `navigator.clipboard.readText()` from the console** and then from a page-load script. What happened each time? Then call it from a button click. That's M27's trap.
4. **Open the page over a plain `http://` address** that isn't localhost, if you can, such as your laptop's LAN address from your phone. Is `navigator.clipboard` even defined?
5. **Copy formatted text from a website** and ask what the clipboard is actually holding. That's the plain-vs-rich decision.

#### GF.6 — Hand-off: what's yours in M27

| Ground Floor left this… | M27 asks you to… |
|---|---|
| Selections logged | Capture selection text and position, and do something visible with it |
| Clipboard calls that failed | Copy and paste on a user gesture, with the API you choose |
| Two kinds of selection seen | Choose Selection API vs field indices for your case |
| Rich content wondered about | Decide plain vs rich clipboard content |
| Uncaught rejections | Handle denied permission gracefully |

Then read the Briefing below.

#### GF.7 — Check yourself before moving on

- What's a text node, and why does a paragraph with bold text in it have more than one?
- What are a Range's four defining values?
- What's a secure context, and does `localhost` count?
- What happened when you read the clipboard on page load vs on a click?
- Why would a browser guard *reading* the clipboard more than writing to it?

### Briefing

Two browser capabilities turn a passive page into something you can _capture from_, and they're the raw material under every annotation tool. The **Selection API** tells you what the user highlighted — the text, and where it is, as a `Range` object. The **Clipboard API** reads from and writes to the system clipboard. The center of gravity is **capturing a selection as a structured Range, and the deliberately-gated clipboard** — because the browser will _not_ let you read the clipboard silently, and understanding _why_ is half the module.

### The trap

The **clipboard permission and security model**, which trips people who expect to just call `read()`. The clipboard is a cross-application secret (your passwords pass through it), so browsers gate access behind a user gesture and a permission, and arbitrary reads are _supposed_ to fail — code that assumes silent access breaks correctly. Add the legacy confusion: the old `document.execCommand('copy')` is deprecated in favor of the async `navigator.clipboard`, so tutorials disagree. And Selection `Range`s are fiddly — a selection can span multiple DOM nodes, so "the selected text" isn't always a simple string grab.

### The build

A capture surface: (a) on selection, read the selection's text and, ideally, position, and do something visible with it; (b) a copy action writing text to the clipboard via the async API, and a paste action reading it — both tied to a user gesture, both handling a denied permission. No backend.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Async Clipboard API vs legacy `execCommand`.** Options: `navigator.clipboard` · `document.execCommand`. _Axis:_ the modern, secure, supported path vs older-browser reach. _Forcing:_ Which are vendors moving toward, and what does the async one require that the old one didn't (a gesture, a permission)?
2. **Where clipboard access is triggered.** Options: on an explicit user action · on load or a timer. _Axis:_ works-and-is-trusted vs blocked-by-design. _Forcing:_ Why does the browser _require_ a user gesture for reads especially? What protection does that gate provide? (Name the protective friction.)
3. **Selection API vs input `selectionStart/End`.** Options: `window.getSelection()` + `Range` · a form field's selection indices. _Axis:_ arbitrary-page selection vs the simple form-field case. _Forcing:_ Is the text in a plain `<textarea>` (simple indices) or anywhere on the page (Ranges across nodes)?
4. **Plain vs rich clipboard content.** Options: plain text · `ClipboardItem` with MIME types. _Axis:_ simplicity vs preserving formatting. _Forcing:_ Do you need formatting, or is plain text the honest scope?
5. **Permission-denied behavior.** Options: assume success · detect rejection and inform/fall back. _Axis:_ less code vs a usable feature. _Forcing:_ When `read()` rejects, does the UI break silently or tell the user?

### Research targets

- _The Selection and Range API._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain how a selection spanning two elements is represented, and how you extract its text. _Seed:_ MDN `Selection`/`Range`.
- _The async Clipboard API and its permission model._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can state what a read requires (gesture, permission, secure context) and how it rejects. _Seed:_ MDN Clipboard API.
- _Why clipboard access is restricted — the reasoning._ — **25 min.** Done when you can articulate the cross-application-secret argument (your protective-friction framing).
- _`execCommand` deprecation — what replaced it and why._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say why the sync API gave way to the async one.

### Out of scope (fence)

Copying images/rich media, drag-and-drop APIs (note, park), full `contenteditable` rich editing, Safari/Firefox permission quirks (note, park), building the whole annotation tool. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Copy via your button, then paste into a _different_ application. **The number:** did the text arrive? Then trigger a clipboard read with no user gesture and confirm it _fails safely_ (the protective gate working, your handling catching it). Two behaviors: works-on-gesture, fails-clean-off-gesture — do both hold?

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>getSelection gives me weird/empty results when the selection crosses elements</summary>

**Orient:** a selection isn't a string — it's one or more `Range`s over DOM nodes, and "the text" is something you extract _from_ the range, not a property handed to you in every case.

**Approach:** get the `Selection` from `window.getSelection()`, read its string form for the simple case, or pull the `Range` (`getRangeAt(0)`) when you need position or when the simple string is empty. A range spanning elements has a start container/offset and an end container/offset in _different_ nodes — the text between them crosses node boundaries, which is why a naive `.textContent` of one node misses it. Extract from the range itself.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ why is the clipboard gated — what secret passes through it — and what does the browser demand before it opens? **Check:** the threat and the two conditions (gesture, permission) stated.
2. **Research.** The four targets. _Objective:_ Selection/Range API shape sketched; the async Clipboard API's permission flow narrated.
3. **Commit (DSA).** The five decisions, written — including exactly what happens on permission-denied. **Check:** the denied path is designed, not hoped away.
4. **Build — the selection.** Read the selection's text and position on select; do something visible with it. **Check:** works across multi-node selections, not just single text nodes.
5. **Build — the write.** Copy to clipboard on a user gesture. **Check:** paste into another app proves it landed.
6. **Build — the read.** Paste-from-clipboard, gesture-gated. **Check:** it reads back what an external app copied.
7. **Walk into the trap, then debrief.** Deny the permission and use the feature anyway. **Check:** your designed fallback appears — no console error as UX. _Ask:_ why did the browser make you work this hard, and are you glad? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** highlight cross-element text and show it captured; copy-then-paste into another app; a gesture-less read failing cleanly.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built the capture primitives for an annotation tool — reading the selection as a Range, and read/write to the clipboard with the async API. The interesting part is that clipboard access is deliberately gated behind a user gesture and a permission, because the clipboard is a cross-app secret — so 'it fails silently off-gesture' is the API working as designed, and I handle that rather than fighting it."
- **Design review:** a peer used deprecated `execCommand` and tries to read the clipboard on load. Explain the modern async API and why the gesture requirement is protective, not an obstacle.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been playing with the Selection and Clipboard APIs — the foundation of any highlight-to-notes tool — and the neat thing is how the clipboard's permission gate is a feature, not a bug."
- **Reach-back (instruments thread):** M26 stored words the user _typed_; this grabs words they _selected or copied_. Combine them — what's the smallest tool-for-thought those two modules already let you build?

---

# PART B — Responsive Surfaces

_UI as a function of a changing input — scroll position, the time of day, playback time. Three surfaces that react to something continuous, and the performance and time-math traps that come with each. The time thread, at browser altitude._

---

## M28 — Full-Bleed Gallery · a scroll-driven image gallery with overlays · (M)

> **Story.** As a design-engineer building a portfolio-grade site, I want a full-viewport image gallery driven by scroll, with text overlays and award-tier polish, so that I can present my own images with the craft that wins Awwwards — and learn scroll-driven UI without the jank. **MVP.** Full-viewport (100vh) images _I supply_, advanced smoothly by scroll, each with a legible overlay, performant. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - Each image fills the viewport full-bleed, with no distortion, across images of varying dimensions.
> - Scrolling advances through the gallery smoothly, with the intended transition, at a steady frame rate.
> - Overlays (titles/captions) stay legible over _any_ image — light or dark.
> - Images appear without janky pop-in as you scroll; the page never stutters.

**Anchors:** _Pixels With Intent_ (rendering pipeline and compositing); reach-backs to M15 (layout), M16 (avoiding reflow), M17 (60fps, award-tier). You'll drop in your own images (photography or a curated set), which also makes this portfolio-real.

### Ground Floor — Viewports, scroll, and what's cheap to animate

*Read this before the Briefing. It assumes M15's Ground Floor on layout and some of M17's on frames, but not that you've driven visuals from scroll or inspected the compositor. You'll build throwaway full-height colored panels first. This tells you what each piece is for and what to look up, never how to write it. The scroll primitive, transitions, image fit, overlay legibility, loading, and reduced motion are the module's.*

#### GF.1 — The picture first

```
viewport = the visible window; 100vh = its height (mostly — phones complicate this)

  scrollY = 0        ┌───────────┐
                     │  panel 1  │  ← in view
  scrollY = 1×vh     ├───────────┤
                     │  panel 2  │
  scrollY = 2×vh     ├───────────┤
                     │  panel 3  │
                     └───────────┘

scroll position ──▶ which panel, how far through it ──▶ visual state

Cheap to change:  transform, opacity            → COMPOSITE only (GPU)
Costly to change: top, height, width, margin    → LAYOUT, then PAINT, then COMPOSITE
```

#### GF.2 — The viewport

**Look up:** `vh`, and the newer `svh`, `lvh`, and `dvh` units, and why mobile browsers' collapsing toolbars made them necessary; `position: sticky` vs `fixed`; and full-bleed layout (an element that spans the viewport edge to edge, ignoring its container's padding).

**Build:** a throwaway page of five full-viewport panels, each a different solid color with a large number in the middle.

#### GF.3 — Scroll as an input

**Look up:**

- The `scroll` event, how often it fires, and passive listeners
- `window.scrollY` and an element's position relative to the viewport
- `IntersectionObserver`: thresholds and what an entry reports
- CSS `scroll-snap`, and CSS scroll-driven animations (`animation-timeline`), as names. Choosing among these is M28's first decision.

**Do:** count how many scroll events fire during one slow scroll, and log it.

#### GF.4 — The compositor

**Look up:** *Pixels With Intent*, its rendering-pipeline and compositing chapters. Then confirm which CSS properties trigger layout, which only paint, and which only composite (csstriggers.com is a classic reference). Look up `will-change` and what it costs.

**Do:** in DevTools' Rendering tab, turn on Paint flashing and Layer borders and scroll your page.

#### GF.5 — Images at full size

**Look up:** an image's natural size vs displayed size; `object-fit` and `object-position`, as names; `loading="lazy"`; `img.decode()`; and `srcset` with `sizes`.

**Do:** replace your colored panels with a few of your own photos of different shapes. Use the simplest markup that works.

#### GF.6 — Run it and observe

**Notice and leave alone:**

1. **Animate something by changing `top` on every scroll event,** then the same visual by changing `transform`. Record both in the Performance panel. Compare frames and paint flashing. That's M28's trap.
2. **Scroll fast to the last photo on a throttled network.** Is there a flash or pop-in? That's the loading decision.
3. **Look at your photos' shapes against the viewport.** Did any distort, crop, or letterbox? That's the fit decision.
4. **Put white text over your lightest photo.** Can you read it?
5. **Open the page on your phone and scroll.** Did `100vh` behave?
6. **Turn on reduced motion** in your OS. Did anything change?

#### GF.7 — Hand-off: what's yours in M28

| Ground Floor left this… | M28 asks you to… |
|---|---|
| A scroll-event counter | Choose a scroll primitive that doesn't thrash |
| `top` vs `transform`, compared | Choose a transition and animate only cheap properties |
| Photos displayed however they landed | Fit varied images full-bleed with no distortion |
| White text you can't read | Keep overlays legible over any image |
| Pop-in on a slow network | Choose a loading strategy with no pop-in |
| Reduced motion ignored | Decide what the gallery becomes for reduced-motion users |
| Colored panels | Your own images, at award quality |

Then read the Briefing below.

#### GF.8 — Check yourself before moving on

- What's the difference between `vh` and `dvh`, and why does it matter on a phone?
- How many scroll events fired during your slow scroll?
- Which CSS properties are composite-only?
- What did paint flashing show when you animated `top`?
- What does `IntersectionObserver` tell you that a scroll event doesn't?

### Briefing

Full-viewport ("full-bleed") imagery advanced by scroll is the signature of award-winning editorial and portfolio sites. Underneath the polish is one idea — **map scroll position to visual state** — done on the browser's terms: on the compositor, not by thrashing layout. The overlays add a second problem, legibility of text over arbitrary images. The center of gravity is **scroll-as-input rendered cheaply, plus contrast over images you don't control.**

### The trap

**Scroll jank, and the wrong primitive.** Scroll events fire at very high frequency; doing layout or heavy work in the scroll handler — or animating layout properties like `top`/`height` — causes stutter (the same performance floor as M17, the same throttle lesson as M5). Its partners: **image pop-in** (a full-viewport image that isn't decoded yet flashes as you reach it) and **overlay illegibility** (white text vanishing over a light photo). The tell: your fan spins up and the scroll feels sticky.

### The build

A full-viewport gallery over a user-supplied, ordered set of images, scroll driving progression with a chosen transition, an overlay per image, and a loading strategy that avoids pop-in. No backend. Data shape (a spec): an ordered list of `{ image, overlayText }`.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building. Design the intended effect / reference board first._

1. **Scroll primitive.** Options: a throttled/`rAF` scroll handler · `IntersectionObserver` · CSS scroll-driven animations (scroll-timeline) · a library (GSAP ScrollTrigger, Lenis). _Axis:_ control vs performance vs modernity vs batteries-included. _Forcing:_ Which runs off the main thread and avoids work per scroll tick? Do you need _continuous_ progress (parallax) or _discrete_ (snap between images)? (`IntersectionObserver` fires on threshold crossings — cheap; a scroll event fires constantly — needs `rAF`.)
2. **Transition style.** Options: hard snap (`scroll-snap`) · crossfade · pin-and-parallax · horizontal-on-vertical-scroll. _Axis:_ simplicity vs the award-tier feel. _Forcing:_ Which properties animate — `opacity`/`transform` (compositor-safe) or `top`/`width`/`height` (layout, janky)? (This is the trap.)
3. **Full-bleed image fit.** Options: `object-fit: cover` · `contain` · art-directed crops per breakpoint. _Axis:_ fill-the-viewport vs no-cropping vs editorial control. _Forcing:_ Your images vary in aspect ratio — `cover` crops, `contain` letterboxes; which serves a full-bleed gallery, and how do you avoid distortion?
4. **Overlay legibility over arbitrary images.** Options: fixed text color · a gradient scrim/backdrop · dynamic contrast (sample the image's luminance). _Axis:_ simplicity vs guaranteed readability. _Forcing:_ What keeps white text readable over a _light_ image — a scrim, a backdrop-filter, or measuring luminance and flipping the color? (Reaches back to M15/M16.)
5. **Image loading strategy.** Options: load all up front · lazy-load with preload-ahead · responsive `srcset` + a blur-up placeholder. _Axis:_ simplicity vs no-pop-in smoothness at award quality. _Forcing:_ How do you avoid a flash when the next full-viewport image isn't decoded yet? What does preloading the next image (or a low-res placeholder + `img.decode()`) buy you?
6. **Reduced motion.** Options: assume motion · honor `prefers-reduced-motion`. _Axis:_ simplicity vs accessibility (award sites still need it). _Forcing:_ What does the gallery _become_ for a reduced-motion user — a plain vertical scroll of images? (Same instinct as M17.)

### Research targets

- _Scroll-driven animation: scroll event vs `IntersectionObserver` vs CSS scroll-timeline._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can say which avoids main-thread work per tick and when each fits. _Seed:_ MDN scroll-driven animations; the `IntersectionObserver` page.
- _Compositor-safe animation (`transform`/`opacity` vs layout properties)._ — **25 min.** Done when you can name which properties are cheap and why (reaches M17). _Seed:_ web.dev "Animations and performance."
- _`object-fit` and full-bleed image handling._ — **25 min.** Done when you can fill a viewport with a varying-aspect image without distortion.
- _Avoiding image pop-in: preloading, `img.decode()`, `srcset`, blur-up._ — **25 min.** Done when you can describe eliminating the flash of an undecoded next image.
- _Overlay contrast over images (scrim/gradient/luminance)._ — **25 min.** Done when you can keep text legible over a light and a dark image.

### Out of scope (fence)

Full page-transition frameworks, video backgrounds, WebGL image transitions/shaders (note — park; that's the deep award end and overlaps M17), infinite scroll, a CMS for the images. → parking lot. **And: study the award-site effects you admire, but save source-diving until after your DSA** (post-commit), same discipline as M17.

### Measure ritual

Open the Performance panel and scroll hard through the gallery. **The number:** does it hold ~60fps (≤16.7ms/frame) with no layout-thrash spikes, and do images appear without pop-in? Then eyeball the worst-contrast overlay — is the text still legible over its image?

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>My scroll animation stutters</summary>

**Orient:** something is running on the main thread every scroll tick, or you're animating a layout property.

**Approach:** move per-scroll work off the hot path — prefer `IntersectionObserver` (fires only on threshold crossings) or CSS scroll-timeline over a JS scroll handler; and animate only `transform`/`opacity` (compositor), never `top`/`left`/`width`/`height` (layout). If you must use a scroll handler, coalesce it into a single `requestAnimationFrame`. In the Performance panel, purple "Layout" bars appearing during scroll _are_ the jank — chase them.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ why do scroll handlers jank — what work is compositor-safe and what isn't? **Check:** `transform`/`opacity` vs layout properties sorted correctly, aloud.
2. **Research.** The five targets. _Objective:_ scroll event vs IntersectionObserver vs CSS scroll-timeline compared with a use case each.
3. **Commit (DSA).** The six decisions — scroll primitive first, reduced-motion not last. **Check:** written before any scrolling code.
4. **Build — the bones.** Full-viewport images in order, correctly fitted (`object-fit`), no interaction yet. **Check:** no pop-in on a hard reload with a cold cache.
5. **Build — the drive.** Scroll advancing the gallery via your chosen primitive with your chosen transition. **Check:** DevTools performance recording during a fast scroll shows no long frames.
6. **Build — the voice.** Overlays legible over your images. **Check:** legible over the worst image (brightest, busiest), not the best.
7. **Walk into the trap, then debrief.** Set `prefers-reduced-motion` and revisit. **Check:** a dignified non-animated experience. _Ask:_ which of M5's throttle lessons and M17's frame-budget lessons reappeared here? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** scroll the full gallery at a held frame rate (show the meter); an overlay legible over a light _and_ a dark image; no pop-in.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a full-bleed scroll-driven image gallery at award-site polish. The decision I'd defend is using `IntersectionObserver`/CSS scroll-timeline over a JS scroll handler and animating only compositor-safe properties — scroll jank comes from main-thread work per tick and animating layout. I handled overlay contrast over arbitrary images with a gradient scrim, and preloaded-and-decoded the next image to kill pop-in."
- **Design review:** a peer animates in a scroll handler mutating `top`/`height` and it janks. Diagnose main-thread work plus layout thrash; propose `IntersectionObserver` and transforms.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I'm building a full-viewport scroll gallery for my portfolio — the craft is all in holding 60fps and keeping captions readable over any image."
- **Reach-back (time + floor threads):** scroll is another continuous input (position instead of the clock), and this sits on the same compositor floor as M17 and the reflow lesson of M16. State the one performance discipline shared across M16, M17, and this.

---

## M29 — Open Now, Then Sorted · time-of-day conditional ranking · (M)

> **Story.** As a front-end engineer, I want a table that's sorted by one criterion but floats "currently open" items to the top based on each item's own hours, so that I can build the "Open now" ranking real apps (maps, delivery) use — and handle the moving-target and midnight-crossing pitfalls. **MVP.** A table of items, each with their own weekly open-hours; items open _right now_ rise above an otherwise-sorted list, and the ranking recomputes as time passes. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - Items open at the current time appear above closed ones, while the existing sort (e.g. rating) orders _within_ each group.
> - An item whose hours cross midnight (open 22:00–02:00) is classified correctly.
> - "Open now" status updates as the clock advances — it is not frozen at page load.
> - Timezone/DST is handled sensibly (you decide how — but decide).

**Anchors:** M12 (time logic, walking backward through time), M14 (time windows), _Where the Data Lives_. The time thread, ranked by a moving "now."

### Ground Floor — Sorting and clock arithmetic

*Read this before the Briefing. It assumes M12's Ground Floor on dates, but not that you've written a custom comparator or reasoned about time-of-day ranges. You'll sort a throwaway table and test whether "now" falls inside a range first. This tells you what each piece is for and what to look up, never how to write it. Where it's computed, hour representation, sort stability, grouping strategy, time zones, and refresh cadence are the module's.*

#### GF.1 — The picture first

```
items ──▶ for each, COMPUTE: isOpenNow(item.hours, now)   ← derived, never stored
      ──▶ SORT with a comparator (a, b) ─▶ negative | 0 | positive
      ──▶ render

                 now changes every minute ─────────┐
                                                   ▼
                                         the answer above changes too
```

#### GF.2 — Comparators

**Look up:**

- `Array.prototype.sort` with a comparator, and what its return values mean
- The default sort with no comparator, and what it does to numbers (try `[10, 9, 1].sort()`)
- `sort` vs `toSorted`, and which one mutates
- What "stable sort" means, as a definition. Whether you can rely on it is M29's third decision.

**Build:** a throwaway table of books, sortable by rating descending, then by title as a tiebreak.

#### GF.3 — Time of day as numbers

**Look up:** getting the current day of the week, hour, and minute in JavaScript; representing a time of day as minutes since midnight; and parsing `"22:00"` into a number.

**Build:** a function that answers "is it lunchtime right now?" for a range of 12:00 to 13:30, given a `now` you pass in. Test it with several invented "nows."

#### GF.4 — Time zones, as vocabulary

**Look up:** `Intl.DateTimeFormat` with a `timeZone` option; IANA time zone names like `America/New_York`; what DST does to local time twice a year; and the Temporal proposal, as a name.

**Do:** print the current time in Philadelphia, London, and Tokyo.

#### GF.5 — Something that changes while you watch

**Look up:** `setInterval` in a React effect, and cleaning it up; and why a value computed once in render goes stale.

**Build:** show the current time on the page, updating each minute.

#### GF.6 — Run it and observe

**Notice and leave alone:**

1. **Change your lunchtime range to 22:00 to 02:00.** Ask it about 01:00. What does it say? That's M29's trap.
2. **Sort your books by rating where several share a rating,** then sort the result again by something else. Did the earlier order survive within the ties? That's the stability decision.
3. **Imagine your list includes a café in Tokyo.** Whose clock decides whether it's open? That's the time-zone decision.
4. **Leave the page open across a lunchtime boundary.** Did anything update on its own? That's the recompute decision.

#### GF.7 — Hand-off: what's yours in M29

| Ground Floor left this… | M29 asks you to… |
|---|---|
| A lunchtime check | "Open now" from each item's own weekly hours |
| A same-day range | Represent hours that cross midnight |
| A sort with a tiebreak | Float open items up without scrambling the rating order |
| Ties tested | Decide whether to rely on stability or build a composite key |
| Three clocks printed | Decide whose time zone judges "open" |
| A clock that ticks | Recompute at the right cadence |
| Everything client-side by default | Decide where "open now" is computed |

Then read the Briefing below.

#### GF.8 — Check yourself before moving on

- What does a comparator return to say "a comes before b"?
- What does `[10, 9, 1].sort()` return, and why?
- How do you turn `"22:00"` into minutes since midnight?
- What did your lunchtime check say about 01:00 for a 22:00–02:00 range?
- What's a stable sort?

### Briefing

This is multi-criteria sorting where the _primary_ key is **computed and time-dependent**. "Open now" is a moving target: whether an item qualifies changes as time passes and depends on each item's own schedule. The center of gravity is **layering a computed, time-dependent primary sort on top of a stable secondary sort** — and surviving the time-math pitfalls that make grown engineers wince.

### The trap

**The moving target, the midnight crossing, and the unstable sort.** "Open now" computed once at load goes stale as the user sits there. Hours that wrap past midnight (22:00–02:00) break a naive `open <= now <= close` check — 01:00 tests as closed. And floating open items up with an unstable sort can scramble the rating order you already established within each group. The tell: it looks right at 3pm on a Tuesday and wrong at 1am.

### The build

A table sorted by a secondary criterion (say rating, descending), a per-item "open now" boolean derived from weekly hours, open items grouped above closed, recomputed over time. Item shape (a spec): `{ id, name, rating, hours: [{ day, open, close }, …] }`. "Open now" is _derived_, never stored.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Where "open now" is computed.** Options: client · server. _Axis:_ recompute-as-time-passes on the client vs consistent server truth. _Forcing:_ Computed server-side at request time, does it go stale while the user reads? Who owns "now"? (Reaches M26's timestamp question.)
2. **Representing hours that cross midnight.** Options: same-day `open`/`close` · allow `close < open` to mean "next day" · store intervals as minutes-from-week-start. _Axis:_ simple vs correct across midnight. _Forcing:_ How do you represent 22:00–02:00 so "is 01:00 open?" is true? (This is the trap.)
3. **Stable vs unstable sort for the grouping.** Options: rely on a stable sort · a composite key. _Axis:_ preserve the secondary order vs scramble it. _Forcing:_ After floating open items up, is the rating order within each block preserved? Is your sort stable, or do you need a composite comparator?
4. **Composite key vs two-pass partition.** Options: one comparator (open-first, then rating) · partition-then-concatenate. _Axis:_ single expression vs explicit blocks. _Forcing:_ Which expresses "open above closed, each block by rating" more clearly _and_ stably?
5. **Timezone/DST.** Options: local device time · the item's timezone · a fixed zone. _Axis:_ simplicity vs correctness for items elsewhere. _Forcing:_ Is a place's "open now" judged in the _user's_ zone or the _item's_? What breaks on a DST boundary? (Reaches M2's `timestamptz`.)
6. **Recompute cadence.** Options: on an interval (tick) · on interaction · exactly at the next open/close boundary. _Axis:_ freshness vs work. _Forcing:_ How often must "open now" refresh to never be wrong — every minute, or precisely at the next boundary?

### Research targets

- _Multi-key / composite sorting and sort stability._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain a comparator that sorts by open-status then rating without scrambling ties. _Seed:_ MDN `Array.prototype.sort` (stability).
- _Representing and testing time intervals that cross midnight._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can correctly answer "is now within this interval?" for a 22:00–02:00 window.
- _Timezone and DST pitfalls._ — **25 min.** Done when you can name why local-time math breaks across DST and what a zone-aware approach fixes.
- _Keeping a time-derived value fresh (interval vs next-boundary scheduling)._ — **25 min.** Done when you can describe refreshing exactly at the next boundary.

### Out of scope (fence)

Full calendar/holiday handling, per-item timezones at scale (note, park), natural-language hours parsing ("open till late"), a real maps backend. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Mock "now" to a time where some items straddle open/closed and one crosses midnight. **The number:** are exactly the open items on top, each block still rating-ordered, and the midnight-crosser classified right? Then advance the clock past a boundary and confirm the list re-ranks.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>My midnight-crossing hours misclassify</summary>

**Orient:** a naive `open <= now <= close` assumes `close > open`, which fails when the interval wraps past midnight.

**Approach:** detect the wrap (`close < open` means it spans midnight) and, for that case, treat "open" as `now >= open` **OR** `now <= close`; or normalize everything to minutes-from-week-start as intervals that can exceed a day, so the wrap disappears entirely. Test 01:00 against a 22:00–02:00 window explicitly — that single case catches the bug.

</details>

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** a mocked "now" showing correct grouping, a midnight-crosser correct, and a boundary re-rank.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built an 'open now' ranking — items open at the current time float above an otherwise rating-sorted table. The subtle parts: hours crossing midnight break naive interval checks, a stable/composite sort so floating open items up doesn't scramble the rating order, and recomputing as time passes because 'open now' is a moving target, not a load-time fact."
- **Design review:** a peer computes open-now once at load with `open <= now <= close`. Name the staleness and midnight bugs; propose the fixes.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been building the 'Open now' sort that maps and delivery apps use — the gotcha is that 'now' keeps moving and some places are open past midnight."
- **Reach-back (time thread):** M12 walked backward through time, M14 windowed a stream; this ranks by a moving "now." What's shared about treating time as a first-class input, and what's unique here?

## M30 — Transport Controls · a JS audio player · (S)

> **Story.** As a front-end engineer, I want an audio player with standard transport controls, so that I can handle real media state — buffering, seeking, autoplay policy — and sync a progress UI without drift. **MVP.** Play/pause, seek (scrubber), volume, current-time/duration display, over a provided audio file. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - Play/pause works and the button reflects _actual_ playback state.
> - The scrubber seeks, and tracks playback as it advances.
> - Volume works.
> - The progress bar advances smoothly (not choppy) and stays locked to real playback time; `play()` handles an autoplay-policy rejection gracefully; seeking while still loading behaves sanely.

**Anchors:** M5 (async and events); M17 (`requestAnimationFrame` for smooth UI); _Waiting, All the Way Up_ (events).

### Ground Floor — The media element and its events

*Read this before the Briefing. It assumes JavaScript and React, but not that you've driven an audio element or listened to its state events. You'll build a throwaway play button and time readout first. This tells you what each piece is for and what to look up, never how to write it. Element vs Web Audio, progress strategy, the `play()` promise, seeking during load, and state reflection are the module's.*

#### GF.1 — The picture first

```
                   you DRIVE it:   play()  pause()  currentTime = 42  volume = 0.5
                          │
<audio src="...">  ◀──────┘
     │
     │  it REPORTS back, as events:
     ├── loadedmetadata   (duration is now known)
     ├── canplay / waiting (buffering)
     ├── play / pause / ended
     └── timeupdate        (a few times per second)
     ▼
your UI: button label, time readout, scrubber position
```

The element is the source of truth. Your UI is a reflection of it.

#### GF.2 — The media element

**Look up:**

- `HTMLMediaElement`: `src`, `paused`, `currentTime`, `duration`, `volume`, `readyState`, `buffered`
- Its events, and the order they fire in when a file loads
- What `readyState`'s numbers mean
- Holding a reference to the element in React with `useRef`

**Build:** a throwaway page with an audio file, a play/pause button, and a readout of current time and duration. Use the simplest code that works.

#### GF.3 — Inputs for the controls

**Look up:** `<input type="range">`: `min`, `max`, `step`, `value`, and its `input` vs `change` events; and formatting seconds as `m:ss`.

**Build:** a volume slider.

#### GF.4 — Autoplay policy

**Look up:** browser autoplay policies (Chrome's and Safari's); that `play()` returns a promise; and what a "user activation" is (M27's Ground Floor touched it).

#### GF.5 — Run it and observe

**Notice and leave alone:**

1. **Log every `timeupdate`** with a timestamp. How many per second? Would a progress bar driven by those look smooth? That's M30's trap.
2. **Call `play()` on page load,** before any click. What happened to the promise? That's the `play()` decision.
3. **Throttle the network and set `currentTime`** before the file has loaded much. What happens? That's the seeking decision.
4. **Pause from your OS media keys or headphone button,** or let the track end. Does your button's label still match reality? That's the state-reflection decision.
5. **Ask whether you'd need a waveform or effects.** That's the element vs Web Audio decision.

#### GF.6 — Hand-off: what's yours in M30

| Ground Floor left this… | M30 asks you to… |
|---|---|
| The `<audio>` element, by default | Choose the element or Web Audio |
| A time readout from `timeupdate` | Smooth progress that never drifts from real playback |
| A `play()` promise ignored | Handle autoplay rejection |
| A seek on a throttled network | Handle seeking before the file is ready |
| A button that may lie | Reflect actual playback state at all times |
| No scrubber | A scrubber that seeks and tracks |

Then read the Briefing below.

#### GF.7 — Check yourself before moving on

- Which event tells you the duration is known?
- What's the source of truth for playback position?
- How many `timeupdate` events fired per second?
- What happened to `play()` on page load?
- What did your button show after your OS paused the audio?

### Briefing

An `<audio>` element (or the Web Audio API) exposes playback and a stream of state events; building transport controls means both _driving_ that state and _reflecting_ it. The center of gravity is **media state — loading, buffering, playing, ended — and syncing the UI to real playback time without drift.**

### The trap

**Async loading plus media state, and progress-bar drift.** `play()` returns a promise that can _reject_ (autoplay policies block sound without a user gesture). Audio has buffering states, so seeking before it's loaded is fiddly. And the `timeupdate` event fires coarsely (~4×/sec), so a progress bar driven by it looks choppy — smooth progress wants `requestAnimationFrame`, but the _source of truth_ must stay the element's `currentTime`, or your animated bar drifts out of sync. The tell: a stuttery scrubber that slowly disagrees with the audio.

### The build

Transport UI over a provided audio file — play/pause, seek, volume, time display, smooth progress. Consumes an audio source URL; reflects the element's state. No backend.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **`<audio>` element vs Web Audio API.** Options: the element · Web Audio. _Axis:_ simple standard controls vs full processing/visualization power. _Forcing:_ Do you need effects or a waveform (Web Audio) or just transport (the element)? (M12-style: right tool for the job.)
2. **Progress updates.** Options: `timeupdate` events · `requestAnimationFrame` reading `currentTime`. _Axis:_ simple/coarse vs smooth. _Forcing:_ `timeupdate` fires ~4×/sec — smooth enough for a scrubber? If you `rAF`, what's the source of truth so you don't drift? (This is the trap.)
3. **Handling the `play()` promise.** Options: ignore it · `await`/`catch` the rejection. _Axis:_ less code vs handling autoplay blocks. _Forcing:_ On some browsers `play()` rejects without a gesture — what does the UI show then?
4. **Seeking during load.** Options: assume loaded · handle the not-yet-seekable state. _Axis:_ simple vs robust. _Forcing:_ The user drags the scrubber before enough has buffered — what happens, and what _should_?
5. **State reflection.** Options: track your own `isPlaying` flag · derive from the element's `play`/`pause`/`ended` events. _Axis:_ simple vs never-out-of-sync. _Forcing:_ If playback ends or the OS pauses it, does your button reflect it?

### Research targets

- _The `HTMLMediaElement` API: `currentTime`, `duration`, `buffered`, `play()`/`pause()`, events._ — **25 min.** _Seed:_ MDN `HTMLAudioElement`.
- _Autoplay policies and the `play()` promise rejection._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain why sound needs a gesture and how to handle the rejection.
- _Smooth progress: `timeupdate` vs `rAF`, with `currentTime` as source of truth._ — **25 min.** (Reaches M17's `rAF`.)
- _Buffering and `seekable`/`buffered` ranges._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say what those tell you.

### Out of scope (fence)

Playlists, Web Audio visualizations/effects (note, park; overlaps M17/M37), streaming/HLS, custom codecs, the Media Session API. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Play the file and watch the scrubber. **The number:** does the progress bar advance smoothly and stay locked to actual `currentTime` (no drift over a few minutes), and does `play()` fail gracefully when blocked? Scrub mid-load and confirm sane behavior.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** play/pause/seek/volume; smooth in-sync progress; a graceful autoplay-block.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built an audio player with standard transport. Two real bits: `play()` returns a promise that rejects under autoplay policies, so I handle that; and a progress bar driven by `timeupdate` is choppy because it fires ~4×/sec, so I animate with `requestAnimationFrame` but keep the element's `currentTime` as the source of truth to avoid drift."
- **Design review:** a peer tracks an `isPlaying` boolean and drives progress off `timeupdate`; it desyncs and stutters. Propose deriving state from media events and `rAF`-with-`currentTime`.
- **Meetup (30s):** "Quick audio player with real controls — the surprise was how much state (buffering, autoplay blocks) hides behind a play button."
- **Reach-back (time thread):** playback time is another continuous input, like scroll (M28) and streams (M5). What smoothing technique do M28, M30, and M17 all share?
