# PART C — Beyond the Browser

_Two modules of code that leaves the sandbox: a desktop app that must reach the OS across a process boundary, and firmware on a chip with kilobytes of RAM and no operating system. The floor thread and the sovereignty thread, meeting at the metal._

---

## M31 — The Window Is a Process Away · Electron window control · (S)

> **Story.** As a developer building a desktop app, I want to control window sizing, transparency, and always-on-top from Electron, so that I can build native-feeling desktop windows — and understand the main/renderer process split and its security model. **MVP.** An Electron app whose window has a set default size, resizes within limits, and can toggle transparency and always-on-top. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - The window opens at a defined default size and respects min/max resize bounds.
> - A control toggles always-on-top (the window stays above other windows).
> - A control toggles transparency (a see-through window).
> - Window controls invoked from the UI actually take effect _via the correct process boundary_.
> - The renderer does **not** have Node integration enabled (secure default).

**Anchors:** the floor thread (Electron is Chromium + Node bundled — a convenience with a receipt); the security material from M4 and the networking primer.

### Ground Floor — Two kinds of process

*Read this before the Briefing. It assumes you know Node and web front ends, but not that you've built a desktop app or crossed a process boundary on purpose. You'll get a throwaway Electron window open and send one message between processes first. This tells you what each piece is for and what to look up, never how to write it. Where control runs, the security setup, transparency, framing, and sizing are the module's.*

#### GF.1 — The picture first

```
┌─────────────────────── your Electron app ───────────────────────┐
│                                                                  │
│  MAIN process (one)             RENDERER process (one per window)│
│  Node.js, full OS access        Chromium page: your HTML/JS UI   │
│  owns BrowserWindow             sandboxed like a web page        │
│        ▲                                   │                     │
│        │◀────────── IPC messages ──────────┘                     │
│        │           (through a PRELOAD script, if you build one)  │
│        ▼                                                         │
│  the OS: windows, files, menus                                   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

#### GF.2 — Processes, as a concept

**Look up:** what an OS process is; why processes can't read each other's memory; inter-process communication (IPC), in general terms; and why Chrome runs each tab in its own process.

#### GF.3 — Electron, the first hour

**Look up:** the official Electron tutorial; what Electron bundles (Chromium plus Node) and what that costs in app size; the `main` entry in `package.json`; `app`, `BrowserWindow`, and `loadFile`; and the app lifecycle events (`ready`, `window-all-closed`).

**Build:** a throwaway Electron app that opens one window showing a plain HTML page.

**Do:** open DevTools in the window. Note which process the console you're looking at belongs to, and where the main process's `console.log` output goes instead.

#### GF.4 — Crossing the boundary

**Look up:**

- `ipcMain` and `ipcRenderer`; one-way `send` vs two-way `invoke` and `handle`
- What a preload script is and when it runs
- `contextIsolation`, `nodeIntegration`, `sandbox`, and `contextBridge`, as names and what each switch controls. The secure combination is M31's second decision.

**Build:** a button in your page that, when clicked, makes the *main* process log a message. Get it working however the tutorial suggests.

#### GF.5 — Run it and observe

**Notice and leave alone:**

1. **From the renderer's DevTools console, try to use `require`, `process`, or a `BrowserWindow` method.** What's available, and what isn't? Why?
2. **Look at your `webPreferences`.** What did the tutorial set, and what did you change to get your button working? Write down what each setting would let a malicious script in your page do. That's M31's trap.
3. **Look up `BrowserWindow`'s options** like `alwaysOnTop`, `transparent`, `frame`, and `minWidth`, but don't wire them to buttons yet.
4. **Try `transparent: true`** on a throwaway window. What do you see on your OS? Any quirks?

#### GF.6 — Hand-off: what's yours in M31

| Ground Floor left this… | M31 asks you to… |
|---|---|
| One IPC message | Drive window control from the UI, on the correct side of the boundary |
| Whatever `webPreferences` got your button working | No Node in the renderer; expose only specific commands |
| `transparent` tried once | Decide real transparency vs a translucent look |
| The default frame | Decide frame vs frameless, and what frameless costs |
| A default size | Set a default size with sensible bounds |
| No toggles | Toggle always-on-top and transparency |

Then read the Briefing below.

#### GF.7 — Check yourself before moving on

- Name the two process types and what each owns.
- Where does the main process's `console.log` go?
- What's a preload script, and when does it run?
- `send` vs `invoke`: which one gets an answer back?
- What can the renderer touch with `nodeIntegration` on?

### Briefing

Electron builds desktop apps with web tech by bundling Chromium (the UI) and Node (system access). Crucially, it runs two kinds of process: the **main** process (one, owns windows and the OS) and **renderer** processes (your web UI, one per window, sandboxed). Window control lives in main; your UI lives in renderer; they talk over **IPC** (inter-process communication). The center of gravity is **the main/renderer split, and controlling the OS window from the right side of that boundary.**

### The trap

**The main/renderer boundary, and the security model.** Window APIs (`BrowserWindow`) live in the _main_ process — you can't call them from the renderer directly, so a button that calls `window.resize` silently does nothing. The dangerous "fix" is enabling `nodeIntegration` in the renderer, which hands full system access to any web content (or any XSS) — a severe hole. The safe pattern is `contextIsolation` plus a **preload bridge** exposing only the specific commands you intend.

### The build

An Electron window with a default size, min/max bounds, and toggles for transparency and always-on-top, driven from the UI through IPC. Config is a spec you may take as given — `BrowserWindow` options `{ width, height, minWidth, minHeight, transparent, alwaysOnTop, frame }` — and the renderer sends _intents_ ("set always-on-top true") that main executes.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Where window control runs.** Options: main · renderer. _Axis:_ correct architecture vs the tempting shortcut. _Forcing:_ `BrowserWindow` lives in main — how does a button in your UI trigger it, and what breaks if you reach for it directly?
2. **The renderer's Node access.** Options: `nodeIntegration` on · `contextIsolation` + a preload `contextBridge`. _Axis:_ convenience vs security. _Forcing:_ With `nodeIntegration`, what can injected web content do to the user's machine? Why is exposing _only_ specific commands via preload safer? (This is the trap.)
3. **Transparency approach.** Options: a `transparent` frameless `BrowserWindow` · a CSS-only translucent look. _Axis:_ real OS-level transparency vs simplicity. _Forcing:_ Do you need the window actually see-through (compositing with the desktop) or just a translucent panel? Transparent windows have platform quirks — what do you give up?
4. **Frame vs frameless.** Options: native chrome · frameless custom UI. _Axis:_ OS title bar and resize handles vs a custom look. _Forcing:_ If frameless, you lose the OS resize handles and drag bar — do you re-implement them, and is that in scope?
5. **Default + constraints.** Options: hardcoded size · remember-last · fit-to-content. _Axis:_ simple vs polished. _Forcing:_ What size on first launch, and do min/max bounds prevent an unusable window?

### Research targets

- _Electron's process model: main vs renderer, and IPC._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain which process owns windows and how the two communicate. _Seed:_ Electron's process-model docs.
- _`contextIsolation`, preload, and the `contextBridge`._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain why exposing only specific APIs via preload beats `nodeIntegration`.
- _`BrowserWindow` options: `transparent`, `alwaysOnTop`, `frame`, min/max._ — **25 min.** Done when you can name the option behind each feature.
- _Transparency/frameless platform quirks._ — **25 min.** Done when you can name one gotcha (e.g. missing resize handles).

### Out of scope (fence)

Auto-updates, packaging and code-signing, native modules, tray/menu apps, multi-window management, the Chromium bundle-size debate (note — park; it's the floor-thread receipt). → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Open the app. **The number:** does the window honor its default size and min/max, and do the always-on-top and transparency toggles actually take effect (stays above others / is see-through)? And confirm `nodeIntegration` is off / `contextIsolation` on in the config.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>My resize / always-on-top call from the UI does nothing</summary>

**Orient:** `BrowserWindow` lives in the main process; renderer JavaScript can't touch it directly.

**Approach:** the renderer must _ask_ main to do it — send a message over IPC (`ipcRenderer` → `ipcMain`), and main calls the `BrowserWindow` method. Expose that send through a preload `contextBridge` so the renderer gets a narrow, safe API (`window.api.setAlwaysOnTop(true)`) rather than raw `ipcRenderer`. If nothing happens, confirm your channel names match and the handler is registered in main.

</details>

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** the window at its default size with min/max enforced; toggling always-on-top (verified against another window) and transparency.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built Electron window controls — default size, min/max, always-on-top, transparency. The architectural point is the main/renderer split: window APIs live in the main process, so the UI sends intents over IPC, and I expose them through a preload `contextBridge` rather than enabling `nodeIntegration`, which would hand system access to any injected content."
- **Design review:** a peer enabled `nodeIntegration` to call window APIs from the renderer. Explain the security hole and the `contextBridge` fix.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been learning Electron's guts — the a-ha is that your web UI can't touch the OS window directly; it's in a different process and has to ask nicely over IPC."
- **Reach-back (floor thread):** Electron bundles a whole browser and Node runtime — web-tech-on-the-desktop, paid for in bundle size and a two-process security model. Name the receipt here, and contrast it with M32's kilobytes.

---

## M32 — Kilobytes and a Screen · ESP32 microSD + OLED firmware · (L)

> **Story.** As a builder who wants to compute on hardware I fully own, I want firmware for an ESP32 that reads a microSD filesystem and drives an animated OLED, so that I can work at the embedded floor — kilobytes of RAM, no OS, direct hardware — and feel the constraints the whole stack hides. **MVP.** ESP32 firmware that mounts a microSD card, reads files from it, drives a smooth OLED animation, and offers a small browse/select interface (on-screen or over serial). **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - The microSD mounts and files are listed/read reliably (across resets).
> - The OLED shows a smooth animation _without blocking_ the rest of the firmware.
> - A simple interface browses SD contents (on-screen or over serial).
> - The firmware runs continuously without exhausting memory or hanging.

**Anchors:** _The Machine Under Everything_ — the floor _below_ its floor: memory-mapped I/O, direct hardware; M18 (manual memory, but with far less headroom) and M19 (the loop that never stops); the sovereignty thread.

### Ground Floor — A computer with no operating system

*Read this before the Briefing. It assumes C from M18 and M19, but not that you've flashed a board or talked to hardware over a bus. You'll blink an LED, print over serial, light the OLED, and list files on the SD card, each on its own, first. This tells you what each piece is for and what to look up, never how to write it. The framework, loop structure, memory strategy, rendering approach, bus sharing, and UI are the module's.*

#### GF.1 — The picture first

```
your laptop ──USB──▶ ESP32 (flash + RAM measured in KB, no OS)
   │                     │
   │ serial monitor ◀────┤  setup()   runs once
   │                     │  loop()    runs forever
                         │
                         ├── I2C (2 wires: SDA, SCL) ──▶ OLED display  (pixels)
                         └── SPI (4 wires: MOSI, MISO, SCK, CS) ──▶ microSD  (FAT filesystem)
```

No processes, no files unless you mount them, no screen unless you draw it.

#### GF.2 — Your board, first

**Look up:** the exact ESP32 board you have (there are many variants); its pinout diagram; which pins are safe to use and which are strapping pins; and how much RAM and flash it has.

**Look up:** *The Machine Under Everything* on memory-mapped I/O, as the floor below this one.

#### GF.3 — A toolchain, any toolchain

**Do:** install a toolchain and flash the classic "blink" program. Arduino IDE or PlatformIO are the fastest ways to get there. Choosing your real framework is M32's first decision, so treat this one as disposable.

**Look up:** what "flashing" means; the USB driver your board needs; boot mode and the BOOT/EN buttons; and the serial monitor, including baud rate.

**Do:** print a counter over serial once a second.

**Question to settle:** your blink program has a `delay()` in it. What else can the chip do while it's in that `delay`?

#### GF.4 — Buses, as concepts

**Look up:**

- I2C: two wires, device addresses, and running an I2C scanner to find what's connected
- SPI: four wires, chip select, and how several devices can share one bus
- Pull-up resistors, as a concept
- 3.3V vs 5V logic, and why connecting the wrong one can damage the ESP32

#### GF.5 — The OLED on its own

**Do:** wire the OLED, run an I2C scanner to find its address, and draw one line of text with the display's common library (for an SSD1306, the Adafruit or U8g2 libraries).

**Look up:** the display's resolution and how many bytes a full-screen 1-bit framebuffer takes; and what "send the buffer to the display" means in that library.

#### GF.6 — The SD card on its own

**Do:** format a microSD card as FAT32, put a few text files on it, wire the SD module, and list the files over serial with the framework's SD library.

**Look up:** FAT32 and why microcontrollers use it; mounting; and the common mount-failure causes (wiring, card size, speed, formatting).

#### GF.7 — Run it and observe

**Notice and leave alone:**

1. **Print free heap memory over serial** after boot, after initializing the display, and after mounting the SD. How much is left? That's M32's memory decision.
2. **Make a simple animation** (a moving dot) on the OLED with `delay()` between frames. Then add an SD file read inside the loop. What happens to the animation? That's M32's trap.
3. **Build a long `String` in a loop** and print free heap as you go. What happens?
4. **Pull the SD card out while running, then reset.** What does your mount code do?
5. **Try running the display and SD at once.** Are they on separate buses, or sharing one? That's the bus decision.

#### GF.8 — Hand-off: what's yours in M32

| Ground Floor left this… | M32 asks you to… |
|---|---|
| A disposable toolchain | Choose a framework and defend it |
| `delay()`-paced loops | A loop where SD I/O never freezes the animation |
| `String`s and default allocation | A memory strategy that survives running for days |
| A dot redrawn however | Choose a rendering approach that fits in RAM without flicker |
| Display and SD tested separately | Run both together, with a bus plan and mount-failure handling |
| Files listed over serial | A browse-and-select interface, on screen or over serial |

Then read the Briefing below.

#### GF.9 — Check yourself before moving on

- What do `setup()` and `loop()` each do, and who calls them?
- I2C or SPI: which uses addresses, and which uses chip select?
- How many bytes is a full framebuffer for your OLED?
- How much free heap did you have after the display and SD were both initialized?
- What happened to your animation when you added an SD read?

### Briefing

An ESP32 is a microcontroller — a whole computer on a chip with _kilobytes_ of RAM (not gigabytes), no operating system, running one program directly on the metal. Firmware talks to peripherals over buses: a microSD card (a FAT filesystem over SPI) and an OLED display (pixels over I2C or SPI). Doing both — a filesystem interface _and_ a screen animation — forces you to live inside real constraints. The center of gravity is **the embedded resource model (tiny RAM, no OS, direct hardware) and a non-blocking main loop juggling SD I/O and screen refresh.**

### The trap

**The resource constraints, and the blocking operation.** An ESP32 has tens of KB of usable RAM — allocate carelessly (big buffers, `String` churn, fragmentation) and it crashes; it's M18's memory discipline with a fraction of the headroom. And blocking: an SD read or a long draw call _stalls_ the loop, so the animation stutters or freezes — the same blocking-loop trap as M19, now with hardware timing. SD-over-SPI adds its own gotchas: mount failures, a shared bus, buffering.

### The build

Firmware mounting a microSD (FAT over SPI), reading files, and driving an OLED animation, with a browse/select interface. The pin configuration (SPI pins for the SD, I2C/SPI for the OLED) and the file-listing interface are specs you define; the firmware is yours.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Firmware framework.** Options: Arduino/PlatformIO · ESP-IDF (closer to the metal) · MicroPython. _Axis:_ fast start and libraries vs full control and performance vs ease at a RAM cost. _Forcing:_ How bare do you want the metal? MicroPython is easiest but eats RAM you barely have; ESP-IDF is closest to the hardware. Pick a point and defend it.
2. **Blocking vs non-blocking loop.** Options: SD reads and draws inline · cooperative scheduling (a state machine / `millis()`-based timing) · FreeRTOS tasks (the ESP32 has an RTOS). _Axis:_ simple vs a smooth animation that doesn't freeze on I/O. _Forcing:_ A slow SD read stalls the loop — how does the animation keep running? (The trap; M19's game loop, in firmware.)
3. **Memory strategy.** Options: dynamic allocation / `String` · fixed static buffers. _Axis:_ convenience vs no fragmentation/crash on tiny RAM. _Forcing:_ With tens of KB of RAM, what does repeated `String` concatenation or a big `malloc` do? Why do embedded devs prefer fixed buffers? (Reaches M18 hard.)
4. **Animation rendering.** Options: full-frame redraw · partial updates; a framebuffer in RAM · draw-direct. _Axis:_ simple vs fits-in-RAM and flicker-free. _Forcing:_ Can you afford a full OLED framebuffer in RAM? How do you refresh smoothly within the display's timing?
5. **SD/SPI handling.** Options: SD sharing the SPI bus with the display · separate; buffered reads. _Axis:_ pin economy vs contention. _Forcing:_ If SD and OLED share the bus, how do you keep them from stepping on each other? What happens on a mount failure — retry, or hang?
6. **UI location.** Options: an on-screen menu · a serial-console interface. _Axis:_ self-contained vs simpler to build. _Forcing:_ Does browsing happen on the OLED (needs input + rendering) or over serial (easier, less embedded-feeling)?

### Research targets

- _The embedded resource model: RAM/flash sizes, no OS, the main loop._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can state roughly how much RAM you have and why that changes everything. _Seed:_ the ESP32 datasheet overview.
- _Non-blocking firmware: `millis()`-based timing / state machines / FreeRTOS tasks._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain keeping an animation running while doing I/O. (Reaches M19.)
- _SPI/I2C buses: driving an OLED (SSD1306/SH1106) and SD over SPI._ — **25 min.** Done when you can name the bus each peripheral uses and the mount step.
- _Memory discipline on microcontrollers (fragmentation, static buffers, avoiding `String`)._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain why fixed buffers beat dynamic allocation here. (Reaches M18.)

### Out of scope (fence)

A custom PCB, deep sleep / power optimization, WiFi/networking (Reticulum-adjacent — that's M33; park), a full GUI framework, writing your own display driver, embedded Rust (note — park; ties to your Rust track). → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Run it for a while. **The number:** does the animation stay smooth (no freeze) _while_ SD reads happen, and does free heap stay stable over time (no leak/fragmentation death)? Watch the free-memory reporting; confirm the SD mounts reliably across resets.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>My animation freezes whenever I read the SD card</summary>

**Orient:** the SD read is blocking — it halts the loop until it finishes, so nothing else (including the animation) advances meanwhile.

**Approach:** you can't let the loop wait. Either break the work into small chunks the loop revisits (a state machine reading a bit per iteration), drive the animation on a `millis()`-based timer independent of the read, or put the SD work and the animation on separate FreeRTOS tasks so the scheduler interleaves them. This is M19's blocking-input trap in firmware — the loop must never wait on one slow thing.

</details>

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** SD mounting and file listing; a smooth OLED animation that keeps running during an SD read; browse/select working; stable free heap over time.

- **Interview (90s):** "I wrote ESP32 firmware that mounts a microSD filesystem and drives an animated OLED. The two hard constraints are memory — tens of KB, so fixed buffers, not `String` churn, or it fragments and crashes — and blocking: an SD read stalls the loop and freezes the animation, so I kept the loop non-blocking with timer-based updates. It's the C-memory and game-loop lessons on bare metal."
- **Design review:** a peer reads a whole SD file into a `String` and draws a full framebuffer, then wonders why it crashes. Diagnose RAM exhaustion/fragmentation; propose fixed buffers and chunked reads.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been writing ESP32 firmware — SD card plus an animated OLED — and the humbling part is doing it in kilobytes of RAM with no OS to catch you."
- **Reach-back (floor + sovereignty threads):** this is the deepest floor in either volume — below C, at memory-mapped hardware — and it runs on a chip you fully own. Name the convenience every higher layer sells you that's simply _absent_ here, against both M18 and M31.

---

# PART D — Networks, Seen and Unseen

_Two modules about the network from opposite ends: build one that needs no infrastructure at all, then learn to see exactly what's talking to your own machine. The sovereignty thread, with a defensive-security turn._

---

## M33 — A Network That Needs No One · a Reticulum feature · (M)

> **Story.** As a builder who values independent, resilient technology, I want to make two nodes talk over Reticulum, so that I understand identity-based, infrastructure-free networking — a fundamentally different model from the internet I grew up on. **MVP.** Two Reticulum nodes that discover each other and exchange an encrypted message, addressed by cryptographic identity, with no central server and no assigned addresses. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - Node A can address node B by its identity and send it a message that B receives.
> - The exchange uses no shared server, no IP address you assigned, and no DNS.
> - It still works after restarting a node (the path is re-discovered).
> - You can explain what a "destination" is in Reticulum and why it isn't an IP address.

**Anchors:** the networking primer in your project (for the TCP/IP contrast); the sovereignty thread; your permacomputing instincts.

### Ground Floor — Keys as addresses

*Read this before the Briefing. It assumes Python and the networking primer's TCP/IP basics, but not that you've used public-key identities or run a mesh stack. You'll install Reticulum, run its daemon, and use its bundled tools to see two nodes find each other first. This tells you what each piece is for and what to look up, never how to write it. Transport, packet vs link, discovery, identity persistence, and trust are the module's.*

#### GF.1 — The picture first

```
The internet you know:         someone ASSIGNS you 203.0.113.7
                               DNS turns names into those numbers
                               packets cross infrastructure others own

Reticulum:                     you GENERATE a keypair  ──▶ that's your IDENTITY
                               a DESTINATION is derived from it (a hash, not an IP)
                               you ANNOUNCE it; announces spread hop by hop
                               others learn a PATH toward your destination
                               traffic is encrypted to your key, end to end
                               over ANY medium: TCP, LoRa radio, serial
```

#### GF.2 — Public-key cryptography, as concepts

**Look up:**

- Public and private keys: what each is for, and which one you never share
- Encrypting to someone vs signing something yourself
- A hash: a fixed-length fingerprint of data
- Why "the key is the identity" means there's no password reset

**Question to settle:** if your address is derived from your public key, what happens to your address if you lose the private key and make a new one?

#### GF.3 — The internet's assumptions, made visible

**Look up**, in the networking primer: IP addressing, DHCP, NAT, and DNS. For each, write one line on who controls it and what happens when it's unavailable.

#### GF.4 — Reticulum, installed

**Look up:** the Reticulum manual's introduction and its "Understanding Reticulum" chapter; the `rns` Python package; `rnsd`, the daemon; where Reticulum keeps its config file; and interface types, especially the TCP interfaces used for testing.

**Do:** install it in a virtual environment, start `rnsd`, and read the config file it generated.

#### GF.5 — Two nodes, using only the bundled tools

**Do:** set up two Reticulum instances that can reach each other, on one machine with separate config directories or on two machines over TCP. Then use the bundled utilities (`rnstatus`, `rnpath`, `rnprobe`) to see their interfaces and to probe one from the other.

**Look up:** what each utility shows; and what an announce is and how often one happens.

Don't write any code against the RNS API yet. The module is where you do that.

#### GF.6 — Run it and observe

**Notice and leave alone:**

1. **Find a destination hash** in the tools' output. Compare it with an IP address. What's different about where it came from?
2. **Stop one node and restart it.** How does the other one find it again, and how long does that take?
3. **Find where the identity file lives.** Delete it (in your throwaway config only) and restart. What changed? That's M33's identity-persistence decision.
4. **Ask yourself how you know the node you probed is the one you meant.** Nothing told you. That's the trust decision.

#### GF.7 — Hand-off: what's yours in M33

| Ground Floor left this… | M33 asks you to… |
|---|---|
| A TCP test setup | Choose a transport for building now and for the real use later |
| Bundled tools only | Write code against the RNS API: Identity, Destination, announce |
| No messages sent | Send one, choosing Packet, Link, or Resource |
| Announces watched | Decide how nodes discover each other |
| A deleted identity file | Decide how identity persists |
| A probe you trusted blindly | Decide how you verify who you're talking to |

Then read the Briefing below.

#### GF.8 — Check yourself before moving on

- What's the difference between encrypting to a key and signing with one?
- What does a destination hash derive from?
- Who assigns your IP address, and who assigns your Reticulum address?
- How did your restarted node get found again?
- What happened when you deleted the identity file?

### Briefing

Let's start from the ground. A _network stack_ is the layers of software that move messages between computers. The internet's stack makes big assumptions: your device is handed an **IP address** by someone (your router, your ISP), names like `example.com` are translated to addresses by **DNS** servers, and your packets travel through infrastructure owned by companies. That works beautifully — until the infrastructure is down, censored, or absent.

**Reticulum** throws those assumptions out. It's a network stack where every participant is identified by a **cryptographic identity** — a public/private key pair — instead of an assigned address; where messages are **encrypted end-to-end by default**; and which runs over _any_ medium that can carry bytes: a LoRa radio, a serial wire, a TCP link over the existing internet. No central authority, no DNS, no addresses handed down from above. It's built for resilience — networks that keep working when the usual infrastructure doesn't. The center of gravity is the **mental shift** from _address-based, infrastructure-dependent_ networking to _identity-based, infrastructure-independent_ networking.

### The trap

**Applying your TCP/IP mental models.** There is no "IP address to connect to," no central server, no endpoint guaranteed to be online. If you reach for the familiar client/server-with-an-address frame, nothing fits: Reticulum is peer-to-peer, addressed by keys, and delay-tolerant, so "connect to this address" is simply the wrong picture. And a consequence people miss: the key _is_ the identity — lose the private key and you lose the identity; there is no account recovery, because there's no account and no authority to recover it from.

### The build

A small Reticulum feature: two nodes that find each other and exchange messages over RNS (the Reticulum Network Stack), using its own concepts. Run it locally over a TCP interface for testing (or over LoRa if you have the hardware). The building blocks — **Identity** (your keypair), **Destination** (an address derived from an identity), **announce** (how a node advertises a destination so others can find a path to it), and **Link/Packet** (an established encrypted channel / a single encrypted message) — are the RNS API; use it, don't reinvent the crypto.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Transport interface.** Options: TCP (for local testing) · LoRa radio · serial. _Axis:_ easy to develop vs the real off-grid use. _Forcing:_ Reticulum runs over anything — which medium lets you _build and test_ now, and which is the point later?
2. **Packet vs Link vs Resource.** Options: a single `Packet` · an established `Link` (an encrypted channel) · a `Resource` (for larger transfers). _Axis:_ one-shot simplicity vs a session vs bulk data. _Forcing:_ For a small message, do you need a persistent channel? For a big file, what does a `Resource` handle that a `Packet` doesn't?
3. **How nodes discover each other.** Options: announces (a node advertises; others learn a path) · a known destination hash. _Axis:_ dynamic discovery vs a pre-shared address. _Forcing:_ How does B learn where A is with no DNS and no central registry?
4. **Identity persistence.** Options: store the keypair · ephemeral per run. _Axis:_ a stable identity across restarts vs a fresh one each time. _Forcing:_ If the identity _is_ the key, what happens to your address when you regenerate it?
5. **Trust.** Options: trust any destination · verify it's the identity you expect. _Axis:_ simplicity vs knowing who you're really talking to. _Forcing:_ How do you know a destination belongs to the party you think, with no central authority vouching for it?

### Research targets

- _What Reticulum is, and how it differs from TCP/IP._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain identity-addressing vs IP-addressing and infrastructure-independence, tab closed. _Seed:_ the Reticulum manual — "What is Reticulum" (Mark Qvist / unsigned.io).
- _Destinations, identities, and announces._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain how a node is addressed by a key-derived hash and how others find a path to it.
- _Links vs Packets vs Resources._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say which to use for a small message vs a large transfer.
- _The delay-tolerant, no-infrastructure model._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain why there's no central server and what that buys.

### Out of scope (fence)

Mesh-routing internals, LoRa hardware bring-up (unless you have it — note, park), the NomadNet/Sideband app internals, building your own cryptography (never — RNS handles it), running a public transport node. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Two nodes locally over the TCP interface. **The number:** can node A address node B by its identity and exchange an encrypted message with no shared server and no assigned addresses — and does it still work after you restart one node (path re-discovery)?

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** two nodes exchanging an encrypted message by identity; a restart-and-recover.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a small Reticulum feature — two nodes exchanging encrypted messages addressed by cryptographic identity, with no server, no IP addresses, no DNS. The mental shift I'd share is from address-based networking, where you connect to an assigned IP, to identity-based networking, where a destination _is_ a key and the network is infrastructure-independent and delay-tolerant."
- **Design review:** a peer models it as client/server with an address to connect to. Explain why that's the TCP/IP frame, and how identity-addressing and announces replace it.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been playing with Reticulum — a networking stack that needs no internet, no IP addresses, just cryptographic identities over any medium. It's what resilient, independent networking looks like."
- **Reach-back (sovereignty thread):** M32 was hardware you own; this is a network no one controls; M34 is knowing who's on your network. Name the shared conviction — computing you don't have to trust an outside authority for.

## M34 — Who's Talking to Your Machine · connection detection & covert channels · (M)

> **Story.** As someone on a security track, I want a defensive monitor that shows what's connected to my own machine and understands how connections hide, so that I can reason about the real 2026 threat landscape — not just the connections that are easy to see. **MVP.** A tool that, on _my own_ device, enumerates open connections, baselines what's normal, and flags unexpected outbound connections with their owning process and remote endpoint. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - It lists the machine's current connections, each mapped to the process that owns it.
> - It establishes a baseline and flags a _new_ unexpected connection without drowning you in false positives on normal traffic.
> - You can name several categories of covert channel and say, honestly, which ones a connection-listing monitor can and cannot catch.

**Anchors:** the networking primer; the security material from M4; your Security+ track. _This is strictly defensive — watching your own machine — and the honest-verb discipline applies: you'll be fluent in the posture and concepts, not a threat-hunting expert._

### Ground Floor — What a connection looks like to the OS

*Read this before the Briefing. It assumes the networking primer's TCP/IP basics, but not that you've read your own machine's socket table or mapped connections to processes. You'll list and watch your own connections with the standard tools first, all on your own machine. This tells you what each piece is for and what to look up, never how to write it. The data source, baseline, what counts as suspicious, polling vs events, and honest scope are the module's. Everything here stays defensive and stays on your own device.*

#### GF.1 — The picture first

```
your machine
├── process: browser (pid 4120) ── socket ── 192.168.1.20:51544 ──▶ 142.250.x.x:443  ESTABLISHED
├── process: sshd    (pid 880)  ── socket ── 0.0.0.0:22          (LISTEN, waiting for others)
└── process: ???     (pid 6612) ── socket ── 192.168.1.20:53001 ──▶ 203.0.113.9:8443 ESTABLISHED

The kernel keeps this table. Tools like ss, netstat, and lsof read it for you.
```

#### GF.2 — Sockets and ports, from zero

**Look up:**

- A socket, a port, and the 4-tuple (local address, local port, remote address, remote port)
- TCP connection states: `LISTEN`, `ESTABLISHED`, `TIME_WAIT`, `CLOSE_WAIT`
- Well-known ports vs ephemeral ports
- TCP vs UDP, and why UDP "connections" look different in these tools

#### GF.3 — Reading your own socket table

**Do:** on your own machine (WSL, Linux, or macOS), list connections with `ss -tunap` or `netstat`, and with `lsof -i`. On Windows itself, try `netstat -ano` and Resource Monitor.

**Look up:** what each flag does; why you need elevated privileges to see other users' processes; and `/proc/net/tcp` on Linux, as the raw source those tools read.

**Do:** open one new website, then list connections again and find the new ones.

#### GF.4 — Process to connection

**Look up:** process IDs; mapping a pid to a program name and command line; and the `psutil` Python library's process and connection functions, as names. Choosing your data source is M34's first decision.

**Do:** for three connections on your machine, find the owning process and say what it is.

#### GF.5 — The threat landscape, as vocabulary

**Look up**, at the level of definitions only:

- Covert channels: storage vs timing channels
- DNS tunneling: why DNS is an attractive hiding place
- Protocol piggybacking, or "living off the land"
- Rootkits, and why one can hide from the very tools you just used
- Beaconing: regular check-ins from malware to its controller

This is the vocabulary you'll use to say honestly what your monitor can't see. You won't build any of these.

#### GF.6 — Run it and observe

**Notice and leave alone:**

1. **Count your established connections right now.** Then leave the machine idle for ten minutes and count again. How many did you recognize? That's the noise M34 has to live with.
2. **Open and close a site.** How long did its connections stay in the table, and in which states?
3. **Find a connection you can't explain.** Look up the process. Was it benign? Most are. That's M34's second trap.
4. **Take two snapshots ten seconds apart.** What would a short-lived connection that opened and closed between them look like? That's the polling decision.
5. **Go back to your GF.5 list.** Which of those would show up in `ss` at all? That's M34's honest-scope decision.

#### GF.7 — Hand-off: what's yours in M34

| Ground Floor left this… | M34 asks you to… |
|---|---|
| Tools run by hand | Choose a data source for the monitor |
| Connections counted | Build a baseline of normal |
| One unexplained connection, probably benign | Decide what's worth flagging, without alert fatigue |
| Two snapshots and a gap | Choose polling vs event-driven |
| Covert-channel vocabulary | Say honestly what a socket listing can and can't catch |

Then read the Briefing below.

#### GF.8 — Check yourself before moving on

- What's a socket's 4-tuple?
- What does `LISTEN` mean, and what does `ESTABLISHED` mean?
- How did you map a connection to its owning process?
- How many established connections did your idle machine have?
- Why can't a rootkit-hidden connection be found with `ss`?

### Briefing

Start from the basics. When two programs talk over a network, the operating system tracks each live link as a **socket** — one endpoint of a connection, identified by an address and a **port** (a number that distinguishes one connection or service from another on the same machine). An **established connection** is a live link between your machine and another. Your OS knows every open socket, and tools like `netstat`, `ss`, and `lsof` list them.

Here's the honest problem, and the reason this is a real module: **not every connection is easy to see.** Malicious software and _covert channels_ hide — smuggling data out over DNS queries, piggybacking on protocols you already allow, or (with deep compromise) hiding below the view of the standard tools. So detection isn't "run `netstat` and you're done." It's knowing your normal **baseline** and spotting **anomalies**, plus understanding the _categories_ of covert channel well enough to know what you might be missing. The center of gravity is **what a connection is at the OS level, enumerating your own machine's connections, and grasping the threat landscape** so your monitor flags the unexpected without crying wolf.

### The trap

**Believing a socket listing is the whole picture** — and its opposite, drowning in noise. The "constantly advancing threat vector" is real: DNS tunneling, protocol piggybacking, timing/storage channels, and rootkit-hidden connections all mean a connection list is _necessary but not sufficient_. The trap is a false sense of completeness. The mirror trap: every browser tab and OS service opens outbound connections, so "an external connection" is almost always benign — flag them all and your tool is useless. Telling signal from noise is the actual skill.

### The build

A defensive monitor for _your own_ machine: enumerate current connections (via the OS tools' output or an API), baseline what's normal, and flag new/unexpected outbound connections with the process and remote endpoint. Purely observational, on your own device. A connection record is a spec: `{ local_addr:port, remote_addr:port, state, pid, process }`. **No exploitation, no building of hidden channels — detection and understanding only.**

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Data source.** Options: parse `netstat`/`ss`/`lsof` output · read the OS directly (`/proc` on Linux) · a library (`psutil`). _Axis:_ quick vs robust vs portable. _Forcing:_ Which gives you the connection _and_ its owning process reliably, without brittle text parsing?
2. **Baseline strategy.** Options: an allowlist of known-good · anomaly-from-history. _Axis:_ explicit vs adaptive. _Forcing:_ How does the tool learn "normal," and how does it treat a connection it's never seen?
3. **What "suspicious" means.** Options: a new remote · an unusual port · an unexpected process · a match against a known-bad list. _Axis:_ sensitivity vs noise. _Forcing:_ Which signals are worth an alert, and which just create fatigue?
4. **Polling vs event-driven.** Options: periodic snapshots · real-time (audit framework / eBPF). _Axis:_ simple vs immediate. _Forcing:_ Do you need to catch a short-lived connection the instant it opens, or is a snapshot every few seconds enough? (eBPF is the deep path — fence it.)
5. **Covert-channel awareness scope.** Options: detect what a socket listing can · explicitly document what it can't. _Axis:_ overclaiming vs honesty. _Forcing:_ Which categories (DNS tunneling, timing channels, rootkit-hidden) are simply _outside_ what your monitor sees — and do you say so?

### Research targets

- _What a socket, port, and connection are at the OS level._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain a TCP connection's endpoints and states and how the OS tracks them. _Seed:_ a "TCP connection states" primer; your networking primer.
- _Enumerating connections: `netstat`/`ss`/`lsof`, `/proc`, or `psutil`._ — **25 min.** Done when you can list your established connections and map each to a process.
- _Categories of covert channel in 2026 (DNS tunneling, protocol piggybacking, timing/storage, rootkit-hidden)._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can name several and say, honestly, which a socket-listing monitor can and can't catch. _Seed:_ search "covert channel taxonomy" and "DNS tunneling detection" — read defensive sources.
- _Baselining and anomaly detection for connections._ — **25 min.** Done when you can describe separating normal from anomalous without a flood of false positives.

### Out of scope (fence)

Writing _any_ covert channel or exploit (never — detection only), deep packet inspection at scale, kernel-level eBPF programming (note — park; the advanced real-time path), full IDS internals (Suricata/Zeek — note, park), attribution and incident response. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Run your monitor on your own machine, let it baseline, then open a new app that makes an outbound connection. **The number:** does it flag the _new_ connection with its process and remote endpoint, _without_ flooding you on normal traffic? And can you name one covert-channel category your monitor would _miss_ (honest limits)?

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** the baseline, then a newly-opened connection correctly flagged with its process; state one category you'd miss.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a defensive connection monitor for my own machine — it enumerates open sockets, baselines normal traffic, and flags unexpected outbound connections with the owning process. The honest framing is that a socket listing is necessary but not sufficient: covert channels like DNS tunneling hide from it, so detection is about baselines and anomalies and knowing what you _can't_ see — not a false sense of completeness. This is defensive posture and concepts, not DFIR expertise I'm claiming."
- **Design review:** a peer alerts on _every_ external connection and drowns in noise, or trusts `netstat` as complete. Discuss baselining to cut false positives, and the covert-channel categories that evade a socket listing.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been building a defensive tool that watches what's talking to my own machine — the interesting part is how much hides from a simple connection list, so it's really about knowing your baseline."
- **Reach-back (security + sovereignty threads):** M4 guarded identity, M39 hardens the app surface; this watches the machine itself. And M33 built a network no one controls, while this asks who's on _yours_. Name the shared instinct about not trusting the network blindly.

---

# PART E — Foundations & Floors

_The rigor under the algorithms, and the machine under the Observable. M35 is a study module — its "build" is a portfolio of proofs — and M36 removes the magic from M5's RxJS by rebuilding it from raw bytes up. The floor thread, at its foundation._

---

## M35 — Why It's True · functions & proofs, a slow walk · (M)

> **Story.** As a self-taught engineer who learns by deriving, I want to write real proofs from zero, so that I can reason rigorously about correctness and termination — the difference between "it passed my tests" and "it's true for all inputs." **MVP.** A small portfolio of proofs, each using a named technique: one direct, one by contrapositive, one by contradiction, and one by induction — ideally one tied to code you've written. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - Each proof states the claim, names the technique, and moves in gap-free steps.
> - The induction proof has a correct base case and an inductive step that doesn't assume what it's proving.
> - You can state, out loud, why a proof establishes something for _all_ cases, not just the ones you checked.

**Anchors:** `functions_and_proofs_slow_walk.md` (the primary text for this module); your discrete-math docs (`Chapter_3_extended`, the Discrete Math guides). This is a _study_ module — no code required, though tying a proof to code you wrote is ideal.

### Ground Floor — The language proofs are written in

*Read this before the Briefing. It assumes nothing beyond school algebra. Before you can write a proof, you have to read and write the statements proofs are about. You'll translate, negate, and truth-table small statements first, without proving anything. This tells you what each piece is for and what to look up. The proofs themselves, and choosing a technique for each, are the module's.*

#### GF.1 — The picture first

```
A CLAIM          "For every integer n, if n is odd then n² is odd."
                    │            │          │
                 quantifier   hypothesis  conclusion
                 (for all)       P     ⇒     Q

DEFINITIONS      "odd" means: n = 2k + 1 for some integer k
                 every proof step eventually cashes out in a definition

A PROOF          a chain of steps from what's assumed to what's claimed,
                 each step justified by a definition, an axiom, or an earlier result
```

You can't prove a claim you can't restate precisely. That's what this Ground Floor is for.

#### GF.2 — Propositions and connectives

**Look up:** `functions_and_proofs_slow_walk.md` and the discrete-math docs in this project, on logic. Then make sure you can explain:

- A proposition: a statement that is true or false
- AND, OR, NOT, and implication (if P then Q), and the truth table for each
- Why "if P then Q" is true whenever P is false (this surprises everyone; sit with it)
- The converse, the inverse, and the contrapositive of an implication

**Do:** write out the full truth tables for "P ⇒ Q" and "not Q ⇒ not P" and compare them column by column. Don't prove anything with that yet. Just notice.

#### GF.3 — Quantifiers

**Look up:** "for all" (∀) and "there exists" (∃); how to negate each; and how to negate a nested statement such as "for every x there is a y with x < y."

**Do:** negate five statements in plain English, then in symbols. For example, "every function in this file has a test" and "some loop in this program never terminates."

#### GF.4 — Definitions, written exactly

**Look up:** the precise definitions of even, odd, divides, prime, and rational number.

**Do:** for each one, write the definition in the form "n is even means there exists an integer k such that ...". Every proof in M35 starts by unpacking definitions like these.

#### GF.5 — Functions, rigorously

**Look up:** function, domain, codomain, and range; and the definitions of injective, surjective, and bijective.

**Do:** for three functions you've used in code (a hash function, a function that squares an integer, a function that turns a string into lowercase), say whether each is injective and whether it's surjective, giving its domain and codomain first.

#### GF.6 — Reading proofs before writing them

**Look up:** a short worked proof in the slow-walk doc. Read it one line at a time, and for each line, write which definition, earlier fact, or rule of logic justifies it.

**Question to settle:** was there a step where the justification was "it's obvious"? Could you replace it with a real one?

#### GF.7 — Hand-off: what's yours in M35

| Ground Floor left this… | M35 asks you to… |
|---|---|
| Truth tables compared, nothing proved | Write a direct proof and a proof by contrapositive, choosing which fits |
| Negations practiced | Write a proof by contradiction |
| Definitions written out | Use them as the steps of every proof |
| Induction not yet touched | Write an induction proof with a sound base case and step |
| Injective and surjective classified | State one function property rigorously |
| One "obvious" step found | Leave no gaps anywhere |

Then read the Briefing below.

#### GF.8 — Check yourself before moving on

- Why is "if P then Q" true when P is false?
- What's the contrapositive of "if it compiles, the tests pass," and is it equivalent to the original?
- Negate "for every x, there exists a y such that x < y."
- Write the definition of odd, exactly.
- Is a hash function from strings to 32-bit integers injective? Why or why not?

### Briefing

Let's begin with a plain question: what _is_ a proof? In everyday life, a few examples convince us. In mathematics and computer science, examples are never enough — you show a claim holds for _every_ case by argument, not by checking. A **proof** is a chain of logical steps from things already known to be true to the claim, with no gaps.

Why should a programmer care? Because the guarantees you lean on daily are proofs in disguise: _this loop terminates_, _this function is correct for all inputs_, _this algorithm is O(n log n)_. The ability to argue rigorously is what separates "it worked on my tests" from "it cannot be wrong." And **functions**, in the rigorous sense (not just code): a function maps each input to exactly one output, and properties like **injective** (no two inputs share an output), **surjective** (every possible output is produced), and **bijective** (both) sit underneath hashing, encoding, and data-structure correctness. The center of gravity is **mathematical maturity** — reading and writing proofs, and the core techniques: **direct**, **contrapositive**, **contradiction**, and **induction** — with induction the one that powers all reasoning about loops and recursion.

### The trap

**The "proof by example" reflex, and the hand-wave.** Checking a handful of cases _feels_ like proof but isn't — a claim can hold for the first thousand inputs and fail at the thousand-and-first (there are famous examples). And the hidden gap: a proof containing an unjustified "clearly" or "obviously" is not a proof. The discipline is leaving _no_ gaps — every step earns its place.

### The build

A portfolio of proofs, written from zero, each with a named technique: one **direct** proof, one by **contrapositive**, one by **contradiction**, and one by **induction** (ideally about something you've coded — a sum formula, or the correctness/termination of a loop). Plus a rigorous statement of one function property (e.g. why a hash collision is exactly a _non-injective_ mapping). Each proof states the claim, the technique, and gap-free steps.

### Decision map

▸ _This module's "decisions" are which technique fits — write your reasoning before proving._

1. **Direct vs contrapositive vs contradiction.** _Axis:_ which makes the argument tractable. _Forcing:_ For "if n² is even then n is even," is a direct proof or the contrapositive ("if n is odd then n² is odd") easier — and why?
2. **When induction is the tool.** _Forcing:_ Which claims are "for all natural numbers n" or about a recursive/looping structure — and why does induction fit exactly those?
3. **Weak vs strong induction.** _Forcing:_ When do you need to assume _all_ smaller cases hold, not just the immediately previous one?
4. **Base case and inductive step.** _Forcing:_ Where does a proof break if the base case is wrong, or if the step quietly assumes the very thing it's proving?
5. **Rigor vs hand-wave.** _Forcing:_ Which of your steps is a "clearly" hiding a gap? Can you justify it explicitly?

### Research targets

- _What a proof is, and why examples don't suffice._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain "true for all cases" vs "checked some," tab closed. _Seed:_ `functions_and_proofs_slow_walk.md`.
- _The techniques: direct, contrapositive, contradiction._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can state when each applies and prove one small claim with each.
- _Induction (weak and strong), and its link to recursion/loops._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can prove a sum formula and explain why induction mirrors a loop invariant. _Seed:_ your discrete-math docs.
- _Functions rigorously: injective / surjective / bijective._ — **25 min.** Done when you can define each and connect one to hashing or encoding.

### Out of scope (fence)

Heavy formal logic and set theory beyond the basics, proof assistants (Coq/Lean — fascinating, note, park), real analysis, the full discrete-math course. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

The real test is reproduction. **The number:** can you, on a blank page with no notes, state and prove one claim _by induction_ from scratch — base case, inductive hypothesis, inductive step, no gaps — and explain aloud why it holds for _all_ n? One clean induction proof reproduced cold is the bar.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>My induction feels circular — I'm using what I'm trying to prove</summary>

**Orient:** the inductive step doesn't assume the claim _in general_; it assumes it for the _previous_ case (the inductive hypothesis) and uses that to establish the _next_ one — that's borrowing, not circularity.

**Approach:** be explicit about three things. The **base case**: prove the claim for the smallest n outright (no assumption). The **inductive hypothesis**: assume the claim holds for n = k (one specific case). The **inductive step**: using only that assumption, prove it for n = k+1. The whole thing works like dominoes — the base tips the first, the step guarantees each tips the next, so _all_ fall. If your step is assuming the k+1 case itself, that's the circularity; you may only assume k.

</details>

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** reproduce one induction proof cold, narrating each part.

- **Interview (90s):** "I did a slow walk through proofs — direct, contrapositive, contradiction, induction. The one that pays off for engineering is induction, because it's the same shape as a loop invariant: prove a base case, assume it holds for the previous step, show it holds for the next. I proved [a sum formula / a loop's correctness] that way, and I can reproduce it on a whiteboard."
- **Design review:** a peer says an algorithm is correct because it passes tests. Explain the difference between testing and proof, and sketch how you'd _prove_ a small loop invariant.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been strengthening my proof muscles — the click was realizing induction is just a loop invariant in math clothes."
- **Reach-back (foundations):** M20's parser and M11's BOM are recursion, and induction is how you prove recursion correct. Take one and state the base case and inductive step you'd prove.

---

## M36 — The Observable, From the Floor Up · C streams to a C++ Observable · (L)

> **Story.** As someone who learns by building the machine under the magic, I want to rebuild an RxJS Observable from raw byte streams, so that I finally understand what an Observable _is_ — not a library feature, but a protocol. **MVP.** A byte stream processed incrementally in C, then a minimal Observable in C++ with `subscribe`, one or two operators (`map`, `filter`), and clean teardown. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - The C stage processes bytes _as they arrive_, without loading everything into memory.
> - The C++ Observable lets a consumer subscribe with on-next/on-error/on-complete callbacks and get back a way to unsubscribe.
> - Values flow through a `map`→`filter` chain transformed correctly; `unsubscribe` stops further callbacks.
> - The C portions are valgrind-clean — no leaks in the callback chain.

**Anchors:** _Waiting, All the Way Up_ (streams, the event loop — M5 was written on top of this); M5 (RxJS — this is its floor); M18 (manual memory and ownership).

### Ground Floor — Bytes as they arrive, and callbacks in C

*Read this before the Briefing. It assumes M18's C memory discipline and M5's use of RxJS, but not that you've read input incrementally or written a callback in C. You'll process stdin in chunks and pass functions as values first, plus get a minimal C++ program compiling. This tells you what each piece is for and what to look up, never how to write it. The Observable protocol, ownership of teardown, hot vs cold, operators, backpressure, and error flow are the module's.*

#### GF.1 — The picture first

```
some producer ──bytes over time──▶ [ read() fills a small buffer ] ──▶ process this chunk ──▶ repeat
                                     never the whole file at once           until EOF

callback:    you hand a FUNCTION to someone else's code;
             THEY decide when to call it.

   your code:  register(on_line)        their code:  for each line: on_line(line)
```

The second picture, turned into a protocol with a lifecycle, is what an Observable is.

#### GF.2 — Streams in C

**Look up:**

- File descriptors, and stdin as descriptor 0
- `read()` vs `fread()`, and why the number of bytes you get back can be less than you asked for
- EOF, and how each function reports it
- Pipes in the shell, so `cat bigfile | ./yourprog` feeds you a stream

**Build:** a C program that counts bytes and lines from stdin using a small fixed-size buffer, refilled in a loop. Feed it a large file through a pipe and confirm memory stays small. Check it with Valgrind.

**Question to settle:** a line can be split across two chunks. What does your program need to remember between reads?

#### GF.3 — Functions as values in C

**Look up:** function pointer syntax (and `typedef` to make it readable); the C standard library's `qsort`, as a function that takes a callback; and the `void *` context pattern for passing state into a callback.

**Build:** a function that takes a function pointer and calls it once per line of input. Write two different callbacks and pass each in. Then make one callback count something, using a context pointer instead of a global.

Don't turn this into an Observable. Choosing how callbacks carry state is M36's first decision; you just need to know how the C version works.

#### GF.4 — C++, the first hour

**Look up:** compiling C++ with `g++` or `clang++` and a `-std=` flag; classes, constructors, and destructors; and when a destructor runs for a stack object.

**Look up**, as names to recognize: lambdas and captures, `std::function`, templates, and RAII. These are the C++ tools M36's decisions are about.

**Build:** a small class whose constructor and destructor each print a line. Create one in a nested scope and watch when each line prints.

#### GF.5 — Revisit RxJS with new eyes

**Do:** go back to M5's Observable scratch code. Write down, in plain words: who calls `next`, who wrote the function being called, and what happens when you unsubscribe.

**Question to settle:** is an Observable more like your chunked reader, or more like your callback-taking function? Or both?

#### GF.6 — Run it and observe

**Notice and leave alone:**

1. **Run your chunked reader on a huge file.** Did memory stay flat?
2. **In your callback program, free the context while something still holds a pointer to it,** then run under Valgrind. That's the M18 bug family inside a callback chain.
3. **Make your callback slow** (add a sleep). What happens to reading? Does input pile up anywhere? That's backpressure, M36's fifth decision.
4. **Ask how you'd tell the producer to stop** before EOF. Your current code has no way. That's teardown.

#### GF.7 — Hand-off: what's yours in M36

| Ground Floor left this… | M36 asks you to… |
|---|---|
| A chunked reader | The C stage, processing bytes incrementally into something useful |
| C callbacks with a context pointer | Decide how callbacks carry state in C and in C++ |
| No way to stop early | Subscriptions with a teardown, and clear ownership |
| A producer that always runs | Decide hot vs cold |
| One callback, no chain | Build `map` and `filter` as wrappers around a source |
| A slow consumer, unhandled | Decide about backpressure |
| No error path | Propagate errors down the chain |

Then read the Briefing below.

#### GF.8 — Check yourself before moving on

- Why can `read()` return fewer bytes than you asked for?
- What did your reader remember between chunks, and why?
- Write, in words, the type of a function pointer that takes a `const char *` and a `void *` and returns nothing.
- When did your C++ object's destructor print?
- In RxJS, who calls `next`, and who wrote the function it calls?

### Briefing

Start from the simplest idea. Data doesn't always arrive all at once — sometimes it comes a piece at a time, over time: bytes off a network, lines from a file, readings from a sensor. A **stream** is exactly that: a sequence of values arriving over time rather than a batch you already hold. In C, the rawest stream is reading bytes as they come.

Now the leap. Back in M5 you used an RxJS **Observable** — an object you could `filter`, `map`, and cancel — and it felt like magic. This module removes the magic by rebuilding it from the floor. Here is what an Observable _actually is_, stated plainly: **not a data structure, but a protocol.** A producer _pushes_ values to a consumer's callbacks — `onNext` for each value, `onError` if something breaks, `onComplete` when the stream ends — and hands back a way to _unsubscribe_ (a teardown). That push relationship, and its lifecycle, is the entire idea RxJS wraps in convenience. Building it up — raw byte streams in C, then the Observable protocol in C++ — teaches streaming and reactivity from the metal. The center of gravity is **the Observable-as-push-protocol and the machinery RxJS hides**: callbacks, subscription lifecycle, teardown, and backpressure.

### The trap

**Thinking an Observable is a container of data.** It is _not_ a list you pull values out of; it's a _push_ relationship where the producer calls _you_. Get that backwards and nothing about teardown or operators makes sense. The lifecycle traps follow: who owns the callbacks, when teardown runs, what happens if the consumer can't keep up (**backpressure**), and — in C and C++ specifically — the memory ownership of the whole callback chain, which is a fertile field for the M18 bugs (leaks, use-after-free) all over again.

### The build

Staged. **In C:** read a byte stream and process it incrementally (never load it all). **In C++:** build a minimal Observable — a `subscribe(onNext, onError, onComplete)` that returns an unsubscribe handle, a producer that pushes values, and one or two operators (`map`, `filter`) that wrap a source Observable into a new one — then demonstrate subscription and teardown. The protocol is your spec: `subscribe(observer) -> Subscription`, where `observer = { onNext, onError, onComplete }` and `Subscription.unsubscribe()`. You are rebuilding RxJS's core, not using it.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Representing callbacks in C vs C++.** Options (C): a function pointer + a `void*` context. Options (C++): `std::function`, or templates / function objects. _Axis:_ raw mechanism vs ergonomic, type-safe closures. _Forcing:_ How does a callback "remember" its state — the `void*` + function-pointer pattern in C, versus a capturing `std::function` in C++? What is each costing you?
2. **Subscription and teardown ownership.** Options: the caller owns and calls `unsubscribe` · RAII (the subscription cleans up in its destructor). _Axis:_ explicit vs automatic. _Forcing:_ When exactly does teardown run, and who guarantees the callbacks aren't invoked _after_ unsubscribe? (Reaches M18's ownership discipline.)
3. **Push vs pull (hot vs cold).** _Forcing:_ Does your Observable start producing when subscribed (cold) or push regardless of listeners (hot)? What changes for teardown?
4. **Operators as source-wrapping.** _Forcing:_ How does `filter` subscribe to _its_ source and conditionally forward `onNext`? How does an `unsubscribe` at the bottom propagate back up the chain?
5. **Backpressure.** _Forcing:_ If the producer pushes faster than the consumer handles, what happens — drop, buffer, or block? Why is this the problem streaming systems obsess over? (Fence the full solution.)
6. **Error propagation.** _Forcing:_ When the source errors, how does `onError` travel down the operator chain, and does it tear everything down?

### Research targets

- _What a stream is; reading bytes incrementally in C._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can process a byte stream without loading it whole, and say why that matters (reaches M40's laziness).
- _What an Observable actually is: the push protocol._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can state, tab closed, that an Observable is a _protocol not a container_, and name its three callbacks plus teardown. _Seed:_ the ReactiveX "Observable contract" docs (read the _contract_, not RxJS source).
- _Callbacks in C (function pointer + context) and C++ (`std::function`, RAII)._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain how a callback carries state in each language.
- _Subscription lifecycle, teardown, and backpressure._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain teardown timing and what backpressure is.

### Out of scope (fence)

A full Rx operator library, schedulers/threading, hot/cold multicasting beyond the basics, a production allocator for the chain (note — park), C++ coroutines (fascinating — park). Keep it to a minimal Observable with one or two operators. → parking lot. **Sealed answer key:** RxJS source and full Rx implementations are post-commit reading — build your minimal version first.

### Measure ritual

Subscribe to your Observable through a `map`→`filter` chain, push values, then `unsubscribe`. **The number:** do the right values arrive transformed, does `unsubscribe` stop further `onNext`, and — running the C parts under **valgrind** — are there zero leaks in the callback chain? Teardown correctness plus valgrind-clean is the bar.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>I don't see how an operator like filter "wraps" a source</summary>

**Orient:** an operator is itself an Observable whose `subscribe` _subscribes to the upstream_ and passes along a modified stream of callbacks — it sits in the middle of the push chain.

**Approach:** `filter(predicate)(source)` returns a new Observable. When someone subscribes to _it_ with their `onNext`, it subscribes to `source` with its _own_ `onNext` that says "if `predicate(value)`, call the downstream `onNext`; otherwise drop it." `onError`/`onComplete` pass straight through. Unsubscribing from the filter unsubscribes from the source. Every operator is this shape — subscribe upstream, transform the callbacks, forward teardown. Once you see one, `map` (transform the value instead of dropping it) is the same skeleton.

</details>

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** values flowing through `map`→`filter`; `unsubscribe` halting them; a valgrind-clean run.

- **Interview (90s):** "I rebuilt an RxJS Observable from the floor up — raw byte streams in C, then the Observable protocol in C++. The insight is that an Observable isn't a data structure, it's a push protocol: a producer calling your onNext/onError/onComplete and handing back a teardown. I built map and filter as source-wrapping operators and made subscription teardown clean and leak-free."
- **Design review:** a peer thinks of an Observable as a list you pull from. Correct the pull-vs-push model and explain teardown and backpressure as the real hard parts.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I removed the magic from RxJS by rebuilding an Observable in C++ — turns out it's just a producer pushing to callbacks with a way to unsubscribe."
- **Reach-back (floor + time threads):** M5 handed you Observables; now you've built one. What was RxJS doing for you that you now see explicitly — and how does this connect to M40's lazy iteration and M43's reactivity-from-scratch?
